That afternoon at Denver I was released, happy, hungry, cramped, and tired. I rented a cheap room for a week, went to a barber shop, and had a bath. I thought twenty-five cents for a bath a rank waste of money and decided to find a swimming hole. In a few days I found one.
There was a sort of camp for bums on the river bank under some trees. They had their fires there, and cans for boiling coffee. They used to lie around, washing and boiling their clothes, and swimming. They stole chickens around the neighborhood and anything else they could get.

===Chinese===

上了大街一打听，我知道这是堪萨斯的道奇城——当年那阵子，一座多半在赌博、斗殴、灌威士忌里讨生活的城。这几样消遣我那时都不感兴趣。我吃了点东西，寻了间廉价客房过夜。累散了架，一觉睡到第二天晌午。然后下调车场，找下一班西行车。车站附近，我遇上一个流浪汉模样的人物。他锐利地打量我一眼。
“下调车场去，小伙子？”
“是，怎么？”
“想上车，最好等到夜里。路警正凶着呢。今早他们在一车皮木材里发现个死了的流浪汉，把车头都拆了才把尸首弄出来。他准是个不懂事的生瓜蛋，才钻得进一车皮刨光木材的头上。那是自杀。”
我折回上城，进了家小饭馆。堂倌闲着，话多。
“出门呢？”他问。
“是。”
“往哪边？”
“丹佛。”
“扒车？”
“是。”
“听我说，这么走，到丹佛得三四天。衣裳要磨烂，兴许还叫人从车上揪下来，在科罗拉多斯普林斯吃三十天——那边大车队服苦役——正满街抓人呢。你要能凑出五块钱，我给你张条子，去找今夜‘翻山号’上的一个乘务员。给他五块，余下的他自会安排。”
“多谢，我试试。”
我找到了那趟车。乘务员收下条子和五块钱，把我塞进布巾间，落了锁。我差点憋死。夜里他开过一回门。“怎么样，伙计？”
“没事。”我说。门又锁上了。第二天早上，他给了我一片夹在面包里的牛排、一瓶咖啡。吃完这些我舒服了些，缩在角落里坐着打盹。
当天下午到丹佛，我放了出来——快活，饿，浑身僵，乏。我按星期租了间廉价客房，进理发店洗了个澡。我认定两毛五洗一回澡是天大的浪费，决定寻一处野水。没几天，寻着了。
河岸的树荫底下有一片流浪汉的营地。他们在那儿生火，罐子煮咖啡，成天躺着，洗衣服、煮衣服、游泳。他们在左近偷鸡，还有一切偷得着的东西。

===Original===

One afternoon I was in swimming, when suddenly there was an alarm and everybody ran. I didn’t pay any attention to it at first; then I got out of the water and ran for my clothes. I had just about got them on when the police arrived and grabbed me.
They put me into the patrol wagon. I was the only one they got. Later they picked up one or two others around in the woods, and took us all down to the jail. I was scared half to death.
They put us all in one big room with a lot of other bums they’d picked up. The police had had a general order to clean up the town.
In the morning they took us all up to court. It was the first time I had been in court. We were all charged with vagrancy. When my name was called, I protested. I said, “Judge, you can’t call me a vagrant. I have twenty dollars down in the jail office.”
He looked at me as coldly and impersonally as if I had been a dish of parsnips. “Fifteen days on the chain gang. Next case.”

===Chinese===

一天下午我正游泳，忽然警声大作，人人乱跑。起初我没理会；随后我爬出水，扑向衣裳。刚穿得差不多，警察到了，一把揪住我。
他们把我扔上囚车。就抓着我一个。后来又在林子里捡了一两个，把我们全押进了监狱。我吓得半死。
他们把我们同早先抓的一批流浪汉关进同一间大屋。警方奉了总命令：清城。
早晨，把我们全提上法庭。这是我头一回进法庭。罪名一律是流浪。念到我的名字，我抗议了。我说：“法官，您不能叫我流浪汉。我在拘留所办公室存着二十块钱呢。”
他望着我，又冷又公事公办，仿佛我是盘欧防风。“车队服刑十五天。下一个。”

## VII / 七
===Original===

I was taken downstairs and locked in a cell; I saw no more of the “bull pen” where I spent the night. My cellmate was a handsome, smiling young fellow about twenty-two or twenty-three. He looked like a country boy, rugged, red-cheeked, blue-eyed, sandy-haired. He seemed to be well acquainted in the jail.
Someone sang out, “Who’s the fresh fish, Smiler?”
“Another vag,” he answered. “Fifteen days.”
I told him about my case at once. I felt outraged.
“Forget it, kid. Your fifteen days will be in before your name’s dry on the commitment. They won’t put you on the gang. You’ll get a trusty job. I’m just finishing ten days, haven’t been out of this cell, goin’ out this afternoon and out of this man’s town, too,” he smiled. “Let’s eat. You’ve got money in the office. I’ll send for a messenger.”
“All right,” I said, “do it.”
A trusty took my name and our orders. Smiler ordered tobacco and papers also. A messenger came in an hour with two meals and the makings of smokes. I signed my name on the check and he was paid at the office. After we ate, Smiler sang songs, danced, or stood at the door waiting to be released.
“I’m going West, kid; hope I see you on the road so I can return that feed,” he said, when the trusty came to release him.
I liked The Smiler and wished I could be with him. In the morning coffee and stale bread were served in the cells. Fifteen minutes later there was a banging of doors. Someone shouted “Chain gang.” My door was opened.
“Outside, kid,” said a trusty.
I followed other prisoners down the corridor to a big, open room where they washed up in running water at a sink and dried themselves with handkerchiefs. The trusty came with my hat.
“You won’t need your coat, kid. I’ll look out for it.”
An officer opened a door to the street and stood inside; another was outside; the chain gang filed out and climbed into an open wagon with seats on each side facing each other. A crowd of curious men and boys stood around the wagon. When my turn came, instead of getting into the wagon I dashed down the street, instinctively, like a wild animal. The guards didn’t chase me for fear the others would escape. The crowd shouted and cheered me on. I was free.
There was nothing in my room. I didn’t go near it. I had no money, and hunger seized me at once.

===Chinese===

我被带下楼，锁进一间牢房；夜里待过的那个“牛栏”，我再没见着。同牢的是个漂亮、爱笑的年轻汉子，约莫二十二三岁。看着像个乡下小伙：结实、红脸膛、蓝眼睛、沙色头发。他在牢里似乎处处熟。
有人喊：“笑面，新来的这条生鱼是谁？”
“又一个流浪的，”他答。“十五天。”
我立刻把我的案子倒给他。我满腔的冤。
“忘了吧，孩子。你这十五天，等你的名字在执行令上墨迹干透就过完了。他们不会派你上苦工队。你会得份杂役的活。我刚坐满十天，没出过这间牢，今儿下午就出去，也出这座城。”他一笑。“开饭吧。你在账房有钱。我叫人去唤信差。”
“行，”我说，“去吧。”
一个杂役来记下我的名字和我们要的东西。笑面还叫了烟丝和纸。一个钟头后信差来了，带着两份饭和卷烟的家什。我在单子上签了名，账房付了钱。吃完，笑面唱歌、跳舞，再不然就站在门口等开释。
“我要往西去，孩子；路上见着你，好还你那顿饭。”杂役来放他时他说。
我喜欢笑面（Smiler），恨不得跟他一道走。早晨，牢里发了咖啡和隔夜面包。一刻钟后，一片摔门声。有人喊“锁链队”。我的门开了。
“出来，孩子。”杂役说。
我随着别的囚犯沿走廊进了一间大敞屋，众人在水槽的活水下洗脸，拿手绢擦干。杂役把我的帽子送来。
“外套用不着，孩子。我替你照看。”
一名警官开了通街的门，自己立在门里；门外还有一个；锁链队鱼贯而出，爬上一辆敞篷马车，两边条凳相对。一群看热闹的男人孩子围在车边。轮到我时，我没有上车，顺着大街撒腿就跑——本能的，像一头野兽。看守没有追，怕跑了其余的。人群喊叫着给我加油。我自由了。
我的住处没什么东西，我连近边都没去。身无分文，饿立刻就攦住了我。

===Original===

I walked out of town, walked all day and till dark, when I found myself exhausted at a bums’ camp twenty miles away, on the outskirts of a small town. Confidently I walked up to the fire. I was one of them. I had escaped; I was hungry; I was ready for anything; I belonged around the fire. I heard an exclamation. A form rose up from the fireside and grabbed me with both hands. It was Smiler.
“That’s doin’ time, kid, what! Did you beat it? Where’s your coat?”
“Yes. My coat’s in the jail.”
“Take a rest for an hour, and we’ll go up to the burg. I’ll get you one.”
On our way into the town he explained. “There’ll be a train through here about ten o’clock, kid; I’ll kick in the first private house that looks good. We’ll surely find a coat and maybe a few dollars and something to eat if we have time.”
He looked about sharply at the houses. Across the street we heard a door slam. A woman and man came out the front gate, the cottage was dark. We walked around the block and back to the cottage. Smiler walked confidently in the front gate, I followed. He rang the doorbell, no answer. We went around to the back where he found a kitchen window open, climbed through, and unlocked the door for me.
“Look around for some chuck, kid, and stay right there till I come back.” He disappeared. In a minute or two I could see about me, and explored the pantry. I found bread and meat and tied them in a cloth.
Smiler returned from the front carrying a bundle of clothes, and we went out. I found a coat that fitted passably. We left the balance, and departed unseen.
The train was pulling in and we made a run to the water tank where it had to stop, and by the time we found a car we could get into it was pulling out.
The lunch was spread out on a paper and quickly disappeared. Smiler had some cheap jewelry, an old silver watch, and a few dollars in cash. He divided the money, and after inspecting the other plunder threw it out the car door. “That junk would get us five years, kid, if we got grabbed with it, and it ain’t worth two dollars.”
This adventure fascinated me. I gave no thought to the burglary. It seemed right that I should have a coat and food. My money was behind in the jail. I couldn’t buy them. I had stolen them. Somehow I felt satisfied, as if I had got even with somebody.
“How do you like this racket, kid?” Smiler asked as we rolled up our coats for pillows.
“It’s great.

===Chinese===

我出了城，走了一整天，走到天黑，一身力竭，到了二十英里外小镇边上的一处流民营。我满有把握地朝火堆走去。我是他们中的人了：我逃了出来；我饿；我什么都豁得出去；这火堆边有我一个位置。我听见一声惊呼。火边一条人影跳起来，双手抓住了我。是笑面。
“这才叫坐牢哪，孩子，怎么样！跑出来了？外套呢？”
“跑了。外套落在牢里。”
“歇一个钟头，咱们上镇里去。我给你弄一件。”
进镇的路上他交代：“十点来钟有趟车过这儿，孩子；我踢开头一户看着像样的私宅。一件外套管保找得着，兴许还有几块钱，来得及再顺点吃的。”
他锐利地四下打量那些房子。街对面传来一声关门响。一男一女出了院门，屋里黑了。我们绕过一个街区又折回那小屋。笑面大大方方进了院门，我跟着。他按门铃，没人应。我们绕到后头，他寻着一扇开着的厨房窗，爬进去，给我开了门。
“找点吃的，孩子，就待在这儿等我回来。”他没了影。一两分钟后我看清了四周，把食品柜翻了一遍。寻着面包和肉，包进一块布里。
笑面从前头抱着一包衣裳回来，我们就出来了。我挑了件还算合身的外套。剩下的没动，神不知鬼不觉地走了。
火车正进站，我们朝水塔跑——车在那里必停——等找到一节能上的车厢，车也开了。
那份吃食摊在一张纸上，转眼没了。笑面得了几件便宜首饰、一块旧银表、几块现钱。钱他平分了；其余赃物验看一遍，丢出了车门。“这些破烂，孩子，叫人赃并获，够咱们吃五年官司；可它值不来两块钱。”
这场历险迷住了我。入室那一段我想都没想。我有外套、有吃的，似乎天经地义。我的钱押在牢里，买不成它们。我偷了它们。不知怎么，我心里倒踏实了，像同谁扯平了一笔账。
“这行当你觉得怎样，孩子？”卷起外套当枕头时，笑面问。
“带劲。

===Original===

How long have you been doing it?”
“Oh, a couple of years. Ever since the coppers run me out of my hometown, Detroit. That was a snide little caper we cut back there and I wouldn’t have touched it only you had to have a coat. How would you like to be a prowler, kid?”
I liked him, always smiling, for his ready help when I needed it and his companionable ways.
“I think I would like it; it’s exciting.”
“All right, kid. When we get to Salt Lake I’ll show you the real thing.”
“Good,” I said. “How long will it take us to make Salt Lake?”
“About a week at this rate.”
“Let’s ride the passenger trains,” I said, anxious to take my first lessons in burglary.
“We’ll get a passenger train out of Cheyenne, kid, if we can duck Jeff Carr,” said Smiler. “Never heard of him? He’s a railroad bull and he’s ‘bum simple’﻿—simple-minded on the subject of killing bums. If you run he’ll shoot you; if you stand he’ll get you six months, and he’d rather have you run.”
I learned all about Carr later, and his feud with the bums. They made many efforts to kill him, but never succeeded. He was later put in charge of the railroad’s train-robber detail, but he never killed any train robbers and the bums rambled through Cheyenne in peace.
We got a train out without falling foul of the murderous Carr and rode the baggage into a division point where there was a twenty-minute stop for dinner. Looking around for a place to get coffee, we passed a jewelry store. The jeweler was working at his bench in front. When we got to the alley, Smiler said: “We’ll make that tray of watches in the window, kid. You get a handful of rocks, go around to the back, and throw them against the jeweler’s door till he opens it. When he does, I’ll make the front. Don’t throw them too hard and scare him. Easy﻿—just enough to make him curious.”
I followed instructions and was preparing to throw the fourth rock when the door opened. The jeweler stepped out and took a good look around. I walked down the alley to the street, and soon caught up with Smiler, who was stepping away briskly.
“We’ve got to plant this junk, kid. We can’t take chances luggin’ it around.”
We were in the yards now and Smiler was looking at the cars curiously as we passed between them. He stopped beside a car of coal and looked at the card tacked on it. “Billed to Butte, Montana,” he said, “this’ll do.”

===Chinese===

你干多久了？”
“哦，两年上下。打警察把我从老家底特律撵跑那会儿起。方才咱俩干的那票，其实下三烂，我本不沾手——可你总得有件外套。想不想做个夜游神，孩子？”
我喜欢他——总笑着，我需要时出手爽快，做伴又随和。
“我看我喜欢；够刺激。”
“好，孩子。到了盐湖城，我领你见识见识真家伙。”
“好，”我说，“到盐湖城得走多久？”
“照这个走法，一礼拜。”
“咱们坐客车吧。”我说，急着上我的入室第一课。
“出了夏延（Cheyenne）咱弄趟客车，孩子——只要躲得开杰夫·卡尔。”笑面说。“没听说过他？铁路上的条子，还是个‘流民一根筋’——一沾流民这档子事，脑子就只剩一根筋，光想弄死他们。你跑，他开枪；你站着，他判你六个月——他还巴不得你跑。”
卡尔的底细我后来全知道了，还有他同流民们的世仇。流民们几番想弄死他，都没成。后来他调去管铁路的劫车专案，可他一个劫车贼也没杀着，流民们照旧在夏延大摇大摆地逛。
我们没撞上那杀人不眨眼的卡尔，上了车，随行李车到了一个段站，停车二十分钟开饭。四下找喝咖啡的地方，路过一家珠宝店，珠宝匠正坐在店前的台子边干活。走到巷口，笑面说：“咱们做橱窗里那一盘表。你抓一把石子，绕到后头，朝珠宝匠的门上扔，扔到他开门为止。他一开门，我从前头上手。别扔太重，吓着他。轻着来——够他好奇就行。”
我照吩咐办，第四块石子正要出手，门开了。珠宝匠出来，四下好好看了一圈。我顺着巷子走到街口，很快追上了正快步走开的笑面。
“得把这些破烂栽下去，孩子。带着到处撞，不能冒这个险。”
这时我们已进了车场，笑面从车皮间穿行时好奇地打量着车。他在一车煤旁停下，看钉在车上的货签。“到蒙大拿比尤特，”他说，“就它了。”

===Original===

He tied the watches, which he took from his coat pocket, in a big handkerchief. We climbed up on the car and the parcel was planted in one corner and plenty of coal put back on top of it. On the ground again, Smiler tore off a corner of the destination card. “Don’t forget that, kid; in case anything happens to me you’ll know this car.”
This was all accomplished in less than twenty minutes, and we had plenty of time to catch our passenger train, which we did, leaving our plunder behind but sure to follow.
“He may not miss that junk till he goes to close up, kid, or he may have missed it already. Anyway we’ll sure be stuck up and frisked at Evanston. All we have to do is tell the truth, say we rode this rattler out of Cheyenne and never left the yards at Rock Springs. And they won’t hold us. They can’t figure that we could touch that joint and go out on the same train. We’ll stop at Evanston anyway and wait for our coal car. Then, instead of going to Salt Lake, we’ll ride the freight over the cutoff to Pocatello and I’ll get the coin on that junk in an hour from Mary.”
“Who’s Mary?” I asked.
“Wait till you see her, tomorrow. She’ll buy anything from a barrel of whisky to a baby carriage.”
It was a warm night, and riding the front end of the baggage was pleasant enough. “If the bulls grab us off, kid, you say nothing; I’ll talk and tell them who we are and where we’re going. You listen, that’s all.
“Say,” he said suddenly, “take off that coat and let me look at it.” He went over it closely by the light from the engine. It was tailor-made, and he found the owner’s name on a piece of white cloth sewed in the inside pocket. He ripped it out, and I put the coat on.
“You never can be too careful, kid. We ought to have looked at that before. If Jeff Carr had picked us up at Cheyenne you might have been charged with that lousy burglary by now.”
How could a boy help admiring such wisdom? I was flattered to be taken up by one so experienced, so confident and active about his work, and withal, so carefree, happy, and smiling.
We were not molested at Evanston, where we got off and waited for our freight train. It came along next day, and that night we dropped off it at Pocatello, Idaho.
Pocatello, at that time, was just a small railroad town. A famous stopping-off place for the bums bound East, West, North and South.

===Chinese===

他从外套兜里掄出那些表，包进一条大手绢。我们爬上煤车，把小包埋进一角，上头压足了煤。落地后，笑面撕下货签的一角。“记住这个，孩子；我万一出事，你认得这节车。”
前后不到二十分钟全办完了，赶那趟客车绰绰有余——我们赶上了，赃物留在后头，却稳稳地会跟上来。
“那家伙兴许到打烊才发觉少了那些玩意儿，孩子；兴许这会儿已经发觉了。反正挨到埃文斯顿（Evanston），咱们准得被拦下来搜身。咱们只消照实说：搭这趟破车出的夏延，在石泉城没出过车场。他们不会扣人。他们想不出：干了那一票还敢坐原车走的。到了埃文斯顿咱们索性下来，等咱们的煤车。然后不去盐湖城，改走岔线的货车去波卡特洛（Pocatello）——不出一个钟头，我就能从玛丽手里把那些玩意儿换成钱。”
“玛丽是谁？”我问。
“等明天见了就知道。上到一桶威士忌、下到一辆婴儿车，她什么都收。”
夜里暖和，坐在行李车前头相当惬意。“条子要是把咱们薞下去，孩子，你一个字别说；我来讲，告诉他们咱们是谁、上哪儿去。你只管听。
“哎，”他忽然说，“把外套脱了我瞧瞧。”他借着机车投来的光，前后细查了一遍。是定做的，内侧口袋里缝着一块白布，写着主人的名字。他撕了下来，我把外套穿上。
“再仔细也不为过，孩子。这层咱们早该查的。杰夫·卡尔若在夏延把咱们提了，这会儿那桩寒碜的入室案兴许已经记你头上了。”
这样的老到，一个孩子怎能不服？被这样一个经验老到、办起事来又笃定又麻利、加上没心没肺、乐乐呵呵、见天带笑的人收留，我受宠若惊。
埃文斯顿没人来搜我们；我们下车等货车。货车次日到，当夜我们在爱达荷的波卡特洛下了车。
波卡特洛那时不过是个铁路小镇，却是流民们东南西北四路中转的名码头。

===Original===

There was a grand jungle by a small, clean river where they boiled up their vermined clothes, or “rags” as they are always called, cooked their mulligans, or, if enough bums got together, held a “convention.” These conventions, like many others, were merely an excuse for a big drunk. Sometimes they would end in a killing, or some drunken bum would fall in the fire and get burned to death, after which they would silently steal away. Oftener, the convention lasted till there was no more money for alcohol, the bums’ favorite drink. The bums then began “pestering the natives” by begging and stealing till the whole town got sore.
The town marshal would then appear with a posse armed with “saps,” which is short for saplings, young trees. He stood guard with a shotgun, while the posse fell upon the convention and “sapped up” on those therein assembled and ran them down the railroad track and out of town.
We found our junk without trouble, and hastened to Mary’s.
If I knew more of composition and writing and talking I might do justice to Mary, the fence, and friend of bums and thieves.
It’s an injustice to the memory of Mary, or, as she was lovingly called by the bums, “Salt Chunk Mary,” to try to crowd her into a few paragraphs or even a chapter. She should have a book.
“Did you eat yet?” was the first thing you heard after entering her house. “I have a pot of beans on the stove and a fine chunk of salt pork in them.” She invariably produced the beans and “fine chunk of salt pork” and always ate as heartily of them as any of her famished guests.
Her principal business was selling wine, women, and song to the railroad men and gamblers. She ruled her half dozen girls with a heavy hand. Her house on the outskirts of the town was a dingy, old two-story frame building with a couple of rooms added to one side of it where she lived and received her friends from the road.
Smiler knew her and we were welcome. The feed of beans and salt pork was spread for us. She locked the door and, while we ate, this most unusual woman estimated the value of our loot, spread out on one end of the oilcloth-covered kitchen table where we sat.
Salt Chunk Mary put no acids on the watches, nor pried into the works. She hefted the yellow ones with a practiced hand and glanced but once at the white ones.
I surveyed her as I ate. She was about forty years of age, hard-faced and heavy-handed.

===Chinese===

小河边有一片了不起的“丛林”——流民们在那里煮他们生虱子的衣裳，即他们口中的“破烂”；炖他们的什锦杂烩；凑得够多，就开“代表大会”。这些代表大会同许多别的会一样，无非是大醉一场的由头。有时以一场命案收场，有时哪个醉流民栽进火里烧死，众人便悄悄散去。更常见的，是会一直开到买酒的钱见了底——酒是流民们的头号饮料。随后流民们开始“骚扰本地良民”，讨的讨、偷的偷，直到全镇上火。
镇警长便带着一队人马出现，人人提着“树条”——saplings（树苗）的简称。他端着猎枪督阵，队员们扑进会场，见人就抡，把大家伙沿铁轨一路轰出镇去。
我们的赃物没费事就寻着了，随即赶到玛丽家。
我若多懂些作文、笔墨和谈吐，也许能把玛丽——这位赃货铺子、流民和贼的朋友——写足。
把玛丽——流民们亲切地叫她“盐块玛丽”（Salt Chunk Mary）——硬塞进几段文字、乃至一章，是对她的记忆的不敬。她该得一部书。
一进门你听见的头一句准是：“吃了没有？炉子上坐着一锅豆子，里头一大块上好咸猪腿。”豆子和“上好咸猪腿”她回回端出来，回回同饿急了的客人们吃得一样香。
她的正业是把酒、女人和歌卖给铁路人和赌棍。她那半打姑娘，被她一只铁腕管着。她的宅子在镇边上，灰扑扑的两层木楼，一侧接出两间——她住着，也接待路上来的朋友。
笑面认得她，我们大受欢迎。豆子咸猪腿摆了上来。她锁上门，趁我们吃的工夫，这位最不寻常的女人，把我们摊在油布餐桌一头的赃物估了价。
盐块玛丽对那些表不上酸、不拆芯。金表她拿行家的手掂一掂；白表的，只扫一眼。
我边吃边打量她。约莫四十岁，脸硬，手粗。

===Original===

Her hair was the color of a sunburned brick, and her small blue eyes glinted like ice under a March sun. She could say “no” quicker than any woman I ever knew, and none of them ever meant “yes.”
She went into the adjoining room and returned with a roll of bills. “Four hundred dollars, Smiler.”
“Good! Give us small bills, Mary.”
He divided the money equally between us and we got up to go.
“Let’s go in and buy a few bottles of beer for the girls, kid, just by the way of no harm.”
“No, don’t drag that kid in there﻿—and here’s something else, listen,” said this plain, blunt woman to Smiler. “I guess that kid is all right or he wouldn’t be with you. If I’m grabbed with this junk I’ll rot in jail before I put the finger on you, and if either of you gets grabbed (she was looking at me), and thinks he can get a light jolt by turning me in, he’s wrong. I’ll throw it in the river, and he can rot in jail.”
We got a room to rest up, and before going to sleep decided that Salt Lake was too small for us. San Francisco was better. Smiler had never been there, and was as anxious to see it as I. Buying tickets was throwing money away. So we beat it down to Ogden and over the Southern Pacific, riding the front end of passenger trains at night, making long jumps, avoiding bums’ camps, paying for our meals, and stealing nothing. We got into Sacramento without mishap, rested up a couple of days, and journeyed along toward the city. We got ditched off our train at Port Costa, and crawled into a hay car for the night. There were a couple of bums in the car already, and more came in later and flopped.
Early next morning we were aroused by a pounding on the side of the car. “Come on out of there!” shouted a man with his head in the door, “yez are all pinched.” We hit the ground, eight of us in all, young and old, large and small. Two men were outside, carrying shortened billiard cues.
“March!” one of them ordered.
One of the bums started to march and the rest followed. He seemed to know just what to do and where to go. A short walk across the tracks brought us up in front of a big barnlike house with a hotel sign and broad steps in front. The bum led us straight up the steps and into a barroom, where he stood up against the wall opposite the bar. We did the same. A stout, grayish, red-faced man with a very positive air was busy serving drinks.

===Chinese===

头发的颜色像晒过的砖，一双小小的蓝眼睛，亮得像三月太阳下的冰。“不”字她说得比我认得的任何女人都快，而且没有一个是“要”的意思。
她进里屋，出来时拿着一卷钞票。“四百块，笑面。”
“好！给小票，玛丽。”
他把钱同我平分，我们起身要走。
“进去给姑娘们买几瓶啤酒吧，孩子，就当没有坏处。”
“不，别把那孩子拽进去——还有一句，听着，”这位直来直去的女人对笑面说，“我看那孩子靠得住，不然也不会跟你。这些玩意儿若叫我人赃并获，我烂在牢里也不会咬你们；你们哪个（她正看着我）要是折了进去，想拿我顶罪换几天轻的，他打错了算盘。我把它们扔河里，让他烂在牢里。”
我们开了间房歇脚。睡前议定：盐湖城对咱们太小了，旧金山好。笑面没去过，同我一样想看。买票等于扔钱。于是我们奔奥格登（Ogden），走南太平洋线，夜里坐客车前头，长跳，避着流民营，饭照价吃，分毫不偷。我们平平安安进了萨克拉门托，歇了两天，朝城里接着走。在科斯塔港我们叫人从车上了下来，钻进一节干草车过夜。车里已趴着两个流民，后来又有几个进来倒下。
清晨，车厢壁上一阵敲打把我们惊醒。“都给我出来！”门口一个脑袋喊，“你们全被捕了。”我们跳下车，一共八个，老的少的、大的小的。外头两个人，拎着锯短的台球杆。
“走！”其中一个喝令。
一个流民抬脚就走，其余跟上。他好像门儿清：该做什么、该去哪儿。穿过铁轨没走几步，我们来到一座谷仓似的大房子前，挂着旅馆招牌，门前宽台阶。那流民领我们径直上台阶进了一间酒吧，他自己往酒吧对面的墙边一站。我们照做。一个矮胖、灰白头发、红脸膛、派头十足的男人正忙着打酒。

===Original===

He paused and spoke to one of our captors:
“What have you there, Mike?”
“Eight bums, judge.”
He served another round of drinks and turned to us again.
“Are you guilty or not guilty?”
The big bum that led us in immediately answered: “We’re all guilty, judge, an’ hungry.”
The judge﻿—and he was no less person than Judge Casey, famous and celebrated in song and story for his speedy trials and the human quality of his justice﻿—waved a short, hairy, muscular arm toward the dining room.
“Feed them first, Mike.”
We followed our leader into the dining room and all sat at one big table where we had a substantial breakfast. When the last bum had his fill, we marched back to the barroom and lined up against the wall again.
“All ready, judge,” said Mike.
The judge stopped dealing drinks and pronounced sentence. His voice sounded tired, weary; there was a note of kindness in it.
“Oh, well, Mike, lave, the big bums take tin days, and the little bums five.”
He turned to his work. The big bum now led us out and to a nearby boxcar that served as a calaboose. One side door was nailed up and both end doors fastened outside. We crawled in and the other side door was shut and padlocked on the outside.
Smiler sat down in the car and laughed. “What’s next, I wonder, kid?”
Someone answered out of the darkness: “They’ll hook this boxcar on to the first freight and haul us over to Martinez, the county seat, an’ slough us in the county jail.”
I had told Smiler about cutting myself out of the car when my companion was killed.
“Better get busy with your shive, kid.” I started cutting on the side opposite the boarding house. The bum that pleaded guilty for all of us saw what I was doing and protested. “Hey, kid, lay off o’ that. You want to get us all six months, destroyin’ railroad property?”
Smiler was on him like a tiger and cuffed him around till he whined. “Go over in that corner and lie down, you greasy, big gay cat, or I’ll cut your tail off.” The bum sat down and stayed quiet. In an hour we kicked the boards out.
“You first, kid.”
I dropped out and Smiler followed. As we dodged across the yards I looked back. Two more bums had wriggled out. The other four elected to stay in the car, too lazy to run away.
We hid all day under a warehouse and at dark rode a freight into Oakland where we got the ferry. We bought new clothes, rented a room, and got cleaned up.

===Chinese===

他停下来，问我们那看守中的一个：
“迈克，你逮的什么？”
“八个流民，法官。”
他又打完一轮，才转向我们。
“服不服罪？”
领我们进来的大个子流民应声便答：“全服，法官——还饿着哪。”
这位法官——不是别人，正是凯西法官（Judge Casey），审讯之神速、断案之人情，歌里书里都有名的——朝饭厅挥了挥一条毛茸茸的短胳膊。
“先给他们吃的，迈克。”
我们跟着领头的进了饭厅，围着一张大桌坐定，吃了顿瓷实的早饭。最后一个流民也吃饱了，我们列队回到酒吧间，重新靠墙站好。
“都齐了，法官。”迈克说。
法官停下打酒，宣判。他的嗓音听上去累了、倦了，里面却有一缕善意。
“哦，这样吧，迈克：大流民十天，小流民五天。”
他回头干他的活。大流民领我们出来，进了附近一节充作拘留所的棚车。一侧门钉死了，两头的外面都上了闩。我们爬进去，另一侧门也关上，外头落了锁。
笑面往车底一坐，笑了。“下文是什么呢，孩子？”
黑暗里有人答：“他们把这节棚车挂上头一班货车，拖到县府马丁内斯（Martinez），把咱们撅进县监狱。”
我早把同伴丧命那回我自己破车厢脱身的事讲给笑面过。
“快掏你的家伙干吧，孩子。”我朝着背对客栈旅店的一面动起了手。替我们全体认罪的那个流民瞧见了，抗议起来：“嘿，孩子，住手。你想叫咱们全吃六个月、破坏铁路财产？”
笑面扑上去像只虎，抡得他直告饶。“滚到那墙角躺下，你这滑头的大懒猫，不然铰了你的尾巴。”那流民坐下，再不作声。一个钟头后，我们把板子蹬开了。
“你先，孩子。”
我跳下去，笑面跟上。穿车场时我回头一望，又有两个流民钻了出来。其余四个宁可留在车里——懒得跑了。
我们在一座货栈底下藏了一天，天黑搭货车进了奥克兰（Oakland），上了轮渡。我们买了新衣裳，租了房，把自己收拾干净。

===Original===

San Francisco fascinated us. We spent days on the waterfront watching the ships and sailors, or at the Cliff House where I first saw the ocean, and in the park. Our nights were spent about the Coast, Broadway and Pacific Street, at Bottle Koenig’s or the Bella Union or downtown at Pete Dorsey’s and other dance halls.
Gambling was open everywhere; we experimented and lost. We wasted our money around the shows, dance halls, and hop joints, which were open and unmolested by the police. In a month we were almost broke, and ready for the road. Salt Lake was decided upon where Smiler had something special in view.
An uneventful week on the road put us into Salt Lake City.
Then swiftly came the tragic night that separated us forever﻿—I to jail and the kindly, lovable Smiler to his grave.

===Chinese===

旧金山把我们迷住了。我们整日泡在滨水区看船看水手，要么去悬崖屋——我在那儿头一回见了海——要么逛公园。夜晚耗在海岸区、百老汇和太平洋街，泡“酒瓶”凯尼希家、贝拉联合，或市中心皮特·多尔西家那些舞厅。
赌，四处敞着门；我们试着来，输了。钱扔在了戏院、舞厅和烟馆（hop）——烟馆也敞着，警察不来碰。一个月下来，我们差不多见了底，该上路了。定下盐湖城——笑面在那边有一桩特别的算计。
路上无话的一礼拜，把我们送进了盐湖城。
跟着，那个拆散我们的惨痛之夜，来得飞快——我进了牢，那位和善可爱的笑面，进了坟。

## VIII / 八
===Original===

There was a legend on the road that the Mormon Tabernacle in Salt Lake City was a veritable storehouse of gold, silver, and precious stones and it was this that lured Smiler back to that city. At that time a high adobe wall surrounded the block on which stood the Tabernacle and the then-unfinished Mormon Temple. We looked it over for several days and nights but could get nothing tangible to work on. Sunday we attended services and the plate was to be seen, silver and gold; more than we could carry away if we got it. At last we decided to go over the wall and give the place a good reconnaissance. If it looked feasible we could get a couple of other idle burglars and give it a thorough looting.
On top of the wall we pulled up our light ladder and placed it inside. Smiler went down first. I barely had my feet off the ladder when a dozen men rose up out of the shrubbery armed with shotguns, and surrounded us. We stood still by the wall. One of them spoke, sternly, evenly: “Go back over that wall.”
Little we knew the Mormons. We went up the ladder, pulled it up, and went down and away.
When Smiler’s good humor returned he held up his hand. “Kid, I’ll never try to rob another Mormon. I’ll go to work first.”
The next day we went into a small gambling house where we hung out to read the papers. We sat at a table near the big safe in a corner of the room. A man in overalls was taking the lock apart. The place had changed hands a few days before and the new proprietors were having the numbers of the combination changed. When the mechanic finished his work he wrote some numbers on two slips of paper. These he threw on the floor beside his tools and went back to the bar for a drink. Smiler stooped, picked up the slips, looked at them closely, and threw them back on the floor.
Outside, he said: “We’ve got the combination of that box, kid. Those two pieces of paper are for the new proprietors, their new combination.”
Neither one of us knew enough about safes to feel sure of opening one even when we had the numbers. Smiler knew a chap doing time in the penitentiary who knew all about safes. The stir was only a mile out of the city and we decided to go up and get some expert advice.
Next day we visited the expert at the stir, who gladly gave us instructions and declared himself in with any money we got.

===Chinese===

路上流传着一桩传说：盐湖城的摩门圣殿是一座名副其实的金银宝石库，正是这个传说把斯迈勒（Smiler）又引回了那座城。那时候，圣殿和当时尚未完工的摩门圣殿堂所在的那一个街区，围着一道高高的土坯墙。我们察看了几天几夜，摸不到一点实在的下手处。星期天我们去做了礼拜，奉献盘是看得见的，银的金的；真到手了，我们俩也搬不动。末了我们决定翻墙进去，把那地方好好侦察一番。若看着可行，就再约两个闲着的贼，把它洗个透。
墙头上，我们提起轻梯，放进墙里。斯迈勒先下去。我的脚才离梯子，十来条汉子就从灌木丛里立起来，端着霰弹枪，把我们围了。我们贴墙站着，一动不敢动。其中一个开了口，严厉而平稳：“从墙上翻回去。”
我们太不懂摩门人了。我们上梯，提梯，下去，走人。
斯迈勒缓回他那副好脾气后，举起一只手。“小子，我再也不抢摩门人了。我宁可先去干活。”
第二天，我们进了一家我们常落脚的小赌场看报。我们坐在屋角那口大保险柜旁边的一张桌边。一个穿背带裤的汉子正拆那把锁。这地方几天前刚换了东家，新东家正叫人换暗码。师傅干完活，在两张纸条上写了些数码，往工具旁的地上一扔，回吧台喝酒去了。斯迈勒弯腰拾起纸条，凑近看了又看，又扔回地上。
到了外头，他说：“那口柜子的暗码到手了，小子。那两张纸是给新东家的——他们的新暗码。”
我们俩对保险柜都懂得有限，纵有数码也未必开得开。斯迈勒认识一个蹲在监狱里的行家，保险柜无所不通。监狱离城只有一英里，我们决定上门讨教讨教行家的意见。
第二天我们去监狱拜见了行家，他欣然指教，还声明入一股：弄着钱有他一份。

===Original===

That night Smiler opened the box as easily as if it belonged to him and locked it again. We got but a few hundred dollars where we expected several thousands. The new owners had no bankroll, just opened up on a shoestring, hoping to get off lucky and win. The next morning they accused each other of the theft, almost fought with pistols, and dissolved partnership, calling each other thieves.
The money was split three ways. The expert at the stir took his bit with bad grace, intimating that we got more than we were dividing, and that we were thieves. As we were leaving, Smiler said: “Goodbye, Shorty. Everything’s all right now, ain’t it?”
“Oh, sure,” he grouched. “Everything’s all right﻿—just like Denmark.”
“Kid, we’ll go up to that joint and give them a chance to win their money back,” said Smiler. “I feel lucky.”
We went up, but they were closed and we had to go elsewhere to gamble. The habit had fastened on him. He became a fiend for gambling. When his money was gone I let him play mine in, and he kept us broke. In a few days the money from our last venture was gone, and we were sparring about for more.
The oldest and commonest system of the house burglar in locating money and jewelry is to stand about theaters, cafés, and the better shops, watching their patrons and following them home if they display wealth. Smiler and I followed a woman and man to their home one night from the theater. The woman wore enough diamonds to stun any thief or pawnbroker. They went into a well-kept, two-story house, back in a big yard full of trees and shrubs, only a dozen blocks from the main street. They let themselves in with a key and appeared to be the only occupants of the place.
We looked the spot over for several days and evenings. There was a servant girl about the place, but no children and no dogs. This was good. Children, particularly young ones, often need attention in the night, which interferes with the prowler. Dogs, young or old, are the bane of the burglar’s life. A dog inside a house where people are sleeping prohibits burglary, and the smaller he is, the louder he prohibits. So far as we could see, the place looked made to order for us. House burglars customarily work single-handed. Two men prowling about in a house in the dark are apt to get confused if anyone wakes up, and shoot each other. It has happened.

===Chinese===

当夜，斯迈勒开那口柜子，像开自家的似的容易，开完又照旧锁好。指望几千块，到手只几百。新东家没有本钱，小本开张，指望赌赢了发迹。第二天一早，他们互相指责偷了钱，差点拔枪相向，散了伙，彼此骂贼。
钱分作三股。监狱里的行家领他那股时没个好脸，言语里认定我们私吞了，认定我们是贼。临走，斯迈勒说：“回见，短子。如今一切都太平了，是不？”
“哦，可不是，”他咕哝道。“一切太平——跟丹麦一个样。”
“小子，咱们上那家去，给他们个把本钱赢回去的机会，”斯迈勒说。“我手气正旺。”
我们去了，可人家歇业，只得另寻地方赌。赌瘾把他咬住了。他赌疯了。他的钱输光，我便把我的放他进场，他叫我们一直瘪着。没几天，上一票的钱光了，我们又四处张罗起来。
入室贼寻钱寻首饰，最古老也最通行的法子，是守在剧院、咖啡馆和高档铺子左近，盯主顾，谁露了财就跟着到家。一天夜里，斯迈勒同我从剧院跟一对男女到家。那女人浑身的钻石，足以晃瞎任何贼或当铺掌柜。他们进了一所收拾得妥妥帖帖的两层楼，坐落在一个满是树木灌丛的大院子深处，离大街不过十来个街区。他们用钥匙自己开门，看去是这宅里仅有的住客。
我们把那地方察看了几天几个晚上。宅里有个使女，没有孩子，没有狗。这就好。孩子——尤其小的——夜里常要人照应，碍夜行人手脚。狗呢，不分老小，是贼的一生之敌。屋里一条狗，睡着一屋子人，这买卖就做不成；狗越小，搅得越响。就我们看，这地方简直是为我们定做的。入室贼照例单干。两个人黑地里在一所宅子里摸，一有人醒就多半乱套，互相开火。出过这样的事。

===Original===

“I wish this stool-pigeon moon wasn’t so bright tonight, kid,” said Smiler as we waited in the shrubbery for our people to put out lights and retire. At eleven o’clock the house was in darkness. “About three hours more, kid, and we’ll get busy.”
We had been in the yard since nine o’clock, tense, watching, listening. Six hours lying on the damp grass and wet from the heavy dew. And still the burglar wonders how he catches those terrible colds that hang on and on and finally develop into T.B.
It was agreed that I should stay outside and lend a hand in case anything went wrong in the house with Smiler. Inside the house, inexperienced as I was, I would be in the way. Smiler looked at his watch for the hundredth time.
He removed his shoes, putting one in each hip pocket as far as it would go, buttoned his coat tightly, and pulled his hat far down over his eyes. Always clean, handsome, smiling, he wasn’t good to look at now. We stepped toward the house, keeping out of the moonlight when possible. On the rear porch Smiler tried the door and windows﻿—all fastened. He motioned me to stay where I was, and disappeared around the side of the house in search of an unlatched window. No burglar uses force till he is sure there is no window or door unfastened.
He was gone so long that I was getting worried when he silently appeared from the other side; he had gone around the house. I left the porch and joined him beside a window he had found unfastened. He pointed to some vines nearby, where I took my stand to wait till he rejoined me. I watched him intently, a pupil, apt at learning. The bottom of the window was about even with his shoulder. He stood with arms upraised, hands against the window sash, slowly, silently pushing it up. I listened, but he made no sound. The night was calm, still, dead.
Then came a blinding flash of fire and the deadly roar of a rifle. Broken glass, falling, tinkled, and a woman shrieked once hysterically.
My eyes had never left Smiler. He staggered back from the window clutching his throat with both hands; his legs trembled like a spent fighter’s, as he sank slowly to his knees.
For a moment I shrank with fear and shock, cold and helpless, into the vines beside the house. The flash and deafening explosion coming out of the still night when my nerves were at their highest tension petrified me on the spot.

===Chinese===

“今夜这告密贼的月亮也未免太亮，小子，”我们蹲在灌丛里，等我们那对男女熄灯就寝，斯迈勒说。十一点，宅子黑透了。“再熬约莫三个钟头，小子，就动手。”
我们九点钟就进了院子，绷着，望，听。在湿草上躺了六个钟头，叫浓露浇了个透湿。贼还纳闷呢：他那些缠绵不去、末了熬成痨病的大风寒，是怎么招上的。
说定我在外头接应，宅里斯迈勒有个闪失好搭把手。宅子里我帮不上忙——我生手，进去反而碍事。斯迈勒第一百次看了看表。
他脱下鞋，两只各塞进一边后裤兜，能塞多深塞多深；把外衣扣得紧紧的，帽檐拉下来盖住眼睛。他平日总是干干净净、漂漂亮亮、笑眯眯的，这会儿可不好看了。我们朝宅子挪，能躲月亮就躲。后廊上，斯迈勒试了门和窗——全闩着。他打个手势叫我原地不动，绕到宅子另一边找没闩死的窗。不到万不得已，贼不用蛮力——总要先确认真没有一扇没闩的门窗。
他去了那么久，我都开始慌了，他才又从另一边无声地现出身来：他绕宅子转了一圈。我离开廊子，凑到他寻着的那扇没闩的窗边。他指指近旁的藤蔓，我到那里站定，等他回来接我。我目不转睛地看他——一个学得飞快的学生。窗台齐他肩膀那么高。他双臂高举，双手抵住窗扇，慢慢地、无声地往上推。我听着，他没有一点声息。夜是沉静的，死的。
随即一道火光刺眼，一声步枪的致命轰鸣。碎玻璃坠落，叮叮当当；一个女人歇斯底里地尖叫了一声。
我的眼睛一直没有离开斯迈勒。他从窗前踉跄后退，双手掐着自己的喉咙；两条腿像力竭的拳师一样抖着，缓缓跪了下去。
有那么一瞬，我又惊又怕，冷透地、无望地缩进宅边的藤蔓里。死寂的夜里炸出这闪光和震耳的轰鸣，我的神经正绷到最紧处，把我当场钉住了。

===Original===

Then my legs ran away with me and I found myself at the back gate to the alley, the way we came in, fumbling at the latch. My mind was clearing up now. The gate was open. I was wondering whether to run up or down the alley, into the city or out of it. I expected to hear more shots. Then I thought of Smiler, and looked back.
He was there in front of the window in the moonlight, on his hands and knees now, shaking his head from side to side slowly.
There was no alarm yet. The night was calm and still again. There was the smell of burnt powder in the air.
This was my first desperate experience. I didn’t know what to do. I wanted to run on and save myself and I wanted to help Smiler. Something﻿—I don’t know what it was, but I don’t think it was courage or bravery﻿—turned me around and I ran back through the shrubbery to a spot opposite the window from which the shot had been fired. In the clear moonlight I could see that it was raised about six inches and one of its lower panes was shattered.
The house was still in darkness. There was no sound inside. I must have been in a panic still. No sane-thinking human would have done what I did next. I ran out in front of the window to Smiler, and standing over him, put my arms under his, lifting him slowly to his feet. Blood was streaming out of his mouth. He slowly and weakly put one arm around my neck. I held it there, grasping the hand that hung limply over my shoulder, and with my other arm around his waist slowly dragged him to the gate and out into the alley where he collapsed. His dead weight was too much for me. I let his body slip gently to the ground. A twitching shudder ran through him. He straightened out on his back and threw his arms wide. He was dead.
Still no sound came from the house. Next door I now heard a window thrown up and voices calling, a light appeared. It seemed an age since the shot was fired, yet it wasn’t three minutes. Smiler was beyond my help. I must be off. I was drenched with the blood that spurted from a wound in his neck. It was on my face and hands. My shirt front was saturated with it. My coat was dripping blood. I thought of our room downtown﻿—no chance to make it in that condition.
Smiler’s watch chain glistened in the moonlight. I tore at his pockets and found his money.

===Chinese===

随后我的两条腿带着我跑了，跑到了进来的那条路的后门，我扒弄着门闩。脑子这时才醒过来。门开着。我正盘算顺着巷子往哪头跑——进城还是出城——等着再响枪。这时我想起斯迈勒，回头一望。
他就在窗前月光里，这时已经双手双膝撑着地，缓缓地左右摇头。
还没有警报。夜又静了下来。空气里有火药味。
这是我头一遭亡命的体验。我不知怎么办。我想接着跑、保自己，又想帮斯迈勒。有个东西——我说不清是什么，可我不信那是勇气——把我调转过来，我穿过灌丛跑回正对那扇窗的地方。清亮的月光里，我看见那窗子抬起了约六英寸，下面一格玻璃碎了。
宅子还黑着，里面没有动静。我一定还在慌。脑子正常的人，决不会做我接下来做的事。我跑到窗前斯迈勒跟前，叉开腿立在他身上方，两条胳膊从他腋下穿进去，慢慢把他架起来。血从他嘴里涌出来。他缓缓地、虚虚地一条胳膊搭上我的脖子。我按住它，攥牢那只从我肩上软软垂下的手，另一条胳膊搂住他的腰，慢慢把他拖到门口，拖进巷子。他一塌就散了。他死沉的分量我架不住。我让他的身子慢慢滑到地上。一阵抽搐的战栗穿过他。他仰面躺直，两臂张开。他死了。
宅子里还是没有一点声息。隔壁这时有一扇窗推上去了，有人喊，灯亮了。自枪响仿佛过了一个世纪，其实不到三分钟。斯迈勒我救不回来了。我得走。他脖子上伤口喷出的血把我浇了个透：脸上手上都是，衬衫前襟吸饱了，外衣直滴血。我想起城里我们那间屋——这副样子是别想回去了。
斯迈勒的表链在月光里闪。我扯开他的衣袋，摸到了他的钱。

===Original===

I went through them all, took everything, and ran for blocks and blocks through the alley toward the outskirts of the city. The dawn was coming fast. I must hide. I turned out of the alley into a street and walked on wondering what to do, where to go.
A “For Rent” sign on a large, neglected-looking residence halted me. Not a soul was in sight on the street. I dodged into the yard, around to the back, and found the kitchen door unlocked. Inside, I went into what had been the dining room and sat down on the floor exhausted, nervous, and covered with blood.
When it became light enough I explored the old house, finding nothing but rags and old papers. There was no water in it and washing was out of the question. I gathered up an armful of the rags and papers, making a pallet of them by a window upstairs in a front room, where I could look out on the street. I sat down on the pallet and began to think things over. It was the first good chance since I had left my father. I heartily wished myself back with him. If this was adventure I wanted no more of it. I was done with it. It had brought me to this. Tired, hungry, bloody, afraid to go to sleep even if I could, lest I should be found and dragged off to jail and surely convicted. Smiler was dead. I had no ties to bind me to the road.
Yes, I would get out of this mess somehow and go back to my father. I was sorry for poor Smiler and would have stayed with him on the road, but now it was different. I felt no remorse for any of the things we had done. I wasn’t sorry. I didn’t even think they were wrong. That phase of it never entered my mind.
Then I fell to thinking of Smiler’s tragic end; trying to puzzle it out: Why the woman shrieked, why the house remained so dark and silent, and why I wasn’t shot when I went to his assistance. There was no answer to it all. I felt easier in my mind now that I had decided to quit the road and go home, and longed for the night to come when I could leave my hiding place and wash the blood off my hands and face and get fresh clothes.
As the day wore on, feeling more secure, I stretched out on my bed of rags and paper and tried to sleep, but it wouldn’t come to me. Later, in the afternoon, I removed my coat and shoes and managed to doze fitfully till evening.

===Chinese===

我把他的口袋一个个翻遍，全拿走，顺着巷子一街一街朝城郊跑。天快亮了。我得藏。我拐出巷子上了一条街，边走边想怎么办，往哪里去。
一座看着久无人管的大宅门上“招租”的牌子留住了我。街上一个人影也没有。我闪进院子，绕到后头，厨房门没锁。进去，我走进那间从前是饭厅的屋子，往地上一坐——力竭了，神经乱了，浑身是血。
天亮得能看了，我把这所老宅探了一遍，只有破布和旧报纸，没有水，洗是不用想了。我抱了一怀破布报纸，在楼上前房窗边铺了个铺，好望得见街。我在铺上坐下，把事情从头想。这是离开父亲以来头一个静下心想的机会。我衷心希望自己回到他身边去。这要就是冒险，我不要再来了。我洗手不干了。它把我带到这步田地：累，饿，一身血，连睡也不敢睡——怕叫人寻着，拖去坐牢，定罪是稳的。斯迈勒死了。路上再没有什么拈着我了。
对，我要从这场祸里脱身，回到父亲那里去。可怜的斯迈勒我难过；他要还在，我会同他一路走到底，如今不一样了。可我们干过的那些事，我一丝悔恨也没有。我不难过。我连想都没想它们是错。那方面的事根本没进过我的脑子。
随后我细想斯迈勒的下场，想解这个谜：那女人为什么尖叫，宅子为什么一直那样黑那样静，我去救他时为什么没人开枪打我。全解不开。既打定主意离路回家，我心里松快了些，只盼天黑，好离开藏身处，把手脸的血洗掉，换身干净衣裳。
天挨到后来，胆子壮了些，我在破布报纸铺上躺下想睡，睡不着。下午晚些，我脱了外衣和鞋，断断续续打盹到傍晚。

===Original===

When it was dark enough to walk through the streets safely I left the house and made my way to a hot sulphur spring that gushed out of a hillside a mile away. There I stripped and scrubbed myself as best I could in the dark, and without soap. My clothes were stiff with dried blood. I threw the shirt and underwear away, put my handkerchief about my neck, and, buttoning my coat, started off in search of a store where I could get a new outfit.
Since the night Smiler stole the coat for me we had never allowed ourselves to get flat broke. With all his passion for gambling he would hold on to a few dollars. With the money I found on him and my own, I counted up thirty dollars. His watch wasn’t worth two dollars, a cheap one he bought in San Francisco. I kept it as a memento. There was nothing else of value in his pockets.
In a few minutes I found one of the many little general stores that flourished in Salt Lake at that time, and purchased a suit, the cheapest one there, for twelve dollars, and a fifty-cent shirt. I went back to the spring with my bundle, where I donned the cheap bullswool suit and threw my good one away. Tired and hungry, I crawled aboard a streetcar citybound from the sulphur springs.
In the car I looked closely at my hands and saw there was still some blood around my fingernails; there were dark spots on my shoes, too. Hungry as I was, I first went into a barber shop, had a bath, and scrubbed my shoes. Then to a restaurant, and while eating I decided to go to our room and have a good sleep and get a suit of underclothes and a few other things that I would need on my way home. I turned the whole thing over in my mind and was sure there was no danger in going to the room.
I was sitting at the table nearest the kitchen, finishing my meal, when a policeman came in and sat opposite me. The waiter brought a steak which was ready and waiting for him.
“Did they identify the dead burglar yet?” the waiter asked.
“No, not a thing on him.”
“How did it happen, anyway?”
“Just like the morning paper says. The woman’s husband was called away unexpectedly. The hired girl had the night out and she was alone in the house, sleeplessly walking around the rooms when this bird appeared at the window. Scared half to death she got her husband’s rifle and fired at him; then she fell in a faint. When she revived, the neighbors were called and they got the police.

===Chinese===

天黑得能安全上街了，我出宅往一英里外山坡上涌出的一处热硫磺泉去。到了那里，我脱光衣裳，黑地里没皂，尽力擦洗。衣裳叫血僵住了。我把衬衫和内衣扔掉，手帕围在脖子上，扣上外衣，出发找铺子置一身新行头。
自打斯迈勒那晚替我偷来外衣，我们从不许自己落到身无分文。他赌瘾再大，也总攥着几块钱。把我在他身上搜出的钱同我自己的并起来，一共三十块。他那表不值两块，旧金山买的便宜货。我留下作纪念。他口袋里再没有值钱的东西。
不几分钟，我寻到盐湖城当年满街皆是的一家小杂货铺，买了一套衣裳——铺里最便宜的——十二块，一件五毛的衬衫。我抱着包袱回泉边，换上那身便宜的粗呢货，把好衣裳扔了。又累又饿，我爬上一辆从硫磺泉进城的有轨电车。
车上我细看我的手，指甲缝里还有血；鞋上也有黑斑。饿归饿，我先进了理发店，洗了个澡，把鞋刷了。然后进饭馆；吃着，我拿定主意回我们那间屋，好好睡一觉，再取一套内衣和回家路上要用的几样零碎。这事我在心里翻来覆去掂过，认定回屋没有危险。
我坐在离厨房最近的一张桌上，快吃完时，一个警察进来，在我对面坐下。堂倌端来一份早已备好等他的牛排。
“那个死贼认出来没有？”堂倌问。
“没有，身上连个纸片都没有。”
“到底怎么回事？”
“同晨报说的一样。女人的丈夫临时叫人叫走了。使女夜里放假，她一个人在宅里，睡不着，在各屋走动，这家伙就在窗口出现了。她吓得半死，抓起男人的步枪就打；打完就晕倒了。她醒过来，邻居们来了，叫了警察。

===Original===

The bird ran as far as the alley, and dropped dead. The bullet cut an artery in his neck.”
From the restaurant I went directly to our room and to bed, where I slept the sleep of exhaustion till sometime next forenoon, when I was roused by heavy knocking at my door. When I opened it, two big, red-faced men shouldered in, told me I was under arrest, and ordered me to put on my clothes. I washed and dressed. One of them put handcuffs on me and made me sit in a chair while they searched the room without finding anything that interested them.
The other then produced a bloodstained piece of paper, and, holding it out before my eyes, said:
“Did you ever see that before?”
I looked at it. It was the landlord’s receipt for our room rent. They had found it in a corner of one of Smiler’s pockets. Smiler had continually drummed it into me never to answer any questions in case we were arrested.
“Just clam up, kid. Tell them you’d rather not say anything till you get a lawyer. They might slug you, but don’t talk; that’s your only salvation.”
I remembered his advice and said to them as respectfully as I knew how:
“You gentlemen have arrested me for something, I don’t know what. But I would rather not make any statement till I can see an attorney and get his advice.”
The landlord now came to the room.
“Was this young man in this room night before last?”
“The bed wasn’t slept in,” he replied.
“That’s good, we’ll lock him up. Maybe he’ll talk then. You come along with us, too. We want you to identify the dead one.”
The city jail was but a few blocks away and all four of us walked there together. They took me inside and unlocked the handcuffs. A man at a desk asked my name and age. I was then taken out the back door, and across a yard into the jail building and locked in a cell with a solid iron door, in a remote corner of the building. They didn’t threaten to beat me up, and asked no more questions. A jailer came at noon with a trusty, who put a pan of very good stew, a tin cup of coffee, and half a loaf of bread in the cell.
I said to the jailer: “Mister, could I see a lawyer?”
He shut the door and locked it without answering me. The door wasn’t opened again till midnight. I was lying awake on a bunk fighting bedbugs in the dark. It seemed they were trying to eat me alive.
“Step out here, young man.”
I stepped out in my stocking feet.
“Put your shoes on, and come with us.”

===Chinese===

“那家伙一路跑到巷子，倒地死了。子弹切断了他脖子上的一根动脉。”
出了饭馆，我径直回我们的屋，上床睡了——那是一觉力竭的酣睡，直睡到第二天上午什么时候，被重重的打门声吵醒。我开了门，两条红脸大汉挤进来，说我被捕了，命我穿衣。我洗了脸穿好衣。一个给我戴上手铐，叫我坐在椅子上，他们把屋子搜了一遍，没搜出什么他们感兴趣的。
另一个随后掏出一张血迹斑斑的纸，举到我眼前，说：
“见过这个没有？”
我一看，是房东开的房钱收条。他们是在斯迈勒一只口袋的角上找着的。斯迈勒常常给我灌输：万一被捕，什么问题也不答。
“闭紧嘴，小子。告诉他们，不见律师什么也不说。他们兴许揍你，可别开口；那是你惟一的活路。”
我记着他的话，拿出我最恭顺的样子对两位说：
“二位先生抓我，总有个罪名，我不知是什么。可在见到律师、领了他的教之前，我不愿作任何供述。”
房东这时进屋来了。
“前天夜里这年轻人在这屋里么？”
“床没人睡过，”他答。
“很好，先关起来。也许他就肯说了。你也跟我们走一趟。要你去认尸。”
市监狱只隔几条街，我们四个一路走去。他们把我带进去，开了手铐。桌边一个人问了姓名年纪。随后我被从后门带出，穿过一个院子进了监狱楼，锁进一间装着实心铁门的囚室，在楼的僻角。他们没动手打我，也没再问话。晌午一个狱卒带着个杂役来，杂役往囚室里放了一盘很好的炖菜、一铁皮杯咖啡、半个面包。
我对狱卒说：“先生，能见律师么？”
他一言不发关上门锁了。直到半夜门才再开。我正醒着躺在铺上，黑地里同臭虫搏斗——它们像要把我活活吃掉。
“出来，年轻人。”
我趿着袜子出来。
“穿上鞋，跟我们走。”

===Original===

They were the same two men that arrested me. They took me out and up the streets into the next block where we stopped at an undertaker’s. They led me to a back room and up beside a table on which a figure was lying, covered by a long, sheetlike, white cloth. I stood there beside the table between the two officers for a moment. Then, suddenly, one of them snatched the sheet away from the upper part of the figure. The other shook me violently and shouted:
“Did you ever see that man before?”
I knew Smiler was there before he snatched the sheet off. When we went into the undertaker’s I felt they were going to show me his body, but I couldn’t understand why. I learned later that some people are unnerved at the sudden sight of a gruesome corpse, and, weakening, talk.
It hurt me to look at Smiler, his smile forever gone, changed to a hideous, snarl-like grin, and a gaping hole in his throat.
I couldn’t think of anything else, so I said: “I want to see an attorney, please.”
“You’ll get a lawyer when we get done with you. You were with that fellow when he was shot and we are going to charge you with an attempt to commit burglary if you don’t tell us where you were night before last.”
I was locked up again.
The following day the two officers took me out to the booking desk where I was formally charged, and on the morning of the third day I went into the police court for my hearing. One of the shyster lawyers, or “stir steerers,” as the bums call them, came over to me.
“They tell me you want a lawyer,” pointing to the officers.
“Yes, I do.”
“Got any money, or friends?”
“I’ve got fifteen dollars in the office﻿—and no friends.”
“Give me an order for the money, and I’ll look after you,” he said.
I gave him the order, he spoke to the two officers for a minute, and then asked the judge for a continuance of twenty-four hours. This was granted, and I was led back to the bedbugs in the dark cell.
The next day, when my case was called, the lawyer stood up and started talking.
“Sit down!” roared the judge.
He obeyed.
A woman wearing a thick veil was called. I could not distinguish her features. In a few words she told of shooting a man at her window. She saw but one man, the one she shot. She was excused and hastily left the courtroom.

===Chinese===

还是逮我那两个人。他们领我出门，上街，走到隔壁街区一家殡仪馆门前停下。他们把我带到后屋，领到一张长桌边；桌上躺着一个身形，盖着一幅床单似的白布。我在桌边那两位中间立了片刻。随后其中一个猛地掀开那身形上半身的白布，另一个狠狠摇我，吼道：
“见过这个人没有？”
他没掀布我就知道躺着的是斯迈勒。进殡仪馆时我就觉出他们要给我看他的尸首，只是想不出为的什么。后来我才明白：有人乍见骇人的死尸会吓破了胆，一软，就招了。
看斯迈勒那一眼叫我痛：他那个笑永远没了，扭成一副狰狞的、龇牙的怪相，喉咙上一个窟窿。
我想不出别的，只说：“我要见律师，劳驾。”
“收拾完你，律师自然有。他挨枪的时候你在场。你不交代前天夜里在哪儿，我们就告你图谋入室行窃。”
我又被关了回去。
第二天，两位警官把我提到登记处正式立了案；第三天早晨，我进违警罪法庭听审。一个下三滥律师——流浪汉们管这号人叫“揽牢客”——朝我走过来。
“他们说你想要个律师，”他朝警官们那边一指。
“是，想要。”
“有钱么，或有朋友？”
“办公室存着十五块——朋友没有。”
“给我一张取钱的条子，我照应你，”他说。
我把条子给了他。他同两位警官讲了一分钟话，随后向法官申请展期二十四小时。获准了，我又被押回黑牢里那群臭虫中间。
第二天，我的案子开庭，律师站起来刚开口。
“坐下！”法官吼道。
他坐下了。
一个蒙着厚面纱的女人被传来。我辨不出她的相貌。三言两语，她讲了在她窗口开枪打人的事。她只见着一个人——她打的那个。她退了庭，匆匆离开了法庭。

===Original===

My landlord was called, and testified that I was the roommate of the dead burglar; that I was not in the room the night he was killed, and when I appeared the following night I had on a different, a new suit.
The officers testified that they arrested me in the room and that I had refused to make any statement. The prosecutor stood up.
“Officer, when you accused this defendant of being an accomplice of the dead man, did he deny it?”
“No, sir, he did not.”
The prosecutor looked wise, and sat down with a satisfied air﻿—as if I had been found guilty.
“Anything more?” asked the judge.
The prosecutor got up again. “It’s as plain as the nose on your face, Your Honor. The dead man and this chap went out to rob that house. When the other fellow was shot, this chap tried to help him away, but he was too far gone. Then, what did he do?
“Your Honor,” he said, ominously pointing a finger at me, “he robbed his dead companion. The dead man’s pockets were turned inside out. But in his haste this man overlooked this﻿—this telltale blood-soaked receipt,” and he waved it about with great effect.
The judge frowned at me. The courtroom chair warmers craned necks in my direction.
“What about that new suit he has on?” continued the prosecutor. “He either bought it or stole it since the shooting. Why? Because the suit he wore that night had blood on it. Blood, Your Honor, blood,” he finished hoarsely.
I looked down at my shoes, wondering if the wizard was going to point out the dark spots on them that I hadn’t succeeded in scrubbing off entirely.
“Anything for the defense?” inquired the judge.
“We waive our defense,” said the stir steerer. He left the room, and luckily I never saw him.
“Defendant held to await the action of the grand jury.”
I was led away.
It was a relief to get into the county jail, away from the bedbugs. It was clean. I was put in a cell with a fine old Mormon farmer, charged with polygamy﻿—unlawful cohabitation, to use the legal phrase. He was well supplied with tobacco and food, from friends outside, which he shared with me. We were confined to the cells all the time, and I made no acquaintances while there. I did not try. I was much discouraged at this turn of things against me, but was still hopeful of getting out and returning to my father.

===Chinese===

我的房东被传来，作证说我是那死贼的同屋；死的那夜我不在屋里，第二天夜里露面时换了另一身——一身新衣。
警官们作证说，他们在那屋里逮的我，我拒绝作任何供述。检察官站起来。
“警官，你指认被告是死者同伙时，他否认了么？”
“没有，先生，没有。”
检察官一副洞悉一切的脸色，满意地坐下——仿佛我已经定了罪。
“还有么？”法官问。
检察官又站起来。“事情明白得像您脸上的鼻子，法官大人。死人和这小子出去抢那所宅子。同伙挨了枪，这小子想拖走他，可他不行了。于是呢，他干了什么？
“法官大人，”他伸出一根指头，不祥地指着我，“他劫了他死去的同伴。死人的口袋全翻了个里朝外。可他慌中漏掉了这个——这张泄露天机的、血泡透了的收条。”他把它挥得虎虎生风，效果十足。
法官朝我皱眉。旁听席上烤板凳的朝我伸脖子。
“他身上这身新衣怎么说？”检察官接着说。“不枪响后买的，就是偷的。为什么？因为他那晚穿的衣裳有血。血，法官大人，血，”他哑着嗓子收尾。
我低头看自己的鞋，纳帘这位半仙会不会点出鞋上我没能刷净的那些黑斑。
“辩护方有什么说的？”法官问。
“我们放弃辩护，”揽牢客说。他离了庭，幸而再没见过他。
“被告还押，候大陪审团起诉。”
我被带走了。
进县监狱是个解脱：总算躲开了臭虫。那里干净。我同一位置很好的摩门老农关一间——罪名是多妻，用法律话说，“非法同居”。他外面朋友接济，烟和吃食都充裕，同我分着用。我们一直关在号里，我在那儿没结识人。也没去结识。事情这样转过来对我不利，我十分丧气，可还抱着出去、回到父亲身边的指望。

===Original===

“You’ve been indicted by the grand jury,” said the jailer one morning, some three weeks later, “and today you go up to the big house.”
Utah then was a territory and all persons indicted were at once transferred to the custody of the United States marshal who, in addition to his other duties, acted as warden of the territorial penitentiary. An hour later I was taken out of my cell and turned over to a big, rawboned man with a worn pistol swinging from a holster on his belt.
“Young feller, I don’t put irons on none of ’em,” he said to me, tapping his gun. “Ef you want ter run, that’s yore business.”
In an hour I was at the penitentiary, where I made friends and incurred obligations that turned my thoughts away from home and sent me back on the road.

===Chinese===

“大陪审团起诉你了，”三个来星期后的一个早晨，狱卒说，“今天你上山大院。”
犹他那时还是领地，凡遭起诉的一律移交联邦法警看管；那法警一身兼差，兼着领地监狱的典狱长。一个钟头后，我从号里提出来，交给一个瘦骨嶙峋的大个子，腰间枪套里吊着一支旧枪。
“小伙子，我谁也不上镣，”他拍拍枪对我说。“你要想跑，那是你的事。”
一个钟头后，我到了监狱。在那里，我交上了朋友，也欠下了人情——正是这些，把我回家的念头引开，又把我送回了路上。

## IX / 九
===Original===

To say I was shocked, stunned, or humiliated on entering the penitentiary would not be the truth. It would not be true in nine cases out of any ten. It would be true if a man were picked up on the street and taken directly to a penitentiary, but that isn’t done. He is first thrown into a dirty, lousy, foul-smelling cell in some city prison, sometimes with an awful beating in the bargain, and after two or three days of that nothing in the world can shock, stun, or humiliate him. He is actually happy to get removed to a county jail where he can perhaps get rid of the vermin and wash his body. By the time he is tried, convicted, and sentenced, he has learned from other prisoners just what the penitentiary is like and just what to do and what to expect. You start doing time the minute the handcuffs are on your wrists. The first day you are locked up is the hardest, and the last day is the easiest. There comes a feeling of helplessness when the prison gates swallow you up﻿—cut you off from the sunshine and flowers out in the world﻿—but that feeling soon wears away if you have guts. Some men despair. I am sure I did not.
Inside the prison I was brought before a convict clerk who took my name, age, and nativity. I lied about them all. I couldn’t cheat the scales or a measuring machine, and they got my correct weight and height. I did screw my face up a little when I was photographed, and felt good about it. My clothes were not taken, nor was my hair cut. I had a bath and was turned loose in the yard where there were about one hundred prisoners, some, like myself, in outside clothes awaiting trial, and others in convict stripes doing time. The penitentiary was small then. There was no work except farming and gardening, which was all done by “cohabs,” Mormons convicted of unlawful cohabitation, and a very decent lot of men they were, never complaining of persecution, always ready to help their fellow prisoners, and freely dividing the food, money, and tobacco with which they were well supplied by friends and relatives.
The other prisoners played poker all day in the yard on blankets, and occasionally a game of baseball, when they could get up enough ambition. The food was fair. There was no discipline. Prisoners were expected to appear at their cells at evening to be locked in, and to stay in them till they were let out in the morning. They didn’t always do that.

===Chinese===

要说一进州监狱我是震惊、发懵还是受了辱，那不是实话。十件里九件都不是实话。若一个人从街上被直接捉进州监狱，那倒是真的；可没人这么办。他先被丢进城监狱一间肮脏、生虱、恶臭的牢房，有时还外加一顿毒打；两三天熬下来，天底下再没有什么能叫他震惊、发懵、受辱。能移去县监狱，叫他能灭了虱子、洗个身子，他简直高兴。等到审过、定过、判过，他从别的囚犯那里早弄清了州监狱什么样、该做什么、指望什么。手铐一扣上手腕，服刑就算开始了。锁上的头一天最难，最后一天最容易。牢门吞下你——把你同外头的阳光花草割开——那一刻自会涌上一阵无助；可有胆气的人，这阵感觉很快就磨掉了。有人绝望。我敢说自己没有。
进监后我被带到一名囚犯文书面前，登记姓名、年龄、籍贯。我一样样全撒了谎。秤和量高器骗不过，体重身高叫他们拿了个准。拍照时我把脸稍稍扭了扭，心里颇为得意。衣服没收，头发没剃。我洗了个澡，被撒进监院——院里约有一百名囚犯，有的像我一样穿着便装候审，有的穿着囚衣条纹服刑。州监狱那时还小。除农活园艺外无工可做，而那全由“同居犯”包了——因非法同居入狱的摩门教徒，而且是一伙很好的人：从不抱怨受迫害，总乐意帮难友，朋友亲戚给他们送来的吃食、钱和烟，他们大方地人人有份。
其余囚犯整日在院里的毯子上打扑克，偶尔攒得起劲头，也来一场棒球。伙食过得去。没有纪律。只要求囚犯晚上回牢房落锁，锁到早晨放人。他们未必回回照办。

===Original===

In prison parlance, the place was a “playhouse.”
The first man to speak to me in the yard was Shorty, the safe expert we had visited. He came directly up to me and put out his hand. “Kid, that was tough about Smiler. I wanted to see you both and apologize. I thought you put me in the hole for some coin, but I found out that the people lost just what you both said. I couldn’t imagine a gambling house with a six-hundred-dollar bankroll.”
Shorty was one of the patricians of the prison, a box man doing time for bank burglary. “I’ll put you in with the right people, kid. You’re folks yourself or you wouldn’t have been with Smiler.”
I had no friends in the place. But the fact that I had been with Smiler, that I had kept my mouth shut, and that Shorty had come forward to help me, gave me a certain fixed status in the prison that nothing could shake but some act of my own. I was naturally pleased to find myself taken up by the “best people,” as Shorty and his friends called themselves, and accepted as one of them.
Shorty now took me into the prison where we found the head trusty who was one of the “best people” himself, a thoroughgoing bum from the road. The term “bum” is not used here in any cheap or disparaging sense. In those days it meant any kind of a traveling thief. It has long since fallen into disuse. The yegg of today was the bum of twenty years ago.
“This party,” said Shorty, “is one of the ‘Johnson family.’ ” (The bums called themselves “Johnsons” probably because they were so numerous.) “He’s good people and I want to get him fixed up for a cell with the right folks.”
“Why don’t you go out and see George and his outfit? There’s an empty bunk in their cell.”
We hunted up “George and his outfit.” They knew all about me apparently, for George said, “Sure, put him in with us. If you don’t they’ll only stick some gay cat in there and we’d have to throw him out in the middle of the night.”
“What have they got on you, kid?” asked George.
I sat down with them all and went over the whole thing from the shooting of Smiler to my arrival at the prison.
“And you’ve made no statement yet?”
“No.”
“Not even to the shyster?” George inquired.
“Not even to the shyster,” I replied.
George turned to the others. “He’ll beat that case.”
“Sure, he will,” they all said.
“Judge Powers can beat that case before lunch any day,” said Shorty.

===Chinese===

用监狱的黑话说，这地方是座“游乐场”。
在院里头一个上来同我说话的，是矮子（Shorty）——我们拜访过的那位保险箱高手。他径直走到我跟前，伸出手。“孩子，笑面那事，真够惨的。我一直想见你们俩一面，道个歉。我原当你们把我塞进黑牢是为几个钱，后来查明白了，人家丢的正是你们说的那个数。我实在想不出一家赌窝才六百块的本钱。”
矮子是监狱里的贵族之一，做保险箱的行家，因银行夜盗坐牢。“我把你引给对的人，孩子。你自己就是同道，不然也不会同比面一起混。”
我在这里举目无亲。可我同比面待过、我闭紧过嘴、矮子又主动出来帮我——这几样给了我狱中一个固定的地位，除非我自己作死，什么也摇不动它。发现自己被“上等人”——矮子和他那伙人这样自称——收留、且被认作他们中的一员，我自然高兴。
矮子领我进监房，找到主管杂役——他本人也是“上等人”，道上十足的老流民。“流民”一词在这里不带任何轻贱意。那年月它指任何一种跑路的贼，如今早已废了。今天的耶格，就是二十年前的流民。
“这一位，”矮子说，“是‘约翰逊家’的。”（流民们自称“约翰逊”，多半因为人多。）“他是好人，我想给他安个牢房，同对的人住一处。”
“你何不去找乔治和他那伙？他们牢里空一个铺。”
我们寻着了“乔治和他那伙”。他们显然早把我的底摸清了，乔治说：“行，搁我们这儿。不搁，回头塞进来一只懒猫，半夜还得把他扔出去。”
“孩子，他们抓你什么罪名？”乔治问。
我在他们中间坐下，从头到尾——从笑面中枪到进监狱——倒了一遍。
“一句口供没录？”
“没有。”
“对那个讼棍也没录？”乔治追问。
“对那个讼棍也没有。”我答。
乔治转向众人。“他能赢这案子。”
“当然能赢。”众人齐说。
“鲍尔斯法官哪天都能赶在午饭前赢下这案子。”矮子说。

===Original===

“How’s he going to get the judge to defend him? He hasn’t a dime, and you’re talking about the best lawyer in the state,” George wanted to know.
It was Shorty’s time to get superior now. “Where did I get that two hundred dollars that’s out in the office? Didn’t him and Smiler bring it up here to me for my end of that chippy gambling house’s bankroll. The judge will take this case for a hundred; it’s only an hour’s work for him.”
George smiled. “Shorty, I knew damned well you’d do that,” and to me, “Kid, that’s what comes of bein’ on the square. If you’d burnt Shorty for his end of that coin, you’d have been here just the same, and you’d have got a beatin’ instead of a lawyer and a lot of good advice from real people.”
My case was disposed of right there. I had an attorney. But the bums had already tried me and I was found not guilty. All I had to do was wait for the day to come and I would be free. I was very grateful to them all, and tried to tell them. “Aw, forget it,” they said.
“You can pay me the money when you get out and are lucky,” said Shorty. “I’ll send for Judge Powers in the morning.”
At supper time I fell in line with my new friends and ate at the same table, after which we marched to our cells and were locked in. These scheming yeggs had managed to get possession of the most desirable cell in the prison, probably by bribing the trusty prisoner whose duty it was to look after the cell house. On each side of the cell was a framework ingeniously made of angle iron that contained two bunks, one above the other. The mattresses were filled with clean straw, the blankets were new and clean. George gave me a small feather pillow. In the center of our cell at the upper end was a table on which stood a fine Rochester oil lamp that gave plenty of light. Newspapers were produced, and the “best people” settled themselves for the evening.
George, who by reason of his age and experience was captain of the outfit, explained to me that, being the last man in the cell, it was my duty to empty the slop bucket in the morning and sweep out.
I must stop to describe briefly my three cellmates, all persistent and professional criminals, because of their influence on my after life.
A name on a prison register doesn’t usually mean anything. Although I knew George well later, I never learned from him his family name or birthplace.

===Chinese===

“他怎么请得动法官来辩护？他一个子儿没有，你说的可是全州最好的律师。”乔治要问个明白。
这回轮到矮子居高临下了。“账房里那两百块哪来的？不是他和笑面给我捎上来的、我那份骰子赌窝的本钱？这案子法官一百块就接；对他不过一个钟头的活。”
乔治一笑。“矮子，我他妈就知道你会这么办。”又对我说：“孩子，这就是实心的好处。你们当时若吞了他那份钱，这监狱你照进，只是挨一顿揍，而不是得一位律师、一伙真人的一番好指点。”
我的案子当场就了了。律师有了。可流民们早审过我，判的是无罪。我只消等日子一到，自由就是我的。我对他们个个感激，想说出来。“嗐，忘了吧。”他们说。
“出去发了迹再还我钱就是。”矮子说。“明早我就打发人请鲍尔斯法官。”
晚饭时我随新朋友们入列，同桌用饭，随后列队回牢，落锁。这伙有心计的耶格，把监狱里最中意的一间牢房攥到了手——多半是买通了专管监房的杂役。牢房两侧各有一副角铁巧妙焊成的床架，上下两层铺。褥子里塞着干净的麦秸，毯子又新又净。乔治给了我一只小羽枕。牢房上头顶端有一张桌，桌上一盏漂亮的罗切斯特油灯，照得满室通明。报纸拿了出来，“上等人”各自安顿过夜。
乔治——凭年纪和阅历做这伙人的头——向我交代：我既是牢里最末一个进来的，早晨倒恭桶、扫地便是我的本分。
这里得停一停，把我的三位同牢简单写一写：三个都是改不了行的职业罪犯，而他们对我后半生的影响非同小可。
监狱名册上的名字通常什么也不说明。我后来同乔治那么熟，却从没问出过他的姓和籍贯。

===Original===

To ask about those things in the underworld is to invite suspicion. All criminals conceal them carefully and resent questions. George was known on the road and to the police as “Foot-and-a-Half George” because of an injury to one of his feet that cost him a couple of toes and caused a slight limp. It happened in this way, as he told me one night when we were waiting to open up the powder house at a rock quarry and get a supply of fresh dynamite, caps and fuse.
“I always crush into these powder shacks for my ‘puff’ for two reasons; first, it’s always in good condition; second, if you buy it you’ve got to leave your mug with the storekeeper. He’s always suspicious of anybody buying explosives and is apt to remember you and cause trouble later in case of a pinch.
“I got this bum foot,” he said, sticking it out, pointing to the shoe with its bent-up toe, “through buying a roll of rotten fuse at an out-of-the-way general store in Montana. I was goin’ against P.O.’s then. I always favored post offices because in the small country ones the postmaster has to furnish the box himself and gets the cheapest one he can find. He don’t care because the government stands the loss if it’s a plain burglary from the outside.
“Besides that, you’re a cinch to get some coin and a bundle of stickers out of every P.O. You can peddle the stamps anywhere at sixty or eighty percent and they can’t be identified. Then again, if you do fall, the government don’t hang a lot of prior convictions on you and bury you. The limit for a P.O. is five years and you never get that if you use a little judgment. Yes, I’m strong for the government,” mused the veteran, reflectively.
“This caper I’m tellin’ you about was a third-class P.O. outside of Butte, Montana. It was soft, and good for a few hundred dollars so I decided to go against it alone. No use takin’ a bunch of thirsty bums along and stealin’ money for them to slop up in some saloon the next day. Anyway, I had a hole in the old box an’ a shot in it in half an hour. I strung the fuse to a window and touched it off from the outside. It spluttered along the floor and up to the door of the box, but nothing happened. After a few minutes I went back inside to put on a fresh piece of fuse. Just as I got in front of the box there was a roar, the door came off, and knocked me flat.

===Chinese===

在黑道上打听这些，等于自招嫌疑。罪犯个个把这些藏得严严实实，最恨人问。路上和警察那里，乔治被叫作“一尺半乔治”（Foot-and-a-Half George），因为他一只脚伤过，断了两根脚趾，走路微跛。伤是这么来的——一夜，我们在一座采石场等着开炸药库、取新炸药、雷管和导火索，他讲给我听：
“我回回潜进这种炸药棚去拿我的‘炮药’，为两个缘故：一，存着的从来是好的；二，买，就得把你的脸留给店家。买炸药的人，店家个个起疑，保准记得你，日后犯了案好给你添麻烦。
“这只跛脚，”他伸出来，指着那只鞋头卷起的鞋，“就是在蒙大拿一家犄角旮旯的杂货铺，买了一卷烂导火索落下的。那阵子我专做邮局。我一向偏爱邮局：乡下的小邮局，邮箱得邮局长自备，他专挑最贱的买。他才不上心——只要是从外头直简单地破门入室，亏的是政府。
“再有一条，每家邮局你稳拿几个钱、一沓邮票。邮票随处可销，六折八折就出手，没人认得来路。还有，你真折了，政府也不会把前科一串串往你身上挂、把你埋了。邮局顶格五年，稍有分寸，判不到顶格。是，我是拥护政府的。”老手若有所思地说。
“我这回要说的这票，是比尤特城外一家三等邮局。活嫩，值几百块，我便打定主意单干。犯不上带一帮酒虫流民去，偷来钱好叫他们第二天在酒馆里糟蹋。总之，半小时我就在那老家伙上开了眼、下了药。导火索拉到窗边，我在外头点了。它噼噼啪啪烧过地板、烧到箱门口，没动静。过了几分钟我回屋里换一截新索。刚走到箱子跟前，一声巨响，箱门飞了，把我砸了个仰面朝天。

===Original===

The edge of it caught my foot on the floor and smashed all the toes.”
“Did you get the coin?”
“You’re damn right, I did.
“After my wind came back I got the coin and stickers, limped outside where I had an old swift tied to a hitching rack. I had no saddle and it was a tough ride into Silver Bow Junction. But I got there before daylight and grabbed a rattler into Pocatello where Salt Chunk Mary put me away, got a doctor, and got rid of my stickers. That’s why I’m so particular about my fuse,” he concluded.
This grizzled old yegg was a byproduct of our Civil War. Apprentice to a village blacksmith, he was drafted into the army, where he learned the disruptive force of powder, and many other things useful to him in his profession of safe breaking. His rough war service, his knowledge of mechanics and explosives combined to equip him for what he became﻿—one of the pioneers of safe breaking. From black powder he turned to dynamite and afterward was one of the first to “thrash out the soup”﻿—a process used by the bums and yeggs for extracting the explosive oil, nitroglycerine, from sticks of “dan” or dynamite. He boasted that he had never done a day’s work outside of prison since he was mustered out of the army, except one year in a safe factory in the East where he went deliberately and worked for starvation wages to learn something of the construction of a very much used make of safe and its lock. Fortified with this knowledge, he followed that particular make of safe to many parts of the world, and, as he said, “knocked ’em open like ripe watermelons.”
George inherited a very ordinary set of features to start with. His war scars and rough bouts on the road and in prison hadn’t served to add anything to them in the way of refinement. His head rose to a point from all four sides. The “Sanctimonious Kid,” one of my cellmates, once said of George: “If you were to put a dime on the top of George’s head and start it sliding down the back it would fall inside his shirt collar; if you started it down his forehead it would wind up in his mouth, his lower jaw sticks out so far.” His eyes were small and cunning. They looked as if they had been taken out, fried in oil, and put back. Dead, pale blue and expressionless, they gave no hint of the cunning, always-busy brain behind them.

===Chinese===

门板边上蹭着我踩在地上的脚，把脚趾全砸碎了。”
“钱到手了没有？”
“你说呢，当然到手了。
“缓过气来，我拿了钱和邮票，一瘸一拐出得门来——栓马架上拴着我一匹备好的老快马。鞍也没得，一路颠到银弓枢纽站，真够受。可我赶在天亮前到了，扒上一列破车进波卡特洛，盐块玛丽把我藏起来，请了大夫，出手了邮票。所以我对导火索才这么挑剔。”他收了尾。
这个头发花白的老耶格，是我们内战的副产品。本是乡间铁匠的学徒，被征了兵，在军队里他领教了火药的掀翻之力，还有许多对开保险箱这一行有用的东西。粗粝的行伍、加上机械和炸药的本事，把他装备成了后来的他——开保险箱这行的开山之一。从黑火药转到炸药之后，他又是最早“洗汤”的人之一——流民和耶格们用来从“丹”（即炸药）药卷里提炼爆炸油、硝化甘油的手法。他夸口说，自打退伍，监狱外头他没正经干过一天活，只在东部一家保险箱厂待过一年——那是他特意去的，领着饿死人的工钱，专为摸透一种用得极广的箱子的构造和锁。揣着这门学问，他跟着那种箱子走遍了半个天下，用他的话说，“敲它们跟敲熟西瓜一个样”。
乔治这张脸，起初继承来的就平常。战争留下的疤、道上和狱中的粗斗，又没给它添半分斯文。他的脑袋四面往顶上收成一个尖。我的同牢之一“假圣人小子”这样说过乔治：往乔治头顶放一毛钱，顺着后脑滑下去，准掉进他衬衫领子里；顺着脑门滑下去，准收在他嘴里——他那下巴颏子撅得那么远。眼睛小而奸，像被抠出来在油里煎过又装回去的。死灰的淡蓝，毫无表情，透不出半点后头那颗奸猾、永远在转的脑子。

===Original===

His nose wasn’t worth looking at, just a small knob of soft red flesh, long since collapsed and hanging like a pendant from between his eyes. He was of medium height, broad, heavy and rugged. With all his ill-favored appearance and his rolling, limping walk that seemed to start at his shoulders and work its way down into his legs, he had a heart of gold and oak, and knowing him one readily forgave him ungovernable temper and violent outbursts of rage. He was square. His life was stormy and his death sudden and violent as Smiler’s. But that has another place in this story.
The Sanctimonious Kid was, in point of years and experience, second to George, and naturally second in say in the cell. All matters of importance were submitted to George first, then to “Sanc” and lastly to “Soldier Johnnie,” the third man. Nothing was submitted to me. I had no say about anything. All I had to do was to keep my mouth shut and listen all I wanted to.
Sanc had everything that George lacked. Tall, six feet, slender and soft stepping, more active than most men half his size, you would not suspect him of two hundred pounds, solid flesh and bone. Straight without stiffness, natural, like an Indian. Dark hair, eyes and skin. Handsome, intelligent. Years after, I saw him in the dock of a crowded courtroom in a big city. His head was the finest, his face the handsomest, and his poise the surest of any there, from the judge down to the alternate juror. His nose, eyes and forehead might have been those of a minister or divinity student. But there was a hard look about his mouth, and something in his jaw that suggested the butcher. He was educated and a constant reader. Whether it was his appearance or his careful manner of speech that got him his monoger, “The Sanctimonious Kid,” I never knew. He was serving a short sentence for house burglary, at which he was an expert.
We traveled together for several years after he was released, and I found him one of the squarest and most resourceful thieves I ever knew. At last, after one of the cleverest prison escapes on record, he went to Australia where he was hanged for the murder of a police constable.
Soldier Johnnie, who had served a term in the army, was the youngest of the three. He was an industrious and trustworthy yegg who made his living serving as “target” or outside man, for the yegg mobs that preyed on country banks.

===Chinese===

他那鼻子不值一看：一小坨软红的肉疙瘩，早塌了，像个坠子挂在两眼之间。中等身量，宽、重、粗壮。长相虽然恶劣，走路又晃又跛——那跛像是从肩膀发起、一路传到腿上的——他心肠却是金子和橡木做的；认得他的人，很容易原谅他那压不住的火气和狂怒的爆发。他是条汉子。他的一生风风雨雨，死得同笑面一样突然、一样暴。不过那在后文另有一处。
“假圣人小子”（The Sanctimonious Kid）论年纪和阅历仅次于乔治，在牢里话语权自然也第二。大事先呈乔治，再呈“桑克”（Sanc），最后呈“大兵约翰尼”（Soldier Johnnie）——第三位。没有事呈到我这里。我什么也插不上嘴，只消闭紧嘴，爱听多少听多少。
桑克样样都有乔治没有的。高个子，六英尺，细挑，脚步轻软，比多半半个身量的人都麻利——你绝想不到他有两百磅，瓷实的肉和骨头。挺拔而不僵硬，天生地像印第安人。深色的头发、眼睛和皮肤。英俊，聪明。多年以后，我在一座大城市拥挤的法庭上见过他站在被告席：满庭上下，从法官到候补陪审员，头颅数他的最好，脸数他的最俊，气度数他的最稳。他的鼻、眼、额，尽可以安在一位牧师或神学学生脸上。可口周有一股狠相，腮帮里有种东西叫人想起屠户。他受过教育，读书不辍。他的诨名“假圣人小子”究竟得自这张脸，还是得自他讲话那副斟酌腔，我始终不知道。他服的是一桩入室盗窃的短刑——那一行他是行家。
他出狱后我们结伴走了好几年，我发现他是我认得的贼里最方正、最有法的一个。末了，在史上最漂亮的越狱之一之后，他去了澳大利亚，因杀一名警员被绞死了。
大兵约翰尼当过一期兵，是三人里最年轻的。他是个勤快可靠的耶格，靠给袭击乡间银行的耶格帮伙当“靶子”——即外围把风——谋生。

===Original===

The “target” is the most reliable man in the mob. To him is given the job of sticking up the town bull if he appears while the others are inside. He is the first one to get shot at and the last. It’s his job to carry the heavy artillery and stand off the natives while the others get the coin, and then to cover the getaway.
He was born lucky. His face and figure were neutral. A hard man to pick up on his description. Medium size and weight. After one look at him you couldn’t say whether his hair was brown or black, whether his eyes were gray or blue. Quiet, unobtrusive, soft-spoken, a copper would hesitate before halting him on the street.
Such were my companions, guides, friends and philosophers, day and night, till the day of my trial, which soon came.
Shorty’s letter to Judge Powers brought one of his office men out to see me the next day, who got the points in my case and went away with one hundred dollars of Shorty’s money. I felt better after his visit, he was so fresh, vigorous, confident. “Nothing to it, young man,” he said breezily, “I’ll be in court when you appear to plead and have a day set for your trial; that is, if they want to waste their time trying you.”
I went back into the prison yard with his card in my hand and gave it to Shorty. He passed it to George and then to Sanc.
“I know him,” Sanc said. “He is in Powers’ firm. Powers will tell him what to do. You are all right. True, he is but a second-class man, but that’s not so bad. These second-class lawyers can skin a first-class bunko man any day in the week. I see no reason why he cannot skin a third-class judge in a territorial court.”
I soon saw that these three cellmates of mine practically controlled the inside of the prison. They had brains and character backed by courage and the valuable background of a reputation for doing things outside.
I say they had character because, while they did wrong things, they always tried to do them in the right way and at the right time. The thief who goes out and steals money to pay back room rent rather than swindle his poor landlady has character. The one who runs away without paying her has no character. The thief who holds out a lady’s watch on his pal to give to his girl has no character.
In the underworld one has good or bad character as in any other layer of society.

===Chinese===

“靶子”是帮伙里最可靠的人。旁人进去时镇上的条子若冒头，顶住他的就是靶子的差事。头一个挨枪的是他，最后一个也是他。扛重家伙、在旁人取钱时顶住本地人、再掩护脱身，都是他的活。
他生来走运。脸和身量都不着痕迹，凭长相极难认领。中等个头中等块头，看过一眼，你说不准他的头发是棕是黑、眼睛是灰是蓝。安静，不显眼，说话轻，条子在街上截他之前都得踌躇一下。
这些便是我的同伴、向导、朋友兼哲人，日也随，夜也随，直到我开审的日子——那日子来得很快。
矮子给鲍尔斯法官的信，第二天就引来了他事务所的一个人。他取走案情的要点，带走矮子一百块钱。他一走我便宽心了：他那样新鲜、精神、笃定。“小案子，年轻人，”他轻快地说，“你出庭答辩定审期时我到场；就是说——如果他们肯浪费时间审你的话。”
我攥着他的名片回到监院，递给矮子。矮子递给乔治，又递给桑克。
“我认得他，”桑克说，“鲍尔斯所里的。鲍尔斯会指点他怎么办。你没事了。不错，他不过是二流人物，可二流不赖。这类二流律师，一个礼拜随便哪天都能剥一流骗子的皮。我看不出他有什么道理剥不掉领地法庭上一个三流法官的皮。”
我很快看明白了：我这三 位同牢，实际掌着监狱的里面。他们有脑子、有品性，底下垫着胆气和“外头办成过事”的值钱名声。
我说他们有品性，是因为他们虽干坏事，却总力求干得在理、干得是时候。一个贼，与其赖掉穷房东太太的房钱，宁可出门偷钱来付——这样的贼有品性。卷铺盖跑掉不付的，没有品性。瞒着搭档扣下一块女表送自家姑娘的，没有品性。
黑道上同社会任何一层一样，人品分好坏。

===Original===

The thief who pays off borrowed money, debts, or grudges has a good character among his fellows; and the thief who does the reverse has a bad character. Thieves strive for good character and make as great sacrifices to keep it as men do anywhere else. A burglar can have friends, but he has to pay his room rent or he will lose them, and they will despise him.
Because of this quality these three men had money in the prison office, sent them by friends at liberty.
They had visitors frequently, who kept them well supplied with books and magazines. The evening mail brought newspapers from many cities. They kept well informed, particularly about criminal and legal doings. The papers were carefully read at night, and the next morning routed throughout the prison till, torn and read ragged, they found their way into the hands of the lowliest bindle stiff in the farthest corner of the yard.
There were fat times when we didn’t go into the dining room for a week. A half-gallon bucket of milk was left at the cell every evening. Loaves of fresh, hot bread were smuggled up from the bakeshop, and juicy steaks from the guards’ quarters. These creature comforts helped to take the curse off the place, and mitigate the prison pangs. Our light was put out, not when the nine o’clock bell rang, but when George, or Sanc, or Johnnie felt like going to sleep. The guards looked the other way when they went by in their felt-soled shoes, on their night rounds through the prison.
In the prison yard there was a deep well from which water for bathing and other uses was pumped to a large tank on the prison roof. The pump was manned by four prisoners who had to work in one-hour shifts. A gang of eight men were detailed each week for this work, which was not hard﻿—nothing more than exercise﻿—and no one ever complained. My name was called one Saturday evening and I was instructed to report at the pump the following Monday morning. I thought nothing of it, and would have pumped cheerfully. But that night Soldier Johnnie, who was something of a jail lawyer and agitator for his and his friends’ rights, remarked that they were wrong in forcing me to work because I had not been convicted of any crime and that I ought to refuse to do it. The other two took the matter up and it was argued pro and con.

===Chinese===

借的钱、欠的账、结的怨，还清的贼，同道里人品好；反着来的，人品坏。贼们为求人品，肯作的地道牺牲，不输给任何地方的人。夜贼可以有朋友，可他得付房钱，不然朋友也要丢，还要招人小看。
因为有这一条，这三位在监狱账房里有朋友从外头汇来的钱。
他们常有访客，书报杂志源源不断。晚间邮件送来许多城市的报纸。他们消息灵通，尤其是刑案和司法的动静。报纸夜里精读，次日在全监流转，直转得稀烂，末了落到院子里最远角落、最不起眼的捆杖客手里。
也有肥得流油的日子：整礼拜不进饭堂。每晚牢房门口搁半加仑一桶的牛奶。烤房里热乎乎的整条面包偷偷递上来，卫兵灶上多汁的牛排也来。这些口腹之惠，替这地方解了几分咒，缓了缓牢狱的苦。熄灯不按九点的铃，看乔治、桑克或约翰尼几时想睡。看守夜里趿着毡底鞋巡过监房，经过我们时眼睛望别处。
监院里有一口深井，洗浴等用水全凭泵打上监狱屋顶的大水箱。泵由四名囚犯值守，一个钟头一换班，每周抽八人当这趟差。差事不重——不过活动筋骨——从没人抱怨。一个礼拜六傍晚点了我的名，叫我礼拜一早晨到泵上报到。我没当回事，本会欢欢喜喜去摇泵。可当夜大兵约翰尼——他多少算个监狱律师、专替自己和朋友的权利鼓噪——发话说：我未定罪就逼我做工，是他们理亏，我该拒绝。另两位也接了这个茬，正反辩了一场。

===Original===

They were pretty technical about it, and the weight of opinion was that it would be establishing a dangerous precedent for an unconvicted man to do any work of any kind in the prison and a test case should be made of it.
I was willing enough, and, looking back at it now, I believe I was glad of the chance to do something to raise myself in the estimation of these distinguished characters.
Monday morning I refused to work, explaining to the officer in charge that I was not lazy, but felt they had no right to order me to work before I was convicted. He appeared surprised, and took me to the office of the prison captain. He heard me out, and turning to the guard, said: “Throw this fresh kid in the cooler and leave him there till he gets ready to work.”
I was given an old pair of overalls and a cotton shirt to wear in the “cooler,” where my outside clothes would have been ruined. The cooler or dark cell was the same as other cells, except that there was nothing in it and the door was solid, admitting no light. The floor and walls were of thin steel and very cold. I slept on the floor without any kind of bedding or cover. There was but one fixture in the cell, a sheet-metal bucket smelling strongly of chloride of lime. My sentence carried the further punishment of bread and water, one thick slice a day and about a quart of water.
A guard opened the cell in the afternoon the first day and put in a quart tin cup of water but no bread. As he was closing the cell, he said, not unkindly: “You’re a damned fool, kid. You’d better weaken and promise to go on the pump. I’ll tell the captain now, if you want, and you won’t have to freeze in here tonight.”
“No, I won’t weaken,” I declared.
“All right, but if you change your mind just rap on the door with that tin cup.”
My teeth were chattering before nine o’clock that night, it was so cold. I could not sleep on the cold steel floor and walked up and down in my stocking feet all night. The next morning it became warmer and I slept in fits till noon when the guard came with my bread and a fresh cup of water. The bread was about half eaten when something got between my teeth that made me stop chewing, hungry as I was.
I examined it, lying on my belly at the bottom of the door, where there was a crack of light. It was a piece of chicken quill about an inch long.

===Chinese===

他们抠得相当讲究，公论是：未定罪之人在监狱里做任何工，都是开一个危险的先例，该拿这一件立个案。
我本就乐意，如今回头想，我相信我还巴不得：好做一件事，在这几位显赫人物眼里抬高我一分。
