他们成桌成桌地坐着喝酒——酒精，最贱也最致命的那种——家什千奇百样：铁皮罐、白镜杯、啤酒杯、大肚杯、裂了纹的汤碗——凡是老板能从收破烂手里买来、摔不破的都行。他们话多。难得笑，从不打架——笑和打架的力气早没了。喝不动了、买不起了，便摇摇晃晃、连爬带蹭挪到屋后一片空地上，仰面一躺排成一排，后脑抵墙，一人一顶帽子扣在紫胀的鬼脸上。那杂役兼厨子兼酒鬼仔细看着睡的人。估摸谁“睡透”了、站得起来了，就狠狠踢他起来，把他们轰到街上去讨、去借、去偷几个小钱，再来一轮。太常见的是踢不应了：他掀起那顶破帽子，朝青紫的脸看一眼，摇铃叫运尸车。
这一伙可怜人，来自天涯海角、社会的层层叠叠，被那神秘而不可抗拒的酒渴抽打得毫无抵抗之力，在酒窖里把自己喝成紫色，死在地板上，或死在城里的人行道底下。酒窖绝迹了；有谁惋惜它们么？酒鬼也绝迹了。顶上来的是牙买加姜精的瘾客、罐装固体酒精和木酒精的饮者。他们难研究、难归类：命太短。
城里最声名狼藉的一家酒窖在卡尼街以下的克莱街，路过那一片，我从不错过。一天夜里我才跨进门，门口一个酒鬼喊了声“黑车来了”，没命蹿了出去。几个最清醒的从后门跑了个精光。我朝前门走，条子已经进来了。我头一个叫他们拿了；条子把我往车里一扔，我正分辩，他说：“哦，有话跟法官说去，我不是法庭。我是个一月一百块的条子，成天把你们这帮酒鬼往车里扔进扔出，扔出一身虱子，怪我谁叫我干这行。”他一脸的灰心。
我对坐牢早起了茧，不慌：料他们也把我怎么样不了。

===Original===

But going over in the wagon my mind turned to the burglary of the week before. I felt uncomfortable and thought of my room and was thankful that it was “clean,” and of the safety box and the keys I had made, and every other crooked transaction in my short life of outlawry. “No danger,” I thought as the wagon rattled over the blocks, “if I cover myself up. My name is William Brown, I am nineteen years old. I was born in Pocatello, Idaho. My parents died when I was fourteen. I have supported myself since, selling papers, washing dishes, and working on farms. I came to this city this morning and am going to get a job. I went into that place out of curiosity.”
I just had this short biography put together when we got to the city prison. It was in the basement of a building that stood where the Hall of Justice now is, and it was the foulest I ever saw, worse than the first one at home. There was a busy spot, that corridor in the city prison! Officers hurrying in and out, lawyers haggling at the desk about releases for prisoners, “fixers,” hawk-eyed and rapacious, lurked about, cheap bail bondsmen coining misery, ignorance, and crime into thick nickels and thin dimes, and on the long bench by the wall sat a thin, wrinkled, poorly dressed woman of fifty, holding a boy’s hand in hers. He sat beside her, silent and stubborn. She was crying.
Ten of us from the wine dump were lined up at the desk. The officer in charge said to the man at the desk. “We’ll vag the chronics and charge the new ones with drunk.” The chronic winos gave their names, etc., and were hurried away down the corridor. Another chap and I were the only “new ones.” We answered all questions without angering anybody and getting knocked down. I protested that I wasn’t drunk. The desk man said: “Oh, that’s all right, you can go out in the morning at five o’clock on the broom.” They searched me, but took nothing except a pocketknife.
We were put in a large cell about twenty feet square, directly across the corridor from the desk where prisoners were received and registered. The front of this cell was of upright iron bars and admitted plenty of light from the corridor. The cell was foul smelling from a fixture in one corner that seemed to connect directly with a sewer. A broken water faucet leaked continually, with a hissing sound.

===Chinese===

可囚车一路走，我的心思转到上礼拜那桩案子上。浑身不自在，想起我的屋子——幸好“干净”——想起保管箱和我配的钥匙，想起我短短亡命生涯里每一件邪门交易。“没有危险，”车轱辘轧过一个个街区，我想，“只要我把自己包严。我名叫威廉·布朗，十九岁，生在爱达荷州波卡特洛，十四岁上爹娘死了，打那以后卖报、刷碗、做农活，自食其力。今天早上刚进城，正要找工作。进那家店是出于好奇。”
这套小传刚拼好，车到了市监狱。它坐落在今天司法厅所在的那座楼的地下室里，是我见过的最脏的，比老家那第一座还糟。市监狱那条走廊，好一个热闹去处！警官们出出进进，律师们在案头为放人讨价还价，“摆平客”们鹰眼灼灼、贪心勃勃地埋伏着，廉价的保释贩子把苦难、无知和罪案铸成一枚枚厚五分、薄一毛，靠墙的长凳上坐着一个五十来岁、干瘦、衣衫寒素的女人，手里攥着一只男孩的手。他挨她坐着，沉默而倔。她在哭。
酒窖来的我们十个在案前排成一列。带队的警官对案头的人说：“老油子按流浪办，新来的告酗酒。”老酒鬼们报了姓名等等，被匆匆押下走廊。另一个小伙子同我是仅有的“新来的”。我们答了所有的问题，没惹恼谁、没挨打。我抗议说我没醉。案头的说：“哦，没事，明早五点你扫帚出去。”他们搜了我，只收走一把小刀。
我们被关进一间约二十英尺见方的大囚室，正对收押登记的案桌，隔着走廊。囚室前脸是直立铁栏，走廊的光透得足足的。屋里恶臭，出自一只角上的什么装置，那东西仿佛直通阴沟。一只破水龙头嘶嘶地漏个不停。

===Original===

About ten men were there, some sleeping on the damp asphalt floor, some on the benches that lined the wall; some stood expectantly at the front waiting to be released, others squatted on their haunches in the corners, staring vacantly at the floor.
I sat on the bench and wondered about “going out on the broom” in the morning. I could have asked my cellmates. I looked for the other “new one” that came with me from the wine dump; he was asleep on the floor. I decided not to ask, but to wait till morning and find out for myself. Even at that age I had stumbled upon one truth, and that is, The best way to get misinformed is to ask a lot of questions. Nearly all my life has been spent in the company of unfortunate people, and while I never looked upon myself as an unfortunate, I was always accepted as one of them. Whatever knowledge I have of them was gleaned by looking and listening, and it is much more accurate than any I could have got by asking impertinent and close-up questions. Your best friend would give you a surly answer if you were to ask him the time of day an hour after his watch had been stolen. Ask any one-legged stranger how he lost his limb, and you will get something like this: “Well, you see, it was this way﻿—I got run over by a ferryboat.”
Of all the many friends I have made since I gave up my larcenous life, Shorty is one of the most respected and highly prized. His friends are legion, and in every state in the Union; not that Shorty has traveled there and made friends of them, but that they have traveled here and made a friend of him.
Shorty’s newsstand is on a busy corner in the shadow of a skyscraper owned by ex-Senator Phelan, one of California’s most distinguished men, and there, under his patronage and protection, he stands on his stumps (“cut-down legs” he calls them), early and late, serving thousands with papers and periodicals. He hobnobs with doctors, lawyers, businessmen, and politicians. He finds lost children and dogs, and returns them to their owners. Shopgirls and strangers ask him which is the best show of the week. Men around town consult him about the chances of a horse in tomorrow’s race. He can borrow more money on his I.O.U. than many businessmen in his block and pays on the minute. He is no stranger at the banks on the opposite corners.

===Chinese===

屋里约摸十个人：有的睡在潮湿的沥青地上，有的睡在沿墙的长凳上；有的满怀指望地立在栏前等着开释，有的蹲在角落里，两眼发直地盯着地板。
我坐在凳上，琢磨明早的“扫帚出去”。原可以问问同室的。我找同我从酒窖来的那位“新来的”；他睡在地板上。我决定不问，等天亮自己弄明白。小小年纪，我已撞上一条真理：想得到错误的消息，最好的法子就是多问。我这一辈子，几乎全在不幸的人们中间过；虽说我从不把自己当不幸的，人家却向来拿我当他们中的一个。我对他们的那点了解，全凭看和听，比冒冒失失贴着鼻子问来的准得多。你最好的朋友，手表失窃一个钟头之后，你问他几点钟，他也没好气。随便问哪个独腿的陌生人腿是怎么没的，你得到的无非是：“哦，是这样的——叫渡船碾的。”
自我撚下偷儿营生以来结交的众多朋友里，短子是最受敬重、最教人珍视的一个。他的朋友遍天下，联邦各州无州无之——倒不是短子云游四方去结识他们，是他们四方云游来此，结识了他。
短子的报摊设在一个热闹的街角，在他所谓加利福尼亚最显赫的人物之一、前参议员菲兰的摩天楼下；在他的庇荫和照拂下，他靠两条“截桩”（他这么叫他的截腿）早出晚归地立着，为几千人递报纸杂志。他同医生、律师、生意人、政客平起平坐。他寻回走失的孩子和狗，送还失主。女店员和生客问他本周哪出戏最好。城里人向他打听明天那匹马的胜算。他凭一纸借条能借到的钱，比他那一街区许多生意人都多，还款分秒不差。对面街角那几家银行，他是熟面孔。

===Original===

His reputation for truth and veracity is such that if he were to tell me the water had all disappeared from the bay I wouldn’t go down to look.
Traveling in search of adventure when young, he lost his legs under a train, but instead of despairing and sinking under this terrible misfortune, he braced himself and, picking out a bare corner on the street, built up, day by day, year by year, a business that has made him independent and respected. I stopped one day at his stand, and, looking at his massive chest, broad shoulders, and fine head, I saw him in fancy a splendid figure of a man towering ruggedly above his fellows.
“Shorty,” I asked, “how tall were you before you lost your legs?”
He flashed me a savage look; then remembering we were friends his eyes fell to his stumps and he said with a laugh, “Oh, I was taller by two feet.”
That’s all one gets by asking questions that wake painful memories.
From my position on the bench I could see every prisoner brought to the desk. About ten o’clock there was a stir in the hall and several policemen came in with Chinamen from a gambling-house raid. This was before they had cut off their queues, and instead of handcuffing their prisoners the cops came in driving the silent, stolid Chinese before them like charioteers. Each cop had the tails of three Chinamen’s queues in each hand. Ahead of the procession walked a white man with a heavy bag of gold in his hand which he put on the desk, and waited till the names of the prisoners had been taken. Then they all went back up the corridor out of my sight﻿—the Chinamen back to Chinatown to their gambling, and the bag of gold into the bond-and-warrant clerk’s office to insure their appearance in court.
Several times during the night men were brought in, questioned at great length, searched thoroughly, and led away to another part of the prison﻿—felony cases. About midnight two young fellows about my age were brought in by a copper and stood up before the desk.
“Vag these two hypos,” said the cop to the desk man. He searched them most carefully, finding a small package in the torn lining of one’s coat. The boy begged for it piteously. “I’ll croak, officer, if you take it away from me.” The cop gave him to the waiting trusty. “Throw him in.” He was put in with us. Nothing was found on the other hypo, and he was thrown in too.

===Chinese===

他那诚实的名声响到这般田地：他若告诉我，湾里的水全没了，我也不会跑下去看。
年轻时出门闯荡，他叫火车轧去了双腿；可他没有绝望，没有被这桩大祸压垮，而是挺起来，在街上拣了个光秃秃的角落，一天一天、一年一年，攒起一份叫他独立、叫人敬重的营生。一天我在他摊前站住，望着他宽阔的胸膛、厚实的肩膀和漂亮的大脑袋，恍恍惚惚看见一个雄伟的男子，嶙峋地高出同伴一头。
“短子，”我问，“没腿之前你多高？”
他狠狠瞪了我一眼；随即想起我们是朋友，眼睛落到他的截桩上，笑道：“哦，高出两英尺。”
这就是打听人家伤心事换来的全部。
从我凳上的位置，押到案前的每个囚犯我都看得见。十点来钟，走廊里一阵骚动，几个警察押着抄赌场抄来的中国人进来。那是他们还没剪辫子的年月；条子们不上手铐，像赶战车似的把一声不响、木呆呆的中国人赶在前头：每只手里攥着三个中国人的辫梢。队伍前头走着个白人，手里提一袋沉沉的金子；他把金子搁在案上，等着录完囚犯姓名。随后一行人沿走廊走回去，出了我的视野——中国人回唐人街接着赌，那袋金子进保释拘票科，保他们到庭。
夜里陆续有人押进来，盘问许久，搜检彻底，押去监狱另一头——重案。半夜前后，一个条子押来两个同我年纪相仿的小伙子，立在案前。
“这两个‘皮下针’按流浪办。”条子对案头的说。他把两人搜了个底朝天，在其中一个撕破的衣衬里搜出一个小包。那孩子苦苦哀求。“您拿走了它，我会死的，警官。”条子把他交给候着的杂役。“扔进去。”他同我们关在一处。另一个皮下针身上什么也没搜出，也扔了进来。

===Original===

They immediately began comparing notes and taking stock, walking up and down the center of the cell nervously. They were in rags and unwashed, their shoes were broken and had no laces, and the tops flapped open showing their bare ankles. They seemed utterly unconscious of their sad condition and walked and talked as briskly as two brokers on Montgomery Street discussing the markets.
“He got my plant, Georgie,” said the first one, “but you saved yours, didn’t you, Georgie? Gee, Georgie, but you’re a fox.” His tones were honey.
“Never mind that,” the other replied; “you don’t have to ‘Georgie’ me. You’re in with what ‘gow’ I’ve got. Let’s bang it up before they come in and take it away from us. See if you can hustle some matches.”
The match seeker glanced sharply around him. When his eye fell on me I produced some matches. “Got any smokes?” I handed him a pack of cigarettes. He took two, gave one to his partner, Georgie, and returned the pack. His mind seemed detached from the cell. He took the matches and cigarettes from me without a word, as if he had reached up to a mantel and taken them off it. Georgie fished about the front of his trousers and brought up a tobacco sack that had been hanging suspended from a button. The sack contained his “plant,” an eyedropper with a hypodermic needle soldered to it with sealing wax, and a small paper of morphine in a little tin box.
They went to a back corner of the cell and prepared their shot. About a spoonful of water and some of their meager store of “morph” were put in the tin box and matches were burned under it till it boiled, completely dissolving the morphine. It was then drawn up into the eyedropper, and Georgie injected his portion into his arm. The other boy did the same with his portion. Their outfit was carefully put away in the tobacco bag and suspended again from a button down inside the front of Georgie’s trousers. Nobody paid any attention to them. They took their shot as coolly as if they had been in their room, or under a sidewalk. They seemed a little more interested in their surroundings in a few minutes. The one I gave the cigarettes to came over to me rubbing his hands briskly, smiling.
“Give us a couple more of them smokes, young fellow.”
“I’d like to buy some,” I said, “if I could.”
“Got money?”
“Yes, a couple of dollars.”
“I’ll fix it for you,” he said most condescendingly.

===Chinese===

两人立刻交换起情报、盘起家底，在囚室当中神经质地走来走去。他们衣衫褴褛，脸也没洗，鞋开了口、没了带，鞋帮耷拉着，露出光脚踝。可他们对自身的惨境浑然不觉，走起路、说起话，活像蒙哥马利街上议论行情的两个经纪人。
“他抄了我的家什，乔吉，”第一个说，“可你保住了你的，是不，乔吉？喍，乔吉，你真是只狐狸。”他的腔调甜得像蜜。
“少来这套，”另一个答，“‘乔吉’不必叫。我这点‘货’有你一份。趁他们没进来抄走，咱们先打上一针。看看能不能寻几根火柴。”
寻火柴的目光利利地扫了一圈，落到我身上，我摸出几根火柴。“有烟么？”我递给他一包纸烟。他抽了两支，一支给他的伙计乔吉，把烟包还我。他的心思像不在囚室里。他接火柴接烟一言不发，仿佛从壁炉架上信手取下来的。乔吉在裤裆前摸索一阵，摸出一只烟丝袋——原本拴在一粒扣子上荡着。袋里是他的“家什”：一只眼药滴管，用火漆焊着一枚皮下注射针，还有一个小铁盒，盒里一纸包吗啡。
他们到囚室后角预备他们的一针。一小勺水加他们那份可怜的“货”放进铁盒，底下烧火柴，烧到沸腾，吗啡化尽，吸进滴管。乔吉把他那份打进胳膊，另一个孩子照样打了自己那份。那套家什仔细收回烟丝袋，照旧拴上扣子，荡进乔吉裤裆前。谁也没理会他们。他们打针打得从容，仿佛在自家屋里、在人行道底下。几分钟后，他们像是才对四周生了点兴趣。接我烟的那个蹭过来，两只手快活地搓着，笑着。
“再给两支烟抽抽，小伙子。”
“我倒想买几支，”我说，“要买得着。”
“有钱？”
“有，两块来钱。”
“我给你办去，”他说得极其屈尊。

===Original===

He went over to the bars and shouted, “Hey, Finnerty!”
In a minute the head trusty, a thin, weazened, rat-eyed, undersized character, came up.
“Cigarettes, matches? Sure. Anything else?”
I produced a dollar.
“What’s the matter with a can of coffee and some snails?” said the hypo.
“Get whatever you can,” I said, giving him the dollar.
Finnerty disappeared, and in a surprisingly short time came back with cigarettes, a gallon fruit tin half full of splendid coffee, and a bag of snails. The two young fellows took charge of the coffee and snails, spread a newspaper on the floor, and very cordially invited me to help myself.
While we were having our lunch the talk was diplomatically turned to dope and the shortage that menaced my two hypo friends and the sufferings they would undergo when there was no more and the habit came on, and the necessity for a shot in the morning as a bracer for them when they faced the judge. They grew so despondent over their plight that when we were done eating they decided to shoot up the small portion of white stuff they had left. They brightened up after this operation was over, and things looked rosier.
“Just think, Georgie,” said the talkative one, looking at me, “what a four-bit piece would do for us. What a lifesaver! We’ll both get a sixer in the morning if we go in front of the judge with our teeth rattlin’ so we can’t put up a talk. If I had a decent shot for the morning I could talk him out of it.
“And that rat, Finnerty, the trusty,” the talker spoke to me now, “has got a ton of it out there to sell, but he wouldn’t give us a jolt if we had the horrors.”
“Can you buy it from Finnerty?” I asked him, interested.
“Can you? Why, that guy Finnerty would peddle you a six-shooter and a road map if you had the coin, and then snitch on you to the desk sergeant, the rat,” he finished.
At that time morphine and opium were almost as cheap as tobacco. Fifty cents’ worth would last them a day. I hastily dug up a half dollar and gave it to him. In answer to his call Finnerty appeared, took the half dollar, and from his shirt pocket drew a bundle of neatly folded packets of morphine. In plain sight of the desk sergeant he counted out the required number of parcels and put them into the purchaser’s hand extended through the bars. They were divided at once.
Georgie said to his partner.

===Chinese===

他踱到栏杆边喊：“喂，芬纳蒂！”
不一刻，大杂役来了——一个瘦小干瘪、老鼠眼的小个子。
“纸烟，火柴？有。还要什么？”
我摸出一块钱。
“来一罐咖啡，再来点‘蜗牛’怎么样？”皮下针说。
“能弄什么弄什么，”我说着把钱给他。
芬纳蒂去了，快得出奇地回来：纸烟，一加仑果酱罐，半罐喷香的咖啡，一袋蜗牛饼。两个年轻人接管了咖啡和饼，往地上铺一张报纸，极其热络地请我随意取用。
我们吃宵夜的当口，话题绕着弯儿引到了毒品上：我这两位朋友缺货的险情，断顿之后瘾来时将受的罪，明早见法官前不得不来一针提神撑住。他们把自己的绝境说得那样愁苦，吃完便决定把剩下的那一小点白货也打了。这一针打完，两人亮堂起来，世道也跟着红润了。
“想想吧，乔吉，”话多的那个望着我，“半个五毛能给我们办多大的事！多大的救命钱！明早牙磕得说不出话，一人一个‘六个月’是稳的。早上有像样的一针，我能把法官说转了。
“还有那条耗子，芬纳蒂，那杂役，”话多的转向我，“外头存着一吨货等着卖，可咱们就是犯了瘾疯死，他一针也不白给。”
“能从芬纳蒂手里买？”我来了兴致。
“能不能！嘿，那家伙芬纳蒂，你有钱，他能卖你一把六响枪外带一张路线图，转头再上案头警佐那儿告你的密，耗子。”他收尾道。
那时吗啡、鸦片贱得几乎同烟叶一样。五毛钱的够他们过一天。我赶紧挖出半个五毛给他。应他一唤，芬纳蒂来了，接了钱，从衬衫袋里抽出一小捆叠得整整齐齐的小包。就在案头警佐的眼皮底下，他数出应得的几包，塞进从栏杆缝里伸出来的买主手里。当场分了。
乔吉对他的伙计说。

===Original===

“I think we’d better cook up a shot just to see if the stuff is all right. That Finnerty would peddle you chloride of lime if he happened to run out of morph.” This seemed to be a very rational reason for taking another shot, and they did.
Given a sufficient quantity of hop, no fiend is ever at a loss for a sound reason for taking a jolt of it. If he is feeling bad he takes a jolt so he will feel good. If he is feeling good, he takes one to make him feel better, and if he is feeling neither very bad nor very good he takes a jolt “just to get himself straightened around.”
Along about two in the morning a young chap about twenty was brought in to the desk. While he was being searched the two hypos had their eyes glued on him. “He ain’t got a dime,” said Georgie to his partner when the searching was done. The desk man gave the young fellow his cigarettes and he was locked in with us. He was neat, well dressed, and very wise looking. He sniffed at the hypos, gave me half a glance, adjusted his tie, and polished his shoes by rubbing each foot on the back of the opposite leg. He hung a cigarette on his lower lip and felt about for matches, but found none.
“Hey, you,” snapping his fingers at Georgie, “gimme a match.” Georgie gave him a few. “I’ll be out of here in an hour,” said the newcomer, inhaling his smoke. “I’ll send you in anything you want. I’m a quick connector. I can get a ten-dollar piece before I get out of the block﻿—sucker born every minute, you know.”
“Yes, I know,” Georgie replied. “I’m sorry for them poor suckers. They’re all asleep down in the Palace Hotel and you’re up here in the can begging matches. There’s one born every minute, all right, but there’s two wise guys going to jail every minute, an’ beggin’ matches.”
The wise guy said no more, but stood by the door waiting to go out. He was standing there when I left in the morning.
Georgie turned to his companion. “That last shot didn’t hit me right; we’d better cook up another an’ begin to get straightened up for court.”
Having bought the stuff for them, I took the liberty to sit by while they took their shot, which they did without seeming to notice me. Their bony arms were gray, like pieces of petrified wood. The skin was pocked with marks, mottled and scarred from the repeated, hourly stabbing of the needle. Their shirt sleeves were encrusted with dried blood from the many punctures.

===Chinese===

“我想咱们顶好再煮一针，验验这货地道不地道。那个芬纳蒂，‘货’一断顿，漂白粉他也敢卖你。”这理由听着头头是道，于是又打了一针。
只要有足量的“货”，瘾君子想打一针，从来愁不着正当理由。难受，打一针好受点；好受，打一针更好受；不好不难受呢，打一针“正一正心神”。
凌晨两点来钟，一个二十来岁的小伙子被押到案前。搜他的时候，两个皮下针的眼睛钉在他身上。“他一个子儿也没有。”搜完，乔吉对伙计说。案头的把纸烟还了小伙子，他同我们关在一处。他整洁，穿得好，一脸精明。他朝两个皮下针嗤了嗤鼻子，扫了我半眼，正了正领带，两只脚交替往另一条腿的腿肚子上蹭，把皮鞋擦亮。他往下唇挂了一支烟，摸火柴，没摸着。
“喂，你，”朝乔吉弹着响指，“给我根火柴。”乔吉给了他几根。“我一个钟头就出去了，”新来的吸着烟说。“你们要什么我送什么进来。我是个快手。不出这个街区我就能弄着一块钱的——傻瓜每分钟生一个，你懂的。”
“是，懂，”乔吉答。“我可怜那些傻瓜。他们全在宫殿饭店里睡着，你倒在这罐头里讨火柴。每分钟生一个，没错；每分钟还关进来两个精明人呢，还讨火柴。”
那位精明人不再言语，立在门边等着出去。我早晨走的时候，他还立在那里。
乔吉转向同伴。“刚才那针不对劲；顶好再煮一针，开始把精神理顺了上堂。”
货是我出钱买的，我便不客气，坐在旁边看他们打针；他们打了，像没觉出我在场。他们那两条瘦胳膊是灰的，像两截化石木头。皮肤上密密麻麻，针头一个钟头一个钟头扎出来的斑痕、花斑和疤。衬衫袖子上结着一层干血，那是无数针眼渗的。

===Original===

And yet they appeared oblivious to it all.
“Have a little shot, young fellow?” Georgie asked cordially.
I declined. “What would happen to me if I did?” I asked.
“Why, nothing; you’d lie down on the bench and sleep like a baby till time to go out in the morning, that’s all.”
“Yes! And what would happen to the balance of my silver while I am sleeping like a baby?”
Georgie’s partner cut in like a flash: “This is what would happen. Me and Georgie would stick right here by you and see that nobody frisked you for it.”
I laughed so loud that the desk officer thinking someone had gone hysterical, stood up sleepily and peered over his desk into the cell. The other bums stirred uneasily in their sleep. Mine was the only laugh there that night. I could laugh then; I didn’t know anything about hop.
My companions didn’t seem hurt or offended because of my intimation that they had designs on my last four-bit piece. They fell to discussing their case and preparing a talk for the judge in the morning. Georgie was for pleading guilty, and his chum wanted to “talk the judge out of it.” They couldn’t agree, and looked at me. I ventured to ask what they had done.
“We’ve got two tough raps,” said Georgie. “In the first place a hypo ain’t supposed to be found within a block of police headquarters, an’ we’re grabbed right alongside of the building. In the second place, a hypo ain’t allowed to leave Chinatown. Of course the cops know we sneak out. That ain’t so bad. Our racket is peddling kindling wood to the Chinks an’ we’ve got to go out of Chinatown to get it. Last night we ducks out and down Jackson Street to the commission houses and gather up a couple of fine bindles of wood. They are pretty heavy and we’re on short rations of gow and don’t feel any too strong, so we decide to dash up Washington Street, the shortest route into Chinatown. We’re bang up against the city prison when a big, flat-footed, harness bull steps out an’ yaffles us﻿—an’ here we are.”
Their case looked so tough that I could think of no solution. “I wouldn’t plead guilty to anything if I were you,” I advised him.
“Me, neither,” said his partner. “If I get six months they’ll have to hang them on me. I ain’t going to reach out an’ grab them.”
They fell into a fresh argument and their words became so personal and threatening that I feared they would do each other some great violence.

===Chinese===

而这一切，他们浑如不觉。
“来一小针么，小伙子？”乔吉热络地问。
我谢了。“我要是打了，会怎么样？”我问。
“喍，没事；你往凳上一躺，睡得像娃娃，一觉睡到明早出去，就这样。”
“是么！那我这下半晌的银钱，趁我睡得像娃娃的时候，会怎么样？”
乔吉的伙计抢着说：“会这样：我跟乔吉就守在这儿，看着，保准没人搜你。”
我笑出了声，笑得那么响，案头的警官只当谁发了癔症，睡眼惺忪地站起来，隔桌往囚室里张望。别的流浪汉睡梦里不安地动了动。那晚整间屋里，笑声只我一个人的。那时我笑得出；我对“货”一无所知。
同伴们对我那暗示——暗示他们惦记我最后那半个五毛——似乎并不见怪。他们转入正题，商量自己的案子，预备明早对法官的说辞。乔吉主张认罪，他的伙计要“把法官说转”。两人说不到一处，看着我。我冒昧问他们干了什么。
“我们是两条死罪在身，”乔吉说。“头一条，警察总局隔一个街区就不许见着皮下针，我们偏偏贴着大楼根儿叫拿了。第二条，皮下针不许出唐人街。条子们当然知道我们偷着出来。这还不算死。我们的营生是给中国佬零卖引火柴，引火柴得出了唐人街才弄得到。昨儿夜里我们溜出去，顺杰克逊街到批发行，收了两捆好柴。柴沉，我们的‘货’又紧，浑身没劲，就抄近道奔华盛顿街回唐人街。正撞上市监狱，一个大脚板子巡警踱出来，把我们一把揪住——就到这儿了。”
他们的案子看着这样死，我想不出活路。“换了我是你们，什么都不认。”我劝他。
“我也不认，”他伙计说。“他们判我六个月，也得他们亲手给我套上。我是不伸手去接的。”
两人又吵起来，话越来越见私人、越来越带威胁，我怕他们要大打出手。

===Original===

I took a chance in the role of peacemaker and suggested that they take another shot and talk it over peaceably and quietly. They quit their wrangling instantly and in a minute they were on their knees in the corner of the cell with their heads together, amicably preparing another shot. Somewhere down the corridor I heard a clatter, and a singsong voice droned, “Get ready for the broom. Get ready for the broom.”
I was going out. I went over to my friends in the corner with the fifty-cent piece I had left. “Here,” I said, “take this.” One of them, still on his knees waiting for his shot, held out his hand; his fingers closed on the half dollar. He neither looked up nor spoke to me﻿—his eyes were on the little tin can where the morphine was dissolving in the boiling water.
The door was opened and my name called.
I stood by the door till a trusty prisoner opened it and let me out. My partner from the wine dump was also taken out and we joined four or five others in the corridor. A trusty came with an armful of brooms and gave each of us one. A husky young fellow, half awake, reached for one of the newest brooms. “No, you don’t,” said the trusty, “the last time you were here I gave you a good new broom and you beat it up to Chinktown and peddled it for a dime. An old broom for you.”
We were detailed in pairs to sweep the sidewalks clean all around the block the prison stood on. My space was from the prison door down to Montgomery Street. An old man had the sidewalk across the street from me. Two or three assistant trusties nosed around like bird dogs to see that we swept clean and didn’t run away with the brooms. When my task was done I helped the old man finish his, and he carried my broom back to the prison. The trusty dismissed me with a wave of his arm and I went up Montgomery Street in search of a restaurant where I could get some coffee with a dime I had saved from the rapacious and cunning hypos in the prison. I decided to keep away from the wine dumps in future, and out of the hands of the police vag detail that rounded up the riffraff when they got too numerous and pesty.
In a few days Sanc was back, quite satisfied with his trip to Salt Chunk Mary’s. The money was split and put in our box in separate parcels. We had more than $1,000 each now, but he had no notion of taking a rest or vacation. He wanted to know right away what I had done in his absence.

===Chinese===

我趁势充了一回和事佬，劝他们再打一针，心平气和地慢慢商量。两人立时不吵了，转眼已并排跪在囚室角落里，头凑头，和和气气预备另一针。走廊那头传来哐啷声，一个拖着调子的嗓子哼着：“扫帚伺候喽。扫帚伺候喽。”
我要出去了。我带着剩下的那半个五毛，踱到角落里我那两位朋友跟前。“拿着，”我说，“这个拿去。”其中一个还跪着候针，伸出手来；手指扣住那半个五毛。他头也不抬，话也不说——眼睛钉在那只小铁罐上，吗啡正在滚水里化开。
门开了，叫到我的名字。
我立在门边，等一个服杂役的犯人来开门放我。酒窖来的我那位同伴也被提出来，同走廊里另外四五个会合。一个杂役抱来一摞扫帚，一人一把。一个半睡半醒的壮小伙子伸手去挑最新的一把。“不给，”杂役说，“上回你在这儿，我给了你把好新扫帚，你拿着跑到唐人街，一毛钱卖了。你的，旧扫帚。”
我们两人一组，分头把监狱所在那个街区四周的人行道扫净。我的一段，从监狱门口到蒙哥马利街。对面人行道归一个老头。两三个副杂役像猎狗似的转来转去，看我们扫得净、别拎着扫帚跑了。我扫完自己那段，帮老头收了尾，扫帚是他替我送回监狱的。杂役一挥手放了我，我顺蒙哥马利街上坡，寻一家饭馆，用我从监狱里那两个又贪又精的皮下针手里保下来的一毛钱，买杯咖啡喝。我打定主意：往后离酒窖远点，也离警察流浪搜查队的手远点——那路人渣一多一烦，他们就清一回。
没几天桑克回来了，这趟盐块玛丽之行他相当满意。钱分了，各自打包存进我们的保管箱。如今我们每人名下都过了一千块，可他毫无歇息或休假的意思。他急着要知道，他不在时我干了些什么。

===Original===

I reported everything, including my night in jail. He asked me what I told the police at the station. I told him. “Not bad,” he said, “but be careful.”
I gave him the hotel room keys I had made, and he complimented me on my work and put them away for future use.

===Chinese===

我原原本本报了账，连同坐牢那一夜。他问我在局子里对警察说了什么。我学了。“不赖，”他说，“不过要小心。”
我把他托付的那些旅馆房间钥匙交还他；他夸了我两句手艺，把钥匙收好，备日后之用。

## XIII / 十三
===Original===

Having plenty of time and spending money I visited San Jose, towns down the peninsula, and across the bay﻿—always with an eye to business. Jewelry-store windows are fascinating to the thief. He must stop and look over the sparkling plunder. Even in these days of my regeneration I stop occasionally from habit at a jewelry-store window. Usually there are others looking, too!
I paused before a jewelry-store window in Oakland one evening, just at closing time. The clerks were clearing it out for the night. In the center of the display stood a slim, polished wood pedestal, on top of which, in a velvet cup, reposed an enormous ruby, the size of my thumbnail, set in a ring. A placard announced that this pigeon-blood ruby, valued at three thousand dollars, was to be awarded by some organization, the name of which I don’t recall, to the winner of a contest they were holding.
The window was cleaned up, the display put away for the night, but the ruby was left on its pedestal when the store was closed. I got the next boat across and found Sanc. We went back to Oakland and got to the store about eleven o’clock.
“I don’t know anything about rubies, kid, but if it’s left there all night it’s phony.”
Just then two men came along, entered the store, and, locking the door behind them, went to the window and got the stone. They disappeared toward the back of the store.
“It must be genuine, kid. They are putting it away in the box.”
As we walked away, Sanc said: “The window has a burglar alarm. You can see the tape around the edges. If I could devise some way to put an inch hole through the glass without cracking it clear across to the tape, the alarm would not sound and I could take the stone out with a stiff wire. Tomorrow night I’ll try something I have long thought of.”
The next day he bought at a secondhand store a small machinist’s hammer, weighing about a pound. One face of it was thick, heavy, and flat, the other was rounded to a nose about the size of a boy’s marble. He bought a whipstock of tough, springy wood and made a handle about eighteen inches long, which he fitted into the hammer.
For several nights we went around to new buildings and into deserted streets where there were plate-glass windows of a weight corresponding with the one in Oakland.

===Chinese===

时间宽裕，闲钱也宽裕，我把圣何塞、半岛沿线各镇、湾对岸都逛了个遍——眼睛始终不离本行。珠宝店的橱窗对贼有磁力。他必得停下，把那一片闪光的浮财品鉴一遍。即便如今我已改过自新，路过珠宝店橱窗，也还会习惯性地偶尔驻足。而且驻足的往往不止我一个！
一天傍晚，正赶打烊，我在奥克兰一家珠宝店橱窗前停了脚。店员们正收拾过夜。陈列中央立着一座细巧的抛光木座，座顶一只丝绒托，托着一枚硕大的红宝石，指甲盖大小，镶在戒指上。告示写着：这枚鸽血红宝石，作价三千元，将由某团体——名字我记不得了——赠予他们正在举办的某项比赛的优胜者。
橱窗收拾了，陈列过夜收起了，可宝石还留在座上，店就打了烊。我搭下一班船过湾，找到桑克。我们折回奥克兰，十一点上下到了店门口。
“红宝石我不内行，孩子；可若真能整夜撂在那儿，它就是假的。”
话音未落来了两个人，进店，反锁了门，走到橱窗前取了石头，往店铺后头去了。
“那就必是真货，孩子。他们这是收进保险箱去。”
往回走时，桑克说：“橱窗有防盗警铃，边上的胶带看得见。若能想个法子，在玻璃上开一个一英寸的孔，又不叫裂纹一直延到胶带，铃就不会响，我拿根硬铁丝就能把石头钩出来。明晚我试一样早就想试的东西。”
第二天，他在旧货店买了一柄小机工锤，约一磅重。一头厚、重、平；另一头收成一个鼻子似的圆尖，男孩的弹子大小。他又买了一根坚韧有弹性的木鞭杆，安了一个约十八英寸长的柄，装进锤头。
接连几夜，我们专跑新楼和僻街——凡有同奥克兰那块分量相当的厚板玻璃橱窗的地方。

===Original===

Sanc used his hammer like a whip, snapping the noselike face of it against windows till he became so expert he could make a hole as clean as if it had been done by a bullet, and without cracking the glass for more than six inches from it.
“Vandalism, they will call it,” he said after destroying probably five hundred dollars’ worth of glass, “but in reality it is a scientific experiment and a success.” The impact of the hammer sounded very much like the breaking of a piece of kindling wood under foot. At last we were ready, and Sanc, as usual, gave me final instructions.
“Oakland is a tough town to take a pinch in, kid. You can’t fix your case, and you’ll get the limit if convicted. I’ll take a gun along. You needn’t. There isn’t much you can do. When the bull is out of the block and pedestrians are a couple of doors away I will do the best I can. You stand nearby and if anybody gets wise to me, you shout to him: ‘Look out, he’s got a gun. Let’s get a policeman!’ ”
At nine o’clock we passed the window. A crowd was admiring the ring. At ten the pedestrians had thinned out, and half an hour later Sanc’s chance came.
The store was in the middle of a block. The patrolman was at the corner going farther away. Everything was exactly right. A passing electric car swallowed the noise of the breaking glass. Sanc disengaged the hammer’s nose from the hole carefully with a rasping noise. The hammer went into his coat pocket, and the wire came from beneath his coat. The top of the pedestal where the ruby rested was higher than the hole in the window, and when Sanc lifted the ring on his wire it slid slowly down and out into his hand. He threw the wire into the street and walked away. I followed behind and overtook him at the corner. A few blocks farther away he tossed the hammer into a lot. We sat apart on the train to the ferry and on the boat across to the city.
In my room Sanc removed the ruby from its setting, which I immediately took out and threw into a nearby sewer opening. When I returned he was admiring the stone. “There’s a stone, kid, that, as Shakespeare says, ‘A Jew would kiss and an Infidel adore.’ ”
The Sanctimonious Kid’s face wore a serious look the next evening when we met to go to dinner. “Kid,” he said, “the more I think of this wonderful pigeon-blood ruby the less I think of it, if you understand what I mean.

===Chinese===

桑克使锤如使鞭，用那个鼻尖弹击橱窗，直练到出神入化：开的孔干净得像子弹打的，孔周六英寸以外玻璃纹丝不裂。
“他们会管这叫破坏公物，”他毁了约莫五百块的玻璃之后说，“其实这是科学实验，而且成功了。”锤子击玻璃的声响，很像脚底下踩断一根劈柴。终于备妥了，桑克照例给我上最后一课。
“奥克兰这地方折了不好翻身，孩子。案子没法疏通，定了罪就是顶格。我带枪去，你不用。你也帮不上多少忙。等巡捕出了街区、行人隔开两三个门面，我就下手。你守在近处；谁看破了我，你就朝他喊：‘当心，他有枪！快去找警察！’”
九点，我们路过橱窗，一群人正赏那枚戒指。十点，行人稀了；又过半小时，桑克的机会到了。
店在街区中段。巡捕在街角，正越走越远。万事恰好。一辆过路电车吞掉了玻璃破裂声。桑克把锤鼻从孔里小心退出，磨出一声锉响。锤进了他的外套袋，铁丝从外套底下探出来。宝石托座顶高于窗上的孔；桑克用铁丝挑起戒指，它便顺着缓缓滑下，滑出孔，落进他掌心。铁丝丢在街心，他走开。我跟在后面，到街角追上他。又隔几个街区，锤子丢进一块空地。去渡口的火车上、过湾的船上，我们分开坐。
在我房里，桑克把宝石从托座上取下，托座我立刻拿出去，丢进近处一个下水道口。回来时他正赏那石头。“这颗石头，孩子，正如莎士比亚所说，‘犹太人要吻它，异教徒要拜它。’”
第二天傍晚碰头去吃饭时，假圣人小子一脸凝重。“孩子，”他说，“这颗奇妙的鸽血红，我想得越多，越不敢往高里想——你懂我的意思。

===Original===

It is possible that we put too much faith in the integrity of the business firm that displayed it so carelessly. I was out to the park today and gave the thing a careful inspection in the sunlight, and there is a cold, clammy suspicion in my mind that we have been swindled﻿—in short, I am afraid the thing is a rank phony.
“And the worst of it is there’s not one person in five thousand who knows a real ruby when he sees it. There are probably three men in San Francisco who would know, but they are all square businessmen and we can’t submit it to any of them. The next nearest man is the buyer for the big Mormon store, Zion’s Cooperative Mercantile Institution, at Salt Lake City. I will take a run back there with it and get someone to have him give an opinion on it. If it’s genuine I can get a thousand dollars on it. I’ll be gone about two weeks, and this is what I want you to do while I am away.
“Put on your best clothes, keep clean and neat, and spend your evenings around the Diamond Palace in Montgomery Street. Go through the whole block on both sides of the street. Look into every hallway, basement, empty store, and saloon. Try to locate some place that we can go through and out into an alley or another street. The getaway’s the thing, and if you can find one in the block we’ll chance the Diamond Palace some evening, maybe Christmas Eve.”
He left next morning after giving me strict orders to keep away from the wine dumps and attend to business. I worked faithfully and soon found half a dozen places that would serve. My time was now my own, and once more I began exploring the byways of the city.
The hypos I spent the night with in the city prison had aroused my curiosity about Chinatown. I put in many nights prowling through the alleys watching these mysterious people gambling, smoking opium, and trafficking in their women slaves. There were rumors of strange, mysterious underground passages below the streets and under the buildings, but I never saw them and I have since come to doubt whether they ever existed.
The spots most favored in Chinatown by the hypos and small beggars were the “cook ovens,” places built in back of Chinese lodging houses where the occupants did their cooking on the community plan. There were warm places around the ovens to sleep in, and always a bite to eat for the asking﻿—no Chinaman refuses to feed a hungry man.

===Chinese===

咱们兴许把太多信心，押给了那家把它撞得如此大意的商号的诚信。我今天去公园，在太阳底下细细验了它，心里有一条又冷又黏的疑影：咱们叫人坑了——说白了，我怕它是个彻头彻尾的假货。
“最糟的是：五千个人里没有一个认得真红宝石。旧金山会认的，撑死三个人，还个个是正经商人，一个也不能递到他们眼前。再近的一个，是盐湖城摩门大店‘锡安合作商行’的采购。我带着它跑一趟，托人请他掌掌眼。真的话，我能押出一千块。我出去约两周；我不在，你做这几件事。
“穿上你最好的衣裳，保持整洁，晚上泡在蒙哥马利街的钻石宫一带。把这条街两面的整个街区走透。每条过道、地下室、空铺面、酒馆都看。要物色一处能钻进去、通到巷子或另一条街的地方。脱身是命门；你在街区里寻着一处，咱们就赶哪个晚上——兴许圣诞夜——碰碰钻石宫。”
他次日一早走了，走前严令我：酒窟别沾，正事要紧。我干得踏实，很快寻出六七处顶用的。时间归我自己了，我又开始探这座城的旮旯。
在市监狱同牢一夜的那些瘾君子，早勾起了我对唐人街的好奇。我许多个夜里穿行那些小巷，看这神秘的人群赌钱、抽大烟、买卖他们的女奴。传说街底楼底有离奇的地道，我从没见过，如今想来，那些地道多半从来就没存在过。
唐人街上瘾君子和小叫花最青睐的去处是“灶窝”——华人寄宿屋后头搭的棚，住客们合伙开伙。灶边有暖和的睡处，讨口吃的也总有——中国人从不拒绝给饿汉一口饭。

===Original===

In my experience with the Chinese I have found them charitable, frugal, thrifty, moral, and honest.
In speaking of the cook ovens I may say that it was there the word “yegg” originated. It has not yet been locked in the dictionary, but it has a place in our language and it’s about time its derivation was settled once and for all. It is a corruption of “yekk,” a word from one of the many dialects spoken in Chinatown, and it means beggar. When a hypo or beggar approached a Chinaman to ask for something to eat, he was greeted with the exclamation, “yekk man, yekk man.”
The underworld is quick to seize upon strange words, and the bums and hypos in Chinatown were calling themselves yeggmen years before the term was taken out on the road and given currency by eastbound beggars. In no time it had a verb hung on it, and to yegg meant to beg.
The late William A. Pinkerton was responsible for its changed meaning. His business consisted largely of asking questions and necessarily he acquired much misinformation. A burglar with some humor fell into Pinkerton’s hands and when asked who was breaking open the country jugs he whispered to the detective that it was the yeggs. Investigation convinced Pinkerton that there were a lot of men drifting about the country who called themselves yeggs. The word went into a series of magazine articles Pinkerton was writing at the time and was fastened upon the box men. Its meaning has since widened until now the term “yegg” includes all criminals whose work is “heavy.”
Sanc returned in a very hostile frame of mind. “We’ve been bilked, kid. It was a French imitation. Been on the market ten years. I sold it to Tom Dennison, who runs the Wasatch gambling house in the Lake, for a hundred and fifty dollars, just enough to pay expenses. If you locate a few more capers like that you’ll put us in the poorhouse.”
I reported my findings in Montgomery Street, and Sanc after looking them over carefully pronounced two of the getaways feasible.
“Kid,” he said, “I read in the papers some time ago that a man named Charlie Rice, in New York City, put on a cheap, black alpaca coat, put his cap in his pocket, and with a pencil on his ear walked into a bank and behind the counter where there were twenty clerks at work. Unnoticed, he picked up twenty-five thousand dollars in bank notes and walked out.

===Chinese===

同中国人打交道的经验里，我认定他们仁善、俭省、勤勉、有德、诚实。
说到灶窝，不妨一说：“耶格”（yegg）这个词正是打那儿来的。词典还没收它，可它在我们语言里已有了位置，来历也该一锤定音了。它是“yekk”的讹变——唐人街诸多方言里的一个词，意思是“叫花子”。瘾君子或叫花凑近一个中国人讨吃的，迎面就是一声：“yekk man，yekk man（叫花子来了）。”
黑道最会抓生词。还没等东去的叫花们把它带上路传开，唐人街的流民和瘾君子们早自称起“耶格曼”来。转眼它又挂上了动词：to yegg，即行乞。
把它的意思改掉的，是已故的威廉·A·平克顿。他那一行大半是问话，问多了，自然也积了一堆错讹。一个带几分幽默的夜贼落到平克顿手里，被问到乡下是谁在撞钱柜，他悄悄告诉侦探：是耶格们。一查，平克顿信了：满国漂着一伙自称“耶格”的人。这个词进了他当时正在写的一组杂志文章，从此钉在了撞箱人的身上。词义越滚越宽，如今“耶格”已囊括一切干“重活”的罪犯。
桑克回来时一肚子敌意。“咱们叫人宰了，孩子。法国仿货，市面上十年了。我一百五十块卖给了汤姆·丹尼森——湖城‘瓦萨契’赌场的老板——刚够盘缠。你再物色几票这样的，咱们就该进贫民院了。”
我汇报了蒙哥马利街的发现，桑克仔细相过，认定其中两处脱身道可行。
“孩子，”他说，“前阵子我在报上读到：纽约市一个叫查利·赖斯的人，穿一件廉价黑羽纱外套，帽子撞兜里，耳朵上夹支铅笔，走进一家银行，绕到柜台后头——二十个职员在干活。没人留意他，他拈起两万五千元钞票，走了出去。

===Original===

“That suggested to me that I can walk into this jewelry store some evening before Christmas when they will have an extra force of clerks employed, and go behind the counter if I am dressed like a clerk. If I can get behind a counter you can surely get in front of it. I will put a tray of stones out for your inspection, and you will walk out with it. We will go over it carefully now.
“I will walk into the place about seven o’clock some evening, dressed in a black suit, white shirt, high collar, and black tie. You will go in directly behind me. You will carry a harmless-looking parcel that looks like a box of handkerchiefs neatly wrapped and tied. There will be no bottom in it. I will walk to the back of the store where there are two or three settees for patrons and visitors. You will stroll about nearby. I will leave my derby hat and overcoat on a settee and try, mind you, try to get behind the main counter. I am depending upon the fact that the place will be full of visitors and customers and that there will be a number of strange clerks and that no one clerk will know all the others.
“You will follow my movements closely. When I get behind the counter I will take out the best tray I can get and set it on the show case in front of you. You will put your bottomless package over the tray, pick it up, put it under your arm, and walk out. We have two weeks to get ready. We will go down there separately and look the place over for a week. When you go in, you rehearse the thing in your mind and don’t think of anything else for the next ten days.”
We thought and talked of the Diamond Palace only for days and days. “Kid,” said Sanc, “I heard Rebel George who invented the goldbrick swindle say: ‘The way to sell a brass brick is to bunko yourself first into the belief that your brick is solid gold﻿—the rest is easy. The most successful bunko man is the one who bunkoes himself before he goes after a sucker.’
“I am going to hypnotize myself into the belief that I am a clerk in that store from the minute I take my hat off till I put it on. You are a visitor from the minute you go in till you get your hands on the stones; then, presto, you are what you are. If we can get one of the larger trays of stones we can get enough money on them to go to Salt Lake or Denver, and open a small gambling house where, while we can’t be entirely respectable, we can at least be secure.

===Chinese===

“这提示了我：赶哪个圣诞节前的傍晚，店里雇着加班的额外店员，我穿得像个店员走进这家珠宝店，绕到柜台后头。我进得了柜台后，你站到柜台前总不成问题。我托出一盘石头请你过目，你端着盘子走出去。现在咱们把这桩仔细过一遍。
“某个傍晚七点来钟，我走进店：黑西装、白衬衫、高领、黑领带。你紧随我进店，臂弯里掉一个无害模样的纸包，包得齐整、扎得利落，像一盒手帕。它没有底。我走到店铺后头，那里有两三张待客的长椅。你在近处闲逛。我把圆帽和外套撞在长椅上，试着——记住，是试着——绕到主柜台后头。我押的宝是：店里挤满访客买主，生面孔店员一大把，没有一个店员认得全部同僚。
“你紧盯我的动作。我一进柜台后，就抽出够得着的最好一盘，摆在你面前的展示柜上。你把没底的纸包罩上盘子，连盘带包端起，夹在腋下，走出去。备工两周。咱们分头下去，把那店相看一个礼拜。进店之后，你满脑子排演这一幕，十天里别的什么都别想。”
我们日日只想着、说着钻石宫。“孩子，”桑克说，“我听发明金砖骗局的‘叛贼乔治’说过：‘卖黄铜砖的诀窍，是先把自己骗得信这砖是足金——其余都容易。最成功的骗子，是下手前先把自己骗倒的骗子。’
“我要把自己催眠成那家店的店员——从摘帽那一刻起，到戴帽那一刻止。你是访客，从进门那一刻起，到手搭上石头那一刻止；然后，说变就变，你还是你。咱们若捞得着一大盘石头，销出的钱够去盐湖城或丹佛，开一家小赌场——虽算不上体面，至少安稳。

===Original===

I am at a stage where I would like to quit. I don’t feel like going to work as a laborer at two dollars a day, or as a clerk for fifteen a week. I’m not speaking disparagingly of them, mind you﻿—fact is, they have more real courage than we have, working for such wages. But this life of ours breeds expensive habits of loose, careless spending that are hard to overcome, and even if I could swallow my objections to being exploited I would still find it impossible to survive on the pay.
“I didn’t jump into this life I’m leading as I jump into bed, and I can’t get out of it in one jump. I drifted in by slow degrees, and if I get out I’ll have to ease out of it by slow stages. A gambling joint would be the first step away. Then maybe something better after that. This can all be threshed out later; we must not let our minds stray from the Diamond Palace.”
Sanc’s philosophy stood up under our bad luck with the near-ruby. “Kid,” he said later, “this is the season of peace on earth and good will to men. Who gives to the poor lends to the Lord, but when I give anything to the poor I am going to have a better motive. However, we are not givers; we are takers, and our taking should be reasoned out rationally. We will reverse this ‘giving and lending.’ We will rob the rich and discomfit the devil; thereby, perhaps, finding favor in the sight of the Lord. And this brings us to the front door of the Diamond Palace. It’s one of the city’s show places and visitors are always welcome. We will visit it.”
And we did. Separately, we made several visits in the evening to familiarize ourselves with the inside, and to locate the trays of stones. At night in my room Sanc drew diagrams of the interior and the show cases. We rehearsed the thing in detail night after night. He made a neat dummy parcel and drilled me in placing it over an imitation tray of diamonds; showed me how to place it under my arm securely yet carelessly and walk out of the store with entire nonchalance.
He bought his black suit, white shirt, and black tie, put them on and rehearsed his part. We put our rooms in order, cleaning out everything that might look suspicious, protecting ourselves against a possible “pinch.” The hotel keys I had made were put away carefully against a rainy day﻿—or night. Our money was taken from the safety box, changed into bank notes, and secreted in our clothes.

===Chinese===

我到了一个想收山的坎上。我不情愿去一天两块钱当苦力，也不情愿一周十五块当伙计。别误会，我没有小看他们的意思——说实话，凭那点工钱干活，他们比咱们有真勇气。可咱们这种日子，养出一身大手大脚的奢习，戒也戒不掉；就算我咽得下受剥削这口气，那点薪水也活不下去。
“我如今这条路，不是像上床那样一步跳进来的，也就不能一步跳出去。我是一寸一寸漂进来的；要出去，也得一站一站地缓出去。开赌场是离岸的头一步，往后兴许还有更好的。这些以后再细盘；眼下，心不许离开钻石宫。”
咱们那颗假红宝石的霉运，没能压垮桑克的哲学。“孩子，”他后来说，“眼下是地上平安、世人蒙恩的季节。施舍穷人便是借给耶和华；可我施舍穷人时，动机还要更高尚些。不过咱们不是施主，是取主；取，也该取得有理性。咱们把这‘施与借’倒个个儿：劫富，折魔的锐气——如此，兴许反倒在耶和华眼前蒙恩。这就把咱们引到了钻石宫的正门。它是本城的名胜之一，访客向来欢迎。咱们就去拜访。”
我们去了。分头去了好几趟，都是傍晚，熟悉店内、摸清宝石盘的位置。夜里在我房中，桑克画出店内和展示柜的图。一夜一夜，我们把这一幕排到细处。他做了一个漂亮的假纸包，操练我把它罩上一盘假钻石；教我怎样把它夹在腋下——夹得牢，又夹得漫不经心——怎样满不在乎地走出店门。
他置办了黑西装、白衬衫、黑领带，穿戴起来排练他的角色。我们把住处收拾停当，一切招眼之物清了场，防一手可能的“提人”。我配的那些旅馆钥匙，仔细收好，备着荒年——或荒夜。钱从保管箱取出，换成钞票，密缝进衣裳。

===Original===

I asked him about the guns. “No gun for you, Kid. You might get nervous. Leave yours somewhere. I’ll take mine along.”
Christmas was ten days away. Every evening we dressed for our parts, got barbered, shined, and slicked up, and walked about the block prepared to go into the store when it looked right. We made sure every day that the getaways I had located were still open.
“I could make it now,” said Sanc as we stood across the street the third evening, “but I’ll wait; it’s getting better every day.”
His last instructions were: “Now, don’t get nervous. If you get your hands on the junk walk out quietly and away. If they tumble me up before I get the tray out you fade away. I’ll do the best I can alone. If there’s a tumble after you get the tray and you are chased, hang on to it and get into one of the spots you have picked out and when you get on the other street don’t run; walk briskly direct to the room. If I’m not there five minutes after you, I’ll be in jail. I don’t like to think of that, but the pitcher can go to the well once too often, and it’s better to know beforehand what you are going to do when it breaks. If I don’t appear you put the stones away safely and wait till I send you word. Don’t try to connect with me.”
The next evening as I stood looking at the window display Sanc came by, glanced inside sharply, and snapped his fingers, which was the “office” to me that he was going in. I went in about three steps behind him. All I saw was a fine-looking, elderly man in a policeman’s uniform walking up the center of the store, active, alert. When I passed him my heart was pounding so with suspense that I was afraid he would hear it.
Sanc walked straight to the back of the big room and sat on a settee. I strolled about looking at, but not seeing, the wonderful display of baubles. The place was fairly filled with Christmas shoppers and sightseers. Beautifully gowned women and distinguished-looking men stood at the counters inspecting rare stones and costly ornaments. Visitors idled around and clerks stepped about briskly. Parties of shoppers were coming in, the place was filling up with people laughing and chatting.
There was a little pucker between Sanc’s eyes as he held his seat, and I knew something was wrong. Looking closer I saw, facing him and but a few feet away, an attaché of the place conversing with a party of women.

===Chinese===

我问枪怎么办。“你不带枪，孩子。你兴许紧张。你的那支找地方撞下。我带我的。”
离圣诞还有十天。每晚我们打扮齐戏中的行头，理发、擦鞋、上油，绕着那个街区遛，看准了时机就进店。我踩好的脱身道，我们天天核实还通着。
“这会儿就能干，”第三天傍晚我们站在街对面时桑克说，“可我再等等；一天比一天火候好。”
他的嘱咐最后是这样：“别紧张。手一搭上货，静悄悄走出去，走远。我还没托出盘就叫人撞破，你就淡出去，我一人尽力周旋。盘子到手之后被人发觉、有人追，那就攝紧它，钻进你踩好的哪一处；上了另一条街别跑，快步直奔住处。你到后五分钟我不在，我就是进去了。这我不愿多想；可瓦罐难免井上破，破之前先想好怎么办，总没错。我不露面，你把石头藏妥，等我捎信。别来寻我。”
第二天傍晚，我正立在橱窗前看陈列，桑克走过，朝店里锐利地一瞥，捻了个响指——这是给我的“暗号”：他要进去了。我落后他三步跟进。一进门只看见一位仪表堂堂的老者，一身警服，正从店堂中央走过，机警，干练。同他擦肩时，我的心悬得咚咚撞，生怕他听见。
桑克径直走向大厅深处，在长椅上坐下。我踱来踱去，看那一柜一柜的奇珍——看而不见。店里挤满圣诞采买的主顾和看热闹的。盛装的女人、气派的男人立在柜前细鉴稀世的宝石和贵重的饰件。访客闲逛，店员疾步。一伙一伙的买主进门，满店笑语。
桑克端坐不动，眉心却蹙起一个小结——我知道出岔了。凑近细看：正对他、不过几英尺处，立着店内的一名职员，正陪一伙女客应酬。

===Original===

Sanc was stuck and couldn’t transform himself into a clerk under the man’s eyes. At last he moved away. Sanc took off his hat, put it on the settee, stood up, and stepped smartly toward the front, a clerk now, and I right behind him with my dummy parcel.
Sanc’s unavoidable delay in transforming himself into a clerk was fatal to our plan. One glance along the show cases that contained the most valuable stones showed us that they were all out and under inspection by patrons who came in after we did. Time was precious, worth more than money. He dared not hesitate. Turning quickly, he crossed the room and stepped behind the long line of show cases, passed two busy clerks, and stopped at a case that held a number of trays of small stones.
“Now, here’s something, sir,” he said to me as he reached in and brought out a tray holding one dozen rings, the best in sight that he could get at. My parcel was on the show case and he did not wait for me to act; he placed it on top of the tray and turned away toward the rear of the store. Shaking with suspense I managed to pick it up and place it properly under my arm, holding it closely to my side.
On the sidewalk I wanted to run, expecting to hear a hue and cry from the store. In turning the first corner I looked back. Sanc wasn’t more than twenty feet behind me. I slowed up for him and we went straight to the room.
“Another bunch of rotten luck, kid,” he said, peeling off his black suit. “This town has me hoodooed. I’m gone from it tonight, and you had better come along. Wrap up that suit and throw it away along with the shirt and tie. I’ll unharness these rocks. We’re lucky if Mary gives us two thousand dollars on them.”
In an hour he had them out of their settings, which I threw away as before. He went to his room for his few belongings. I threw mine in a bag and we met at the ferry where we got a boat to Oakland, leaving nothing behind us but the hotel keys I had made with such pains and trouble.
The theft was not reported in the papers. There may be a record of it in the police archives, for all I know. “I can’t explain their silence,” said Sanc. “This much is certain, and it’s the worst part of this business of ours. Some innocent party is going to ride the blame. They will never figure that as an outside caper.”

===Chinese===

桑克叫钉住了，在那人眼皮底下变不成店员。末了那人走开。桑克摘下帽子撞在长椅上，起身，利落地朝前堂走去——此刻他已是店员——我抱着假纸包紧随其后。
桑克变不成店员的这段延误，送了我们整盘计划。沿展示柜一扫便知：最贵重的那几柜宝石盘全数端出，正供我们之后进门的买主们鉴赏。时间贵过金钱，他不敢犹豫。他疾转身横穿店堂，绕到长长一列展示柜后，越过两名忙碌的店员，停在一柜前——柜里若干盘小件。
“先生，这儿有您中意的，”他说着探手取出一盘——十二枚戒指，是他够得着的范围内眼前最好的。我的纸包已在展示柜上，他不等我动作，把它扣上托盘，转身朝店后去了。我抖着，抖着还是把包连同托盘端了起来，妥帖地夹紧在腋下，贴着身侧。
到了人行道上，我想跑，只等店里炸起呼喊。拐第一个弯时我回头一望：桑克在我身后不到二十英尺。我放慢等他，我们径直回了住处。
“又是一票霉运，孩子。”他一面剥下黑西装说。“这城克我。我今夜就走，你最好同行。把那身西装包上，连衬衫领带一起扔了。我来卸这些石头。玛丽肯给两千，就是咱们的造化。”
一个钟头后他卸完了托座，照旧由我拿去扔了。他回房取他寥寥的家当，我把我的塞进一只提包，我们在渡口会合，搭船去了奥克兰，身后只撇下我费尽心血配的那把把旅馆钥匙。
报上没登这桩失窃。也许警察档案里有一笔，谁知道呢。“他们不作声，我解不开，”桑克说，“有一点是定的，也是咱们这行当最糟的一点：会有个无辜的人替咱们背这口锅。他们做梦也想不出这是外路贼干的一票。”

===Original===

The next day we got an eastbound train and in due time found ourselves in the town of Pocatello, the stronghold of Salt Chunk Mary. Our knock at her door was answered by a young and pretty Swedish girl, blue-eyed and fair-haired. In answer to our call for Mary she laughed and her big, innocent blue eyes danced. “Aye tank you have bad luck. Miss Mary she bane on big yamboree.”
We went downtown and rented a room. In Pocatello, as in every other Western town in those days, it was the correct custom and usage for sporting people to go on a big jamboree once or twice a year. A jamboree was usually preceded by a run of good or bad luck. If the celebrant got hold of a bunch of easy money he or she “went on a tear” to celebrate the good luck. If the luck got too bad, the way to change it was to go out and get drunk. The length of these celebrations was determined by the size of the party’s bankroll or the strength of his constitution.
Salt Chunk Mary’s bankroll had no bottom, and her constitution was flawless. So it followed that her periods of relaxation were somewhat extended. Being a very positive-minded person, inclining to action rather than words, her procedure at these times differed greatly from the ordinary. When she “went on a toot” the town marshal went fishing or hunting, and her more timid business rivals closed their places and remained in a state of siege like storekeepers in Chinatown when a tong war was declared. It was her custom to visit her friends’ places first, where friendships were renewed and emphasized by much spending and drinking, and where obligations were acknowledged and discharged promptly. She poured liquor into the bums, beggars, ragtags, and bobtails that hung around the saloons till they were legless drunk, and unable to follow her triumphal march through the town. She never let up for the want of money, nor because of inability to “carry her licker.” Her jamboree closed when she had “made” the last place in town and that was always the joint of someone she held a grudge against.
Sanc and I were fortunate enough to witness the windup of one of her most memorable celebrations. Leaving her hack at the curb, she walked into her victim’s saloon and ordered all hands to drink. When the drinks were disposed of and paid for, she put both hands on the inner edge of the bar and pulled it over on the floor.

===Chinese===

次日我们上了东行的车，适时到了波卡特洛——盐块玛丽的根据地。敲门应声的，是个年轻标致的瑞典姑娘，蓝眼睛，金头发。听说找玛丽，她笑了，一双无辜的大蓝眼睛直转：“我看你们运气不好。玛丽小姐赶上大狂欢啦。”
我们进城租了间房。波卡特洛，同当年西部所有的镇子一样：道上人一年一两次大狂欢，是正经的风俗。狂欢多半由一段运气的起落引头。手气好、捞着一笔轻松钱，就“纵一场”贺好运；手气太坏，改运的法子也是出门大醉。狂欢的长短，看主家钱袋的深浅，或看体格的强弱。
盐块玛丽的钱袋没有底，体格也没有破绽。于是她的休整期也就格外地长。她是极有主意的人，行动多于言语，逢到这种时节，做派同旁人大不一样。她一“开酗”，镇警长就去钓鱼打猎，胆小的同行关张闭门，如临大围困——像唐人街宣布堂斗时的铺户。她的规矩是先遍访朋友的场子：情谊靠大把花钱大碗喝酒来重申、加固；欠的情，当场清偿。她把围着酒馆的流民、乞丐、破衣烂衫之辈灌到腿软腿断，跟不动她凯旋的队伍。她从不因缺钱歇手，也不因“载不住酒”歇手。她的狂欢收在“办完”全城最后一家——那一家，永远是某个同她结了仇的人的场子。
我和桑克有眼福，赶上她一场最难忘的庆祝的收梢。她把马车撞在路缘，步行进那倒霉蛋的酒馆，吩咐全场的人都喝。喝罢付讫，她双手扣住吧台内侧沿，把整座吧台掀翻在地。

===Original===

Out of the wreckage she gathered an armful of bottles. One of them was accurately hurled into the mirror, and the remainder at anybody in sight. The boss, bartender, and saloon bums disappeared out the back way and Mary stalked out the front. On the sidewalk she threw away her hat, tore up what money she had left, and crawled into her waiting hack. Inside she kicked all the glass out, lay down on the back seat, and, with her feet out through the broken window, was driven home in state while the town stood mute.
We allowed her a couple of days to recuperate before calling. When we appeared she welcomed us as usual with an invitation to eat from the bottomless bean pot. Sanc threw her the small parcel of stones which she examined carefully with practiced eye, and a high-powered glass. Commercial white diamonds were sixty dollars a carat, wholesale, then, and when she offered us eighteen hundred dollars we took them, satisfied.
We jumped to Denver, where Sanc got the dice-game concession in the Chicken Coop, a small gambling house. We had three thousand dollars between us. Two thousand went into the bankroll and we opened up bravely. Soapy Smith, gambler and bunko man, noted for his high plays and big winnings and losings, won our two thousand in three successive plays. Sanc wanted to continue with the balance of our money, but I refused and stubbornly held on to my last five hundred. We had to quit.
Sanc was a hard loser and followed Soapy around town for a week trying to “elevate” him. He never got away from the bright lights, and Sanc gave up the notion of sticking him up.
We located a big poker game in a soft spot and decided to line up the players. The biggest gamblers in town sat there nightly and there were thousands of dollars in sight. After many nights of careful checking, we were ready to go against it.
Robbery has none of the complications of burglary. It is simple as one, two, three. You get it or you don’t.
Sanc with a gun in his hand opened the door softly. I was behind him. The players, six of them, were in the midst of a big play. None looked up. At the opposite side of the table, facing us, scrutinizing his cards, sat Bat Masterson, the last of the real bad men, the fastest man alive with a gun, and with a record of twenty killings while marshal of Dodge City, Kansas.

===Chinese===

从瓦砾里她抱出一掃酒瓶。头一瓶分毫不差地砸进穿衣镜，余下的见谁砸谁。老板、酒保、酒馆闲人从后门溜尽，玛丽昂然出正门。人行道上她摔掉帽子，撕碎手边余钱，爬进候着的马车。车厢里她把玻璃尽数踹碎，仰卧后座，双脚从破窗伸出去，浩浩荡荡打道回府——全镇鸦雀无声。
我们容她将养了两天才登门。她照旧以一锅不见底的豆子相邀。桑克把那一小包石头抛给她；她拿行家的眼、外加一具高倍镜细验一过。那时商用白钻批发价每克拉六十元；她开价一千八，我们收了——知足。
我们跳到丹佛，桑克在“鸡窝”——一家小赌场——拿到了骰子摊的承办权。两人手握三千元。两千入了本钱，我们雄赳赳开张。“肥皂”史密斯——赌棍兼骗匠，以豪赌、大输大赢闻名——连玩三把，赢了我们的两千。桑克想拿余额接着赌，我不肯，死攝住我最后那五百。只好收摊。
桑克输不起，跟着“肥皂”满城转悠了一礼拜，想“抬举抬举”他。那家伙从没离开过灯火明亮处，桑克只得作罢，不抢他了。
我们踩着一处软点的豪赌牌局，打定主意收拾那桌玩家。全城最大的赌棍夜夜坐庄，台面上几千块。仔细核了多夜，我们备齐了下手。
抢劫没有夜盗那些枝节，简单得如一、二、三：到手，或没到手。
桑克握枪轻轻推开门，我在他身后。玩家六个，正酣。无人抬头。桌子对面正对我们、低头看牌的那一位，是巴特·马斯特森（Bat Masterson）——末代真正的狠角色，世上拔枪最快的人，任堪萨斯道奇城警长期间有二十条人命的记录。

===Original===

Sanc closed the door on the absorbed poker players as softly as he had opened it, and “officed” me to follow him out. “What’s wrong?” I asked.
“Nothing but this, Kid. Bat Masterson is sitting in the game. He has a reputation as a killer and he earned it. When he was marshal of Dodge City bad men rode from the Texas Panhandle and from Deadwood, Dakota, to shoot it out with him, and he dropped them all. Nobody knows how many; he probably don’t know himself. He beat them all to the pull and even now, when he is not exactly young, he is the fastest human being with a gun. All he has left is his reputation, and he would die rather than lose it to a couple of stickups. If I had him in a dark street I wouldn’t hesitate to throw him up and he would go up too, but he would never stand for it with others looking on. I could have put the gun on him, said: ‘Masterson, I know you; I’ll kill you if you bat an eye,’ and you could have started to gather up their coin. So far, so good; but if the brim of your hat or the tip of your shoulder had got between Mr. Masterson and my eye he would have pulled on us and I would have had to let go at him. If I didn’t kill or disable him with the first shot, he’d have slaughtered the pair of us.
“I’m not afraid of him, but I am not knowingly going to put myself in the position of killing anybody for his money. When Masterson sits in a game, he lays a gun on his lap. His life is forfeit to any ambitious young bad man looking for a reputation. Anybody but a stickup could kill him and get away with it, if he were fast enough or dirty enough to shoot him in the back. He has the right to sit with a gun on his lap, and besides, it gives him just a slight psychological edge on the other players.
“In short, Kid, I don’t want any of Mr. Batterson Masterson’s money by the stickup route. There are a lot of bad men here getting by on gall, but he has guts and I flatter myself that I’ve been around enough to know the difference. That’s my philosophy, Kid; you can take it or leave it,” he concluded.
That was the tribute Sanc, who killed one man and was hanged, paid Bat Masterson, who killed twenty and died peacefully in his bed at a ripe and respected old age.
Then came a night when the Sanctimonious Kid failed to show up at the room.

===Chinese===

桑克把门照原样轻轻带上，朝我“递了个眼色”：撤。到了外头我问：“怎么了？”
“没什么，孩子，就这一条：巴特·马斯特森坐在这个局里。他杀人的名声是挣来的。他掌道奇城那会儿，狠角色们从得克萨斯潘汉德尔、从达科他的戴德伍德赶去同他对枪，全叫他撞倒了。多少个，没人知道，他自己多半也不知道。比拔枪，他把他们全比了下去；如今纵然上了年纪，他仍是人间拿枪最快的人。他只剩这副名声了，他宁可死，也不肯折在两个毛贼手里。黑街上撞见他，我照抢不误，他也照举手不误；可当着满堂看客，他绝不会认。我原可以拿枪顶住他，说：‘马斯特森，我认得你；你眨一下眼我就要你的命’——你便动手收他们的钱。到这一步都好；可你的帽檐或肩头若有一瞬挡在我眼睛同马斯特森先生之间，他就先开了火，我也只得对他开火。我第一枪放不倒他、放不残他，咱们俩都得叫他包了。
“我不是怕他，可我不肯——明知故犯地——把自己放进‘为几个钱杀人’的境地。马斯特森入局，膝上必横一杆枪。他的命，抵给天下每一个想挣名声的年轻狠角色。除了劫道的，谁都可以杀他而全身而退——只要手够快，或心够脏，从背后打。他有把枪横在膝上的权利；何况，这还在心理上压其余玩家一头。
“总之，孩子，巴特·马斯特森先生的钱，我不走明抢的路子去取。这城里靠脸皮混的狠角色不少，可他有真胆气；我自负阅人不少，还分得出这两样。这就是我的哲学，孩子；听不听随你。”他收了尾。
这就是桑克——杀过一人、最终被绞死的桑克——献给巴特·马斯特森的赞辞：后者杀过二十人，寿终正寝于德高望重的高龄。

===Original===

I was worried and made the rounds of the gambling houses, joints, and hangouts, but failed to find him or anybody who had seen him. My fears were put to rest later when Soapy Smith, who won our bankroll, appeared in the Missouri House and told with the good grace of a man who had lost fortunes how he had been taken by a stickup man for fifteen hundred dollars.
“It was the first time in a year I had been off Larimer Street, and it serves me right,” he laughed. “Anyway, I know him and told him so; and I’m going to kill him on sight.”
“Soapy,” Jefferson Smith, was a colorful character of the West, the educated, refined, renegade son of a distinguished Southern family who turned his wits to crooked ways. When his luck at the faro tables switched and left him broke, he mounted his box on a corner and sold nickel bars of soap for fifty cents and one dollar. The fifty-cent bars were guaranteed to contain a five-dollar bill and the dollar ones a ten. The bills were wrapped with the soap in plain view of the prospective purchasers, and when he finished his spiel the rush was on.
His cappers, boosters, and shills fought with the yokels for a chance to get something for nothing and always beat them to the pieces of soap containing the money.
As a sleight-of-hand man Soapy had no equal off the stage. When the Gentiles wrested the Salt Lake City government from the Mormons, Soapy was brought out from Denver and sat as an election judge in the busiest polling booth in the city. Equipped with a poker player’s sleeve holdout he went out with enough Mormon ballots to swing a close election in favor of a “clean city administration.”
Always a pioneer, he joined the gold rush to the Klondike. He was killed at Skagway, later, by a bully in the pay of the Vigilance Committee.
After the robbery Sanc disappeared, and it was long till I saw him again. I decided to leave Denver. I owed them fifteen days on the chain gang, and had no wish to pay by shoveling snow in the streets. I bought a ticket to Butte, Montana, regretting the money but not caring to hit the road in the dead of winter across half a dozen of the coldest states in the Union. I did it in after years, but it was from necessity﻿—not choice.
Butte was never a “Wild West” town in the accepted sense. Cowboys were seldom seen. Miners and gamblers ruled the town.

===Chinese===

随后来了这样一个夜晚：假圣人小子没有回房。
我急了，把赌场、场子和落脚点兜了个遍，人没找着，见没见过的人也没找着。后来我心才落地：赢走我们本钱的“肥皂”史密斯出现在密苏里楼，以一种输过几番家业的人特有的豁达，讲他如何叫一个劫道的抢去一千五百块。
“一年了头一回离开拉里默街，活该，”他笑道。“好在我认得他，也告诉了他；见一面我杀一面。”
“肥皂”史密斯是西部一个有颜色的人物：南方望族出了个知书达理的逆子，把心思用到了歪道上。法罗桌上运气回了头、输了个精光，他就在街角登上他的木箱，把五毛一块的肥皂条卖给路人。五毛的保包一张五元钞，一块的保包一张十元。钞票同肥皂卷在一起，明晃晃摆在买主眼前；他一通吆喝收了口，人群便扑上来。
他的“托儿”、帮腔、装客的，同乡巴佬抢夺那“无本之利”，回回抢在他们前头，把包钱的肥皂先捞到手。
论手上功夫，“肥皂”在舞台之外没有对手。非摩门派从摩门教手里夺下盐湖城政权的那些日子，他从丹佛被请去，坐镇全城最热闹的投票站，当选举监察。凭一副牌手的藏牌袖具，他出门时怀里撞的摩门选票，足以把一场胶着的选举扳向“清廉市政”一边。
他一贯是开路的，淘金潮一起便去了克朗代克。后来在斯卡圭，他死于警备委员会豢养的一个恶棍枪下。
那桩抢案之后桑克没了踪影，过了很久我才再见着他。我决定离开丹佛：我还欠着他们十五天的锁链队，可不想靠在街上铲雪来还。我买了去蒙大拿比尤特的车票——钱花得心疼，却也顾不得：隆冬腊月，横穿合众国六个最冷的州，那路我不敢走。后来我走过了，那是出于必要——不是出于选择。
比尤特从来不是严格意义上的“狂野西部”镇子，难得见到牛仔，掌管镇子的是矿工和赌徒。

===Original===

The miners were orderly, hard workers, deep drinkers, and fair fighters. They had none of the cheap, shouldering swagger of the gold-rush miner. Nearly everybody owned a gun, but the bullying, gun-toting, would-be bad men and killers never flourished in Butte. When one of them got peeved and started to lug out his cannon some hard-fisted miner beefed him like an ox with a fast one to the jaw, and kicked his “gat” out into the street where small boys scrambled for it.
The mines were worked by Irishmen and “Cousin Jacks” (Cornishmen), who settled their differences with good, solid blows and despised the use of weapons. Gambling flourished and was licensed in Montana by an act of legislature. The possession of any crooked gambling device was a felony. Consequently the cheap cheaters and tinhorn, shoestring gamblers never got a footing there. Many of the faro dealers never made a bet of their own money, and many professional players were married men, who looked after their families faithfully, feeding and educating their children with money honestly and systematically won over the faro layouts. Everybody gambled. Messenger boys stopped to make a bet. The copper on the beat would walk in and bet “two and a half to win breakfast money.” If he won his two and a half he went out satisfied. If he lost it, he got stuck, forgot all about his beat, sat down, and played in his bankroll.
The town boasted of an all-night barber shop and a clothing store that never closed, for the convenience of late-at-night winners who couldn’t hold on to their winnings till morning.
As I watched a game my first night in Butte, a seedy, torn-out looking chap stepped up to the layout and made a bet. He won it, and, expertly shifting his checks about, won a dozen more bets before the deal closed. “Gimme money,” he said to the dealer, pushing his checks in, “I’m going to get dressed up this time.” He took off his rusty derby hat and tore the crown out of it. Putting his hands behind him, he grasped the tails of his frock coat and with a jerk ripped it up the back clear to the collar. Money in hand he sought the all-night store, and came back in an hour, spick and span from head to heel.
I experimented and soon laid a solid foundation for the faro-bank habit which fastened on me later and kept me broke for years.

===Chinese===

矿工们规矩，干活狠，喝酒深，打架凭真章。淘金矿工那种廉价的横肩阔步，他们一丝没有。差不多人人有枪，可是仗枪欺人、自命狠角色和职业杀手的，在比尤特从来兴不起来。哪个惹毛了动手拽炮，自有哪个铁拳的矿工一记快拳把他放倒像放倒一头牛，再把他那“家伙”踢到街上，任落子孩们哄抢。
矿上干活的是爱尔兰人和“杰克表亲”（康沃尔人）：有了过节凭结结实实的拳头解决，鄙夷动刀动枪。赌博兴旺，蒙大拿以立法许可。私藏赌具歪门是重罪，于是那类便宜骗子、假牌客、线头赌棍在那儿站不住脚。许多法罗荷官从不押自家的钱；许多职业玩家是有家室的人，照管家小尽心尽力，儿女的饭食学费，全是从法罗台面上规矩赢来、按部就班挣出的钱。人人都赌。送信的男孩停下来也要押一注。巡街的条子踱进来，押“两块半，赢个早饭钱”。两块半赢了，他心满意足出去；输了，他陷住了，巡逻忘个精光，坐下，把整袋工钱押了进去。
镇上引以为豪的，是一家通宵理发店和一家永不打烊的服装店——专为方便那些攋不住手气、等不到天亮的夜间赢家。
到比尤特的头一夜，我正看一局，一个邋遢的、浑身塌架模样的汉子踱到台前押了一注。赢了；又灵巧地倒腾筹码，一局下来连赢十几注。“给我兑现，”他把筹码推进去对荷官说，“这回我要置办行头。”他摘下锈毡帽，撕了帽顶；双手背到身后，抓住燕尾服的衣摆，一扯，把后襟从中缝一直撕到领子。钱一到手，他寻着那家通宵服装店，一个钟头后回来，从头到脚焕然一新。
我也试了试手，很快为法罗赌瘾打下了坚实的地基——这瘾日后缠上了我，叫我穷了好些年。

===Original===

Another night a player at the table who had lost steadily for an hour placed his last stack of checks on a card, saying to the dealer, “Turn the cards, Sam, that’s the last button on Gabe’s coat.” The cards were turned and the player lost.
As he was leaving, I looked at him closer, and was sure I knew him. I got up and stuck my hand out to him. He took it curiously. “You are George,” I said, “the gentleman who made my fight in the Kansas City jail the night of my first pinch.”

===Chinese===

又一夜，桌边一个连输了一个钟头的玩家，把最后一摞筹码押上一张牌，对荷官说：“翻牌吧，山姆——加百列外套上最后一颗扣子了。”牌翻了，他输了。
他起身要走，我凑近细看，认定我认得他。我起身伸出手去。他莫名其糊涂地握了。“你是乔治，”我说，“我头一回被捕那晚，在堪萨斯城拘留所里替我打那一架的先生。”

## XIV / 十四
===Original===

George smiled and took a better grip on my hand. “Yes, I remember you, young fellow. You’ve grown some. Have you gathered any wisdom?”
“I’ve gathered enough to know that you are entitled to any part of this,” I said, producing my small bankroll.
“This is payable tomorrow night,” he said, taking twenty dollars.
The money was returned promptly and a bond of friendship and confidence was formed that remains unbroken. I came to know him as “Rebel George,” prince of bunko men, the man who developed and perfected the “goldbrick” swindle. After stealing a fortune and losing it at faro bank, he quit in answer to the prayers of his faithful wife, who had for years shared his vicissitudes in and out of prison. At the age of sixty, prison bent and money broke, he started life on the level and when last I heard of him he was in a fair way to succeed in his small business.
From Butte I journeyed to Spokane, Washington, and then to Seattle. I marvel now that I did not stop in one of those spots of golden opportunity and go to work. With the money I would have saved in a couple of years I could have bought land or lots that would have made me independent in ten years. I think land hunger is inherited. I had no desire then, nor have I now, to own land. The desire to possess land, whether inherent or acquired, appears to me to be a sure safeguard against a wasted, dissolute, harmful life.
I had now become so saturated with the underworld atmosphere that no thought of any kind of honest endeavor entered my mind. Fully realizing their value, I passed by many splendid opportunities in the booming Western towns; not that I was lazy or indolent, but that business and the hoarding of money had no attraction. I will leave it to the scientists and investigators to explain why Johnnie Jones lands in a pulpit, and his chum next door, with equal opportunity, lands in a penitentiary. It’s too deep for me. I know I never had any money sense and never will have, and I know that had I been blessed, or cursed, with land hunger and money sense I would today have more honest dollars than I ever had crooked dimes.
Twenty years of moderate application to his business will make most any man independent.

===Chinese===

乔治笑着，把我的手握得更紧了些。“记得，记得你，小伙子。长了个子了。长了点见识没有？”
“长了一条：这点钱里有你的一份。”我说着掏出我那一小卷钞票。
“这一份明晚才到期呢。”他说着，抽走了二十块。
钱很快还上了；一桩友谊与信任的纽带就此结成，至今未断。我从此认得了他——“叛军乔治”，骗子行里的亲王，“金砖局”这场骗局的发明和完善者。他偷下过一份家财，又输在法罗牌桌上；他的贤妻多年随他进出监狱、同甘共苦，靠她的祈求，他金盆洗手。六十岁上，牢狱压弯了腰，钱财荡尽，他从头做起正行人；我最后一次听到他的消息时，他那盘小生意正有起色。
离开比尤特，我去了华盛顿州的斯波坎，再到西雅图。如今我纳帘：怎么没在这几处黄金机会之地停脚做工。凭两三年攒下的钱，我本可以置地置业，十年便独立了。我想，“土地饥渴”是娘胎里带的。我那时不想置地，如今也不想。占有土地的欲望——不管是天生的还是后来的——在我看来，是一份稳当的保险，保人不走荒废、放浪、害人的路。
这时的我，已叫底层世界的空气浸了个透，什么正经营生再也进不了脑子。西部那些蒸蒸日上的城镇里，多少大好机会，我尽知其价值，却一一走过；不是懒，不是惰，是买卖和攒钱于我毫无引力。约翰尼·琼斯何以落脚讲道坛，他一墙之隔、机会均等的伙伴何以落脚监狱——这个，留给科学家和研究者们去解释吧，太深，我不懂。我只知道：我对钱从来没有感觉，往后也不会有；我若蒙赐——或者说蒙祸——了土地饥渴和金钱感觉，今天我名下的正路银元，早多过我挣过的邪路铜子。
二十年不大不小地埋头一门营生，随便什么人都能独立。

===Original===

In twenty years a journeyman mechanic will handle more money than a first-class burglar, and at the end of that time he will have a home and a family and a little money in the bank, while the most persistent, sober, and industrious burglar is lucky to have his liberty. He is too old to learn a trade, too old and broken from doing time to tackle hard labor. Nobody will give him work. He has the prison horrors, and turns to cheap larcenies and spends the balance of his life doing short sentences in small jails.
In rare instances the broken thief finds friends, sympathetic, understanding, and ready to help him. Strong and kindly hands at his elbows ease him over the hard spots and direct him to some useful place in the world. Some understand such kindness and respond by breasting the current and battling upstream with their best strokes; others do not, or can not understand, and, like dead fish, float down and away forever.
My apprenticeship under the Sanctimonious Kid was all that could be desired by either of us, yet my education was far from finished. At Seattle, almost broke, and doubtful about being able to do anything worth while by myself, I cast about for a sidekicker. Seattle was rebuilding after her big fire. Money was plentiful, and I never saw such an aggregation of beggars, tramps, thieves, and yeggs as were gathered there. Gambling, prostitution, and the smuggling of opium flourished unmolested. The thieves hung out in Clancey’s gambling house, and were protected and exploited by him. They thought Clancey was a little “Hinky Dink” in a little Chicago.
Every time a thief showed up with a hundred dollars he was pinched, but Clancey took him out in an hour﻿—for the hundred. When it was too late they found out that it was he who had them pinched. Clancey died broke.
In Clancey’s I found “St. Louis Frank,” a product of Kerry Patch, a poor quarter of St. Louis, Missouri. I knew him from the bums’ convention at Pocatello, where I met him first. He was clean looking and healthy and I liked him. About my own age, he was an honest, industrious, intelligent thief.
One night as we were looking around one of the smaller gambling houses, all the lights went out suddenly, but before we could get our hands on any of the money, they came on again.

===Chinese===

二十年里，一个满师的匠人经手的钱，胜过一个一流的窃贼；到头来他有家、有眷、银行里有一小笔；而那最勤谨、最清醒、最卖力的窃贼，能有自由之身就算走运。他老得学不动手艺了，坐牢坐得又老又垮，苦力也扛不动。没人给他活干。他害着“监狱恐”，转去干小偷小摸，余生都在小监狱里吃短刑。
难得有几例，垮掉的贼遇上朋友：同情、体谅、肯帮。几双有力的、和善的手在肘边搀他一把，渡过难关，把他领到世上有用的一处。有人懂这份恩，挺胸逆流、奋力上游作报；也有人不懂，或不能懂，像死鱼，顺水漂下去，永不回头。
我在“假圣徒小子”桑克门下的学徒期，我们师徒两个都再满意不过，可我的教育还远没有完。在西雅图，我几乎盘缠用尽，又拿不准独自干能干出什么名堂，便物色起搭档来。西雅图大火之后正在重建，钱涌着；我平生再没见过那样一窝蜂的叫花子、流浪汉、窃贼和响马。赌、嫖、走私鸦片，无人过问。窃贼们聚在克兰西的赌场，受他保护，也受他盘剥。他们当克兰西是小小西雅图里的一个小小的“欣基丁克”。
窃贼一露出一百块，就叫人拿了；克兰西一个钟头就把人捞出来——为一百块。等他们明白正是他叫人抓的，已经迟了。克兰西死时一文不名。
在克兰西那里，我找到了“圣路易斯弗兰克”（St. Louis Frank）——克里补丁区的产物，密苏里州圣路易斯的一个穷区。波卡特洛的流浪汉大会上我头一回见他，认得他。他干净、健壮，我看得中。同我差不多年纪，一个本分、勤快、聪明的贼。
一天夜里，我们在一家小赌场里张望，灯忽然全灭了；不等我们的手够着钱，灯又亮了。

===Original===

This started us to figuring a way to put them out at a given time when one of us could stand near a table and grab the bankroll. We found where the wires entered the building, and Frank volunteered to cut them if I would stand inside by the faro game and snatch the bankroll which was lying exposed in an open drawer beside the dealer. It looked so good that we enlisted two other “Johnsons,” one to plant himself by the dice game, and the other by the roulette wheel. All three of us were to be in readiness to make a grab when the lights went out and make our way out of the building in the darkness.
Saturday night, when the biggest play was on, Frank made his way to the roof and we took up our posts inside.
I took a position by the faro game. The drawer that held the bankroll was so situated that a right-handed man would be handicapped in reaching for the money. Being left-handed, the spot fell to me. The drawer was open and the big leather pocketbook containing the money was lying in the bottom of it in plain sight, and not two feet from where I stood. The second man, in charge of the game, the “lookout,” sat in a high chair at the dealer’s right. One of his feet was resting on the edge of the open drawer, and I saw at a glance that if he jammed the drawer shut with his foot when the lights went out he would trap my hand.
While I was thinking that over, Frank cut the wires and everybody in the big room did just what we expected; they remained perfectly still for a second waiting for the light to come on.
My hand was on the big fat poke. I heard a jingle of gold coins across the room, and somebody shouted: “Thieves! Thieves!” The drawer was jammed shut on my hand.
Reaching down with my right hand I got the poke and tore my left hand out of its trap, leaving a piece of skin the size of a half dollar behind. There were three exits and we all got out safely in the scramble, and weighed in at my room.
Frank was there ahead of us. My grab yielded two thousand dollars in bills. The chap at the dice game put his hand on a stack of twenty twenty-dollar gold pieces, but it was too heavy and he fumbled it, getting only half. Our assistant at the roulette wheel got a couple of stacks of silver only.
The money was split at once, and our friends departed. Frank took most of our money out and left it with a saloon man we knew.

===Chinese===

这叫我们琢磨起一个法子：定个时候把灯弄灭，一个人守在赌台边，一把抓走赌本。我们寻着电线进楼的地方，弗兰克自告奋勇去割线，由我守在里头法罗牌旁边，抢那只摊在庄家手边敞开的抽屉里的赌本。这买卖看着太肥，我们又招了两个“约翰逊家”的：一个钉住掷骰台，一个钉住轮盘。三个人都预备好，灯一灭就下手，趁黑出楼。
星期六夜里，赌注最旺的时候，弗兰克上了房顶，我们在里面各就各位。
我守在法罗牌旁。放赌本的那只抽屉，位置对惯用右手的人不便；我惯用左手，这位置正归我。抽屉开着，装钱的大皮夹就躺在抽屉底，一望而知，离我站的地方不到两英尺。监管牌局的第二个人——“瞭望”——坐在庄家右手的高椅上，一只脚正搭在敞开的抽屉沿上。我一眼看出：灯一灭他若用脚把抽屉踹死，我的手就成了夹子里的肉。
我正盘算这个，弗兰克割断了电线，大屋里人人果然如我们所料：静了一瞬，只等灯亮。
我的手已经搭上那只肥鼓鼓的钱包。屋子那头金币一响，有人喊：“有贼！有贼！”抽屉咔哒夹死了我的手。
我右手探下去抓住钱包，左手从夹子里硬拽出来，留下了半块银元大的一块皮。屋子有三个出口，乱哄哄里我们全平安脱身，到我屋里碰头过秤。
弗兰克比我们先到。我那一把得了两千块钞票。守骰子台的小伙子一只手按上一摞二十枚一摞的二十元金币，太沉，没抓牢，只捞走一半。轮盘那边的帮手只抓了两小摞银元。
钱当场分净，朋友们散去。弗兰克把我们的钱大半带出去，存在一个我们相熟的酒馆老板那里。

===Original===

I stayed in my room a couple of days waiting for my hand to heal.
One great failing of the thief is that when he gets money he immediately makes tracks for some hangout where he throws a few dollars on the bar just to “give the house a tumble” and let them guess where he scored and how much he got.
He looks wise, says nothing, spends a few dollars, and goes out. Then the guessing begins and it’s surprising what good guessers some poor thieves are.
Frank insisted that we go downtown a few nights later. I took the bandage off my hand and went along. We dropped into Billy the Mug’s, bought a few drinks, and departed. Half a block away we were pounced on by a couple of “dicks.” One of them jerked my left hand out of my coat pocket where I had been keeping it to conceal the skinned place. “That’s enough,” he said, looking at it. They marched us down to the gambling house and showed us to the game keepers. They got no satisfaction there, the gamblers either could not or would not identify us. We were then taken down to the city can, where they searched us thoroughly, finding nothing but a few dollars in silver. We were questioned separately and together, but refused to talk﻿—a guilty man’s only refuge. The officers ordered the man on the desk to put us on the “small book,” meaning to hold us as suspicious characters. “Maybe Corbett can get something out of you,” one of them said to us as they were leaving.
John Corbett was the officer in charge of the city jail. He was feared and hated from one end of the country to the other because of his brutality to prisoners. I doubt if a more brutal, bloodthirsty jailer ever flourished anywhere. He did not limit his beatings to underworld people. He beat up rich men, poor men, beggarmen, and thieves impartially. Anybody that didn’t crawl for Corbett got a good tamping. He was repeatedly brought before the commission for his cruelty to unfortunates falling into his hands, but for years mustered enough influence to hold his job. Corbett’s treatment of prisoners was the shame and scandal of Seattle, and he kept it up until the women of that city got the right to vote. Then their clubs, in a body, went to the mayor and demanded Corbett’s removal. He was removed.
Corbett appeared and took me downstairs where the cells were, in a moldy, damp, dark half basement. He was a powerful man, not tall, but thick and broad.

===Chinese===

我在屋里躲了两天，等手上的伤愈合。
贼的一大毛病：一有钱，立时奔往老落脚点，往吧台上拍几块，“给店里报个响儿”，由他们猜你这一票在哪里做的、得了多少。
他一脸高深，一言不发，花掉几块，出门。随后猜测开场，而有些穷贼猜东西的本事之准，叫人吃惊。
弗兰克非要我们几天后的夜里进城一趟。我解了手上的绷带，同行。我们进“大杯比利”的店，喝了几杯，走了。半个街区外，两个“暗探”扑上来。一个把我左手从我大衣兜里拽出来——我一直揣着它，遮那块蹭掉的皮。“这就够了。”他看着那手说。他们押我们下那家赌场，把我们亮给开局的看。赌场那边没给他们想要的——开局的认不出我们，或不愿认。我们又给押到市“罐头”里，搜了个透，只搜出几块银钱。分开问、合着问，我们一概不开口——罪案中人惟一的庇护所。警官吩咐案头的把我们记上“小黑册”：按形迹可疑收押。“兴许科贝特能从你们嘴里掏出点什么。”临走时一个对我们说。
约翰·科贝特是市监狱的主管。全国上下，从这头到那头，人人怕他恨他，为他对囚犯的凶暴。我疑心再没有比他更凶暴、更嗜血的狱吏在哪处得志过。他的毒打不限于底层人。阔的、穷的、讨饭的、偷儿的，他一视同仁地打。凡不肯在科贝特面前爬的，都领一顿结实的捶。他一犯再审地因虐待落入他手的倒霉人被传到委员会面前，可多年来总有足够的花花势力保住他的差事。科贝特待囚犯的手段是西雅图的耻辱和丑闻；他一直干到西雅图的女人们争得选举权那一天。于是她们的社团全体出动，去见市长，要求撤科贝特。撤了。
科贝特来了，把我带下囚室所在的楼层——一间霉湿漆黑的半地下。他是个蛮有力气的人，个子不高，厚而宽。

===Original===

He was black-browed, brutal-faced, heavy-jawed. He opened a cell door and I started to step in but he detained me. I sensed something wrong. His brownish-red eyes gleamed like a fanatic’s. “You’d better tell me all about that robbery, young man.” His voice was cold, level, and passionless.
“I know nothing about it, sir,” I answered very decently; I was afraid. Like a flash one of his hands went to my throat. He pinned me to the wall, choking me, and brought something down on my head with the other hand that turned everything yellow and made my knees weaken. Still holding me by the throat he lifted me clear of the floor and threw me into the cell like a bundle of rags. There was about a half inch of water on the cell floor. I lay there in it, and looked about me by the dim light of a gas jet out in the corridor. There was nothing in the cell but a wooden bench.
After a few minutes I crawled over to it, and, pulling myself up, stretched out, more dead than alive. If people can be corrected by cruelty I would have left that cell a saint.
St. Louis Frank, in another part of the jail, got a worse beating than I did.
Our friends outside were busy. At ten o’clock next morning James Hamilton Lewis, affectionately called “Jim Ham,” later United States senator from Illinois, then an ambitious fighting young lawyer who never laid down on a client, came to see us. At two that afternoon he had us out on a writ, free.
From that day on St. Louis Frank smiled no more. He became snarly, short spoken, and ugly. We got our money and parted. He went out on the road “bull simple,” simple on the subject of shooting policemen. The stories told about him are almost unbelievable. Years later I saw him in the San Francisco county jail where he was waiting trial for the murder of a police officer in Valencia Street. The day he went to San Quentin where he was hanged, he sang out to me: “So long, Blacky. If I could have got Corbett I wouldn’t care.”
All Corbett’s beating did for me was to make me a little more careful. I got a boat to San Francisco, not knowing just what to do, but with a notion of killing time till old Foot-and-a-Half George finished his time in Utah, and meeting him. I dug up the hotel keys Sanc and I had planted and experimented a little in hotel prowling. I hadn’t the sure touch that came in later years with experience, and didn’t do much good.

===Chinese===

黑眉，凶脸，重颚。他拉开一扇囚室的门，我抬脚要进，他把我按住。我觉出不对。他那双棕红色的眼睛，亮得像狂信徒。“你顶好把那桩抢案一五一十告诉我，年轻人。”嗓音又冷又平，不带感情。
“我一点不知情，先生，”我答得规规矩矩；我怕他。说时迟那时快，他一只手已掐上我的喉咙，把我钉在墙上，掐得我出不来气，另一只手朝我头上一记，打得眼前一片金黄、双膝发软。他掐着喉咙把我整个提离地面，扔进囚室，像扔一捆破布。囚室的地上积着约半英寸的水。我躺在水里，借着走廊外一盏煤气灯的微光四下看。囚室里只有一条木凳。
过了几分钟，我爬过去，攀上去，躺平，只剩半口气。人若真能靠残酷矫正，我出那间囚室时早成圣徒了。
圣路易斯弗兰克在监狱另一处，换的打比我重。
外面的朋友们忙起来了。第二天上午十点，詹姆斯·汉密尔顿·刘易斯——人称“吉姆·汉姆”，后来做过伊利诺伊州的联邦参议员，当时是个雄心勃勃、从不在委托人身上撒手的年轻讼师——来看我们。下午两点，一纸令状，把我们捞了出去。
打那天起，圣路易斯弗兰克不再笑了。他变得狂躁、寡言、凶恶。我们取回钱，分了手。他上路去了，得了“牛魔怔”——见不得警察，一心要开枪打警察。关于他的传闻，几乎叫人不敢信。多年后，我在旧金山县监狱见到他——他在那里候审，罪名是在瓦伦西亚街杀死一名警官。他解往圣昆廷受绞的那天，朝我喊了一声：“回见，布莱基！可惜没干成科贝特，干成了我就不亏。”
