One of them, a giant, redheaded Irishman with shaggy, white eyebrows and pebbly blue eyes, said: “Don’t worry, me boy, there’ll be no lynchin’ here.”

===Chinese===

“请进来一下，年轻人。”他说。屋里坐着一群人，给了我一把椅子。我打定主意一个问题不答。长篇大套地解释自己的行踪，回头叫调查一一戳穿、坐实，倒把案子朝自己身上加固——没这个必要。
治安官朝我开腔：“年轻人，我们要你交代你在此地的营生：你是谁，哪里来，靠什么过活。你被疑为昨夜进入快递站长家宅、掠走四千八百块现款的窃贼。今早日出时有人见你进了旅馆。解释给我们听。”
我的心跳回那些鞭打、那些面包白水的黑号、那次越狱、以及我不皱一下眉受下来的一切苦处，我朝他笑了。眼前不过是个矮小、自负的胖子，戴顶黑色瓜皮帽，坐在一张摆着几张法律文书的破桌子后头咋咋呼呼，想叫我开口。
我对他说：“您在我那家旅馆睡过没有？”
“睡过。”他答，忘了他的威严。
“那么您知道，盥洗房和厕所设在两翼之间搭顶的敞廊里，在楼外后头；去那儿得下后楼梯、出后门。”
“很好，”他回，“那说说，前两回饷银卸车，你为什么都在车站？为什么跟着它到矿上去？昨夜为什么跟着站长回家？”
我拿全副能攒出的愠怒硬顶，提醒他我是英国臣民，有权缄默、不答一字。矿监工故意让我听见似的对众人说：“我的人要私刑 somebody——no → “我的人憋着要吊死个把人；管得住管不住，难说。”矿工那一套我全懂：他们从来在哪儿也不私刑谁。我也知道我在加拿大，私刑从没在这片土地上得势过。
我转向屋后那群矿工。“这位说你们要私刑我。动手之前，我想先弄明白为什么。”
其中一个——红头发的爱尔兰巨人，白眉毛浓密，蓝眼睛像两粒卵石——说：“放心，小子，这儿没有私刑。”

===Original===

Turning to the magistrate, I said: “You telegraph, at my expense, the Minister of Justice at Ottawa that this man,” pointing to the superintendent, “is trying to incite these miners to lynch me. And I want a copy of the telegram and my request made a matter of record in your office.”
The magistrate looked confused. The superintendent said, “I’ll make the complaint. I want this man held.” The constable arrested me “In the queen’s name,” searched me and locked me in the town jail, a one-room affair in a lot back of his house. He brought me blankets and supper, and left me to meditate in silence and privacy. I went over everything again. The shoe was on the other foot this time; they hadn’t a thing on me. No court in Christendom would convict me on those shreds of suspicion. I rolled up in the blankets and went to sleep with the comfortable thought that the forty-eight hundred dollars might help me to forget the awful lashing I took at New Westminster when I got to San Francisco or Chicago with them.
Next morning in court it was shown by witnesses that I was at the depot twice when the money arrived; that I went to the mine once on the same train with it; and a woman testified that she saw me walking behind the station agent and his neighbors as they went home the night of the burglary. The porter who spilled the beans finished the trial by testifying that he met me on the stairs in the early morning; that I was nervous, acted suspiciously, and tried to avoid him.
I made no defense, but set up a loud and long protest that did no good. I was held for trial and whisked away to the provincial jail. I was convinced that the mine superintendent had engineered the thing on the theory that I was guilty and in hopes that something would transpire in his favor while I was waiting to be tried.
The provincial jail was like the one I escaped from. The jailer was sober and decent, giving me plenty of books and fair treatment. I had a cell to myself, kept away from the other prisoners, tried my case mentally every day, and got acquitted every time, and at night I spent large chunks of the payroll that was so securely planted in the post hole.
After three months of leisure, reading and pleasant anticipations, I went to court. A judge had arrived for the fall assizes and mine was the only felony case. The witnesses were all there.

===Chinese===

我转向治安官：“请您拍电报——费用我出——给渥太华的司法部长，就说这个人，”我指着监工，“企图煽动这些矿工私刑我。电文抄件及我的请求，要在您办公室立案存档。”
治安官一脸慌乱。监工说：“我来控告。我要押住这个人。”警长“以女王之名”逮捕了我，搜了我，把我锁进镇监——他屋后大院里的一间单屋。他送来毯子和晚饭，撇下我，让我在清静里沉思。我把一切又过了一遍：这回风水倒了，他们手里没有一件东西。基督世界随便哪家法庭，凭这几丝嫌疑也定不了我的罪。我裹进毯子睡去，揣着一个熨帖的念头：那四千八百块，等我把它们带到旧金山或芝加哥，兴许能帮我把新威斯敏斯特那顿可怕的鞭打忘掉几分。
第二天早晨开庭。证人证称：饷银两回到达时我都在车站；有一回同车随它去了矿上；一个女人作证，盗窃那夜她看见我缀在站长和他邻居们身后回家。倒豆子的杂役给审讯收了尾：清晨他在楼梯上撞见我；我神色紧张、行迹可疑、想躲他。
我不作辩护，只 loudly 抗议——no → 我不作辩护，只高声长抗议了一场，无济于事。我还押候审，被飞快解往省监狱。我确信这是矿监工一手促成：他认定我有罪，指望我候审期间出点什么岔子，好遂他的愿。
省监狱同我逃出的那座一个样。狱卒清醒正派，书给得足，待遇公道。我一人一间号，同别的囚犯隔开；每天在脑子里开庭审我自己的案子，回回判无罪；到了夜里，我把那笔稳稳埋在桩洞里的饷银，大块大块地花。
三个月的清闲、读书与美好憧憬之后，我上了庭。秋季巡回的法官到了，重案只有我一件。证人们都在。

===Original===

I pleaded not guilty and my case was set for trial at two o’clock in the afternoon. The judge asked if I had counsel. I told him I had no lawyer and didn’t want one; that I had but little money and would need it when I was acquitted.
“Better get counsel,” he snapped. “A defendant that tries his own case has a fool for a lawyer.”
I replied by asking him to read the testimony from the lower court before wasting his time trying me.
While I was eating dinner the mine superintendent came to my cell and said if I would give up the money I could go free. I threatened to sue him for false imprisonment when I got out. He went away mad. I decided they wanted me to get a local lawyer in hopes I would unbosom myself to him so he could do the same with them.
At two o’clock I was back in court. The judge was a brusque, businesslike little old man with the brisk, aggressive air of a terrier. His hair was clipped short and his cropped beard grew in every direction. He wore a coarse suit of tweeds, hobnailed shoes, and a cheap flannel shirt, with a linen collar and no tie. He had two sheets of paper in his hand, looking at them.
“Have you anything more than this?” he asked the Crown counsel.
“Nothing, Your Lordship.”
“And do you expect me to try a man on this ragged rot?” Throwing the papers on his desk, he turned toward me. “Defendant discharged.”
I walked out and over to the jail, where I got my belongings﻿—forty dollars, a pocketknife, and a lead pencil. The constable who made the arrest followed me all over town﻿—to the barber’s, the restaurant, and at last to the depot. A train came in going in the opposite direction from the scene of the burglary, and I boarded it without buying a ticket. He watched the train leave, but did not get on. At a junction I got off and traveled south to a stage-line connection, where I took the stage back in the direction of my money. After ten days of maneuvering and detouring, I reached a settlement off the railroad about thirty miles away from where I was arrested.
Hiring a saddle horse there, I jogged away in high spirits, timing myself to get into the town about midnight, when I could lift the plant and be far away before daylight. Everything favored me as I rode into the town. There wasn’t a stray dog or cat on the back streets, and every soul was in bed asleep. Turning a corner, I came in sight of the big, vacant lot.

===Chinese===

我辩称无罪，案子定在下午两点开审。法官问我有无律师。我说没有律师，也不要：我钱不多，无罪开释后还用得着。
“最好请一位，”他厉声说。“自己替自己打官司的被告，请的是个傻瓜律师。”
我回请他先读一读下级法院的证词，再费工夫审我，免得白费。
我吃晚饭时，矿监工到号里来，说钱交出来我就走人。我威胁说出去要告他非法拘禁。他悻悻去了。我明白了：他们巴不得我雇个本地律师，指望我对他吐露心腹，他再吐给他们。
两点钟我回到法庭。法官是个急躁、公事公办的小老头，那股利落劲头像条梗犬。头发剪得极短，乱须四面长。一身粗花呢，钉着铁掌的鞋，廉价法兰纶衬衫，亚麻领子，不打领带。他手里捏着两页纸，看着。
“除了这个，还有别的吗？”他问王室律师。
“没有了，大人。”
“你们指望我拿这堆破烂审人？”他把纸撂在桌上，转向我。“被告开释。”
我走出法庭，去监狱取我的行李——四十块钱，一把小刀，一支铅笔。逮捕我的警长满城跟着我——理发店、饭馆，末了到车站。一列往盗窃现场反方向去的车进站，我没买票就上了。他望着车走，没有上。到一个枢纽站我下车，南行接上驿车线，坐驿车折回我钱的方向。十天的迂回腾挪之后，我到了一个离被捕之地约三十英里、不靠铁路的居民点。
在那儿租了匹坐骑，兴冲冲上路，掐着时辰午夜前后进镇——那时我能起出赃，天亮前人已在几十里外。一路进镇，样样遂我的意：后街没有一条闲狗野猫，家家户户睡得沉。拐过一个街角，那片大空场在望了。

===Original===

Yes, there it was. My eye took in the row of leaning, rotten posts down one side and across the front, then to the inner line of the lot where I put the leather pouch in its post hole. There seemed to be a change there. Was I at the right lot? Yes, there, a block away, was the house I entered, plain in the moonlight. My eye followed the route I took from the house to the lot. I pulled up the tired horse, certain I was at the right spot; and I was.
But on top of the spot, directly on top of it, stood a long, wide, well-built, substantial, two-story frame building.
That hour when I saw the money was lost to me was probably the saddest of my life. I never got a greater shock. I am telling this story because it is an interesting incident, not to cause anybody misery or mirth. Yet I know that any thief reading it will groan in sympathy with me. The honest reader will laugh and say: “It served him right.”
In the last ten years I have learned money-honesty. I have come to like it, it has become a habit. I practice it daily. Someday I may learn to laugh at the loss of that forty-eight hundred, but I’ll never learn to like laughing over it. It will never become a habit to be practiced daily.
Slumping off the horse, I threw the bridle rein over his head, left him standing patiently in the street, and walked stiffly over to the building. As near as I could judge one corner of it was directly over the spot where I made my plant. The front and one side of it covered the line where the decayed fence had formerly marked the boundary of the lot. Small barred windows in the cement foundation showed there was a basement and crushed my hopes that the money might be under the building. A careful survey of the place convinced me that the payroll was gone and there was no use in hanging around and inviting another pinch. Right here I should have muttered a string of oaths, thrown myself into the saddle, sunk my spurs, rowel deep, into the flanks of my horse, and dashed madly out of town. Sore from the long ride, I was barely able to throw a stiff leg over the saddle and settle down in it. Turning the horse’s head in the direction of his home, I threw the reins on his neck and let him jog along as he liked. I was crushed. It was an hour before I could think rationally. Then the question came into my mind that is in your mind now, reader. Who got the money?

===Chinese===

在，就在那儿。我的眼睛扫过一侧和正面那排歪斜的烂桩，再扫到我把皮袋埋进桩洞的那一线内界。那里像是变了样。是这块场子么？是，那儿，隔一个街区，月光底下分明立着我进过的那所房子。我的眼睛顺着那夜我从房子到场子的路线——我勤住乏马，笃定是那块地方；一点不错。
可那地方的顶上——不偏不倚正顶在上面——立着一座长方宽敞、建造结实、敦敦实实的两层木楼。
望见钱完了的那个时辰，多半是我一生最惨的时辰。我受过的打击，没有比这更重的。讲这段，是因为它是一桩有味的轶事，不为叫谁难过或谁发笑。可我知道，哪个贼读到它，都会陪着我哼一声；正派的读者会笑道：“活该。”
这十年我学会了钱的干净。我渐渐喜欢它，成了习惯，天天习练。有朝一日我兴许也能学会对那四千八百块的损失一笑，可我永远学不会喜欢笑它。它永远成不了天天习练的习惯。
我滑下马，缰绳往它头上一搭，撂它立在街上耐心等，僵着两条腿走到那楼跟前。凭目测，楼的一角正压着我藏宝的那一点；楼的正面和一侧，罩住了原先烂篱笆标出的那道场界。水泥地基上一扇扇装着铁栏的小窗告诉我底下有地下室，也掐灭了我指望钱在楼底的最后一点念头。细看一遍，我信了：饷银没了；赖在这儿招人再抓一回，毫无意义。按说，此刻我该骂上一长串，翻身上鞍，马刺扎进马腹，疯了一样冲出镇去。长鞍磨得我浑身酸痛，勉强才把一条僵腿甩过鞍子坐实。我拨转马头朝它家的方向，缰绳往脖子上一撂，随它自己颠去。我垮了。过了一个钟头，我才能正常地想事情。随后，此刻正盘踞在您心里的那个问题，也进了我的心：钱落到谁手里了？

===Original===

I wish I could tell you, but I can’t. I don’t know. I wish I could tell what became of Julia, the girl from Madam Singleton’s. I wish I could tell you what was done at the Diamond Palace when the tray of stones was missed, and whether Chew Chee, the Chinese boy, was recaptured and had to go to prison.
I can’t answer any of those questions. The nature of my business was such that I preferred to leave them unanswered rather than bring disaster by inquiring too closely. I know the mine payroll never got back into the owner’s hands. I assume it was found by a laborer, who kept it. The reconstruction of the finding of my money and the picturing of its finder furnished me with many hours of mental relaxation. Lying in the dungeon or in the hop joint or on the grass in the public parks, I pieced it together painfully. But never could I see the lucky finder as an honest man; nor by any effort of imagination could I ever picture him as putting the money to any good use. In the end, he always turned out to be a drunken, dissolute day laborer, sweating in the sun as he dug out the ditch for the foundation of that building. I saw him turn up the leather pouch with his shovel, seize it, open it stealthily, and thrust it inside his shirt. Then he threw down his shovel, walked over to the boss, and demanded his “time.”
I heard the foreman say: “All right, you’re no good anyway. I was going to fire you tonight.”
I followed the finder to the little depot where he bought a ticket to the wide-open joyful town of Montreal. There I watched him dissipate my money riotously in the slums. At last, and with great satisfaction, I saw him dead in the gutter without a dime, and followed his body to the morgue, where the coroner pronounced it a case of “acute alcoholism.”
After a long tiresome ride I delivered the fagged horse to his owner and went off to a quiet spot to sum up and see how I stood with the world. My loss was not put down to poetic justice or attributed to the law of compensation. I just classified it as a mess of tough luck and tried to forget it. An inventory of my possessions showed one pocketknife, one pencil, a bandana handkerchief, tobacco and papers, and nine dollars. I was a hundred miles away from any place where I could put my hands on a dollar without getting arrested at sunrise. I had no gun, no keys, no instruments. I was a shorn lamb.

===Chinese===

我倒想奉告，可我不能。我不知道。我也想告诉您，辛格尔顿夫人家的朱莉娅后来怎样了；想告诉您，钻石行那一盘宝石失踪后出了什么事；想告诉您，中国小伙周齐有没有被追回、蹲了监狱。
这些问题，我一个也答不上。我那门营生的性子，就是宁可留着它们不答，也不为寻根问底招来塌天的祸。我只知道那笔矿饷再没回到主家手里。我假定它叫某个劳工拾了，昧下了。这笔钱的失而复得怎样发生、拾主又是何等人物——重构这一幕，供了我许多个钟头的心神消遣。躺在黑牢里、烟馆里、公园草地上，我一寸一寸把它拼出来。可无论怎样拼，那幸运的拾主总不是个正派人；无论怎样驰骋想象，我也想不出他把钱用在任何好处上。到头来，他永远是那个替这楼挖地基、在日头底下淌汗的、酗酒放荡的计日零工。我看见他一锹翻出皮袋，一把攫住，四下张望，塞进怀里。随后他撂下铁锹，走到工头跟前讨他的“工钱”。
我听见工头说：“行啊，反正你也不是什么好货。我今晚正要撵你呢。”
我跟着拾主到那座小车站，他买了张票，去那座敞着大门、欢天喜地的蒙特利尔。在那儿的贫民窟里，我看他拿我的钱穷奢极欲。末了，我大为满意地看见他死在阴沟里，身上一个铜子也没有，尸首进了陈尸所，验尸官判为“急性酒精中毒”。
骑了长长一段乏人的路，我把累垮的马交还马主，寻了个清静地方盘点，看看自己在世上站到了哪一步。这笔损失，我不归给什么诗性报应，也不推给什么补偿法则；我只把它归作一大团倒霉，想法子忘掉。清点家当：一把小刀，一支铅笔，一条大手帕，烟叶和纸，九块钱。方圆一百英里内，没有一个地方能让我不挨日出时被捕地摸到一块钱。没有枪，没有钥匙，没有行头。我是一只剪了毛的羊。

===Original===

No one but myself was to blame for the shearing, and it was up to me to get busy and temper the wind as quickly as possible.
In the way of clothes I had those I left Los Angeles in﻿—overalls, blue shirt, a stout coat, and a cheap cap.
After bumming a stage ride, beating my way over a jerkwater branch road, and stowing away on a Columbia River boat loaded with dynamite and explosive oil for the mines, I got into one of the prosperous camps with a lone dollar in my pocket. The town was booming; crowded with miners, prospectors, and speculators. Beds were two dollars a night and meals a dollar. This caused me no uneasiness, for I knew it would be easier to get three dollars there than fifteen cents in the big cities where, in those days, a chicken dinner could be had for a dime and a flop on a bare floor for a “jit,” as the Southern negro affectionately calls his nickel.
In a restaurant I met an old friend in the person of the waiter, who had formerly been a very active member of the “Johnson” family. He was still in good standing, although he had retired from the road after several unfortunate experiments with burglary and robbery. It was late at night; I took an hour to eat, and he listened with genuine sympathy to all the harrowing details of my latest experience. At the cash register, where he also officiated, my dollar was no good. He offered to share his room with me, and when I declined, explaining that I would be too busy to sleep, he magnanimously suggested that I prowl the joint he lived in. This looked all right, and I took his room key with the understanding that if anything went wrong in the place I could say I was looking for his room and he would alibi for me.
It was late when I started to look through his hotel. I lost half an hour locating a sleeper with his door unlocked, and another half hour pulling his moneyless trousers from under his head, where he had placed them from force of habit. I fared better in another room, and, calling it a night, hastened to my friend’s place to return his key and look over my takings.
The restaurant was deserted. I joined him at a back table, where he sat cleaning a row of water glasses. Blowing his breath into one, like a housewife with a lamp chimney, he polished it carefully with a soiled napkin. “Did you score?” he inquired.
“Yes,” I said.
I spread my money on the table to look it over.

===Chinese===

这身羊毛怨不得别人，怨我自己；要紧的是赶紧忙起来，好歹给这阵风加加温。
衣裳么，还是离开洛杉矶那身：背带裤、蓝衬衫、厚外衣、一顶便宜鸭舌帽。
讨了一段驿车，扒过一条支线小铁路，又藏在一条满载矿用炸药和炸油的哥伦比亚河船上，我进到一处兴旺的矿区，兜里孤零零一块钱。镇子正 boom——no，镇子正闹热，挤满矿工、探矿人和投机客。铺位一夜两块，饭一餐一块。这倒不叫我不安：我知道在这儿挣三块，比在大城市挣一毛五容易——那年月的大城市，一毛钱一顿鸡饭，五分一个铺位（南方黑兄弟亲热地管他的镍币叫“吉特”）。
在一家饭馆，我遇上个老朋友——就是那位堂倌，“约翰逊家”昔年极活跃的一员。他至今信誉良好，虽说几番盗窃抢截的倒霉试手之后，已从路上退了下来。夜深了；我吃了一个钟头，把最近这场惨事的种种细节讲给他听，他听得真心难过。在他兼管的收银台前，我那一块钱他不肯收。他让我同他睡一间屋；我谢了，解释说我要忙，睡不成；他慨然建议：去他住的那家旅馆摸一摸。这看着妥当，我带了他房间的钥匙，讲定：万一头里出事，我就说寻他的房间，他替我作不在场证明。
开始巡他那家旅馆时夜已深。我花了半个钟头才寻着一间没锁门的睡客，又花半个钟头，才把他那件没有一文钱的裤子从他头底下拽出来——习惯使然，他把它枕在了头下。另一间屋子我手气好些；到此为止吧，我赶到朋友店里还钥匙、清点收获。
饭馆空了。我坐到他后头一张桌边，他正擦一排水杯。他朝一只里哈气——像主妇对着灯罩那样——拿一块脏餐巾细细拋光。“得手了？”他问。
“得了。”我说。
我把钱摊在桌上清点。

===Original===

My sad experience with the twenty-dollar bill had made me careful. The paper money looked safe enough, and so did the silver except one fifty-cent piece that was worn smooth and had a monogram engraved on one side of it﻿—a pocket piece or keepsake.
“Take this out in the back and throw it away,” I said to him; “it’s deadly poison.”
Instead, he rang it up on the register, saying, “That will pay your check; no use wasting it. It will go to the bank in the morning.”
I went to his room and to bed. At seven o’clock he came in with this amazing story, and I suggest a careful study of it by any young man who thinks stealing is an exact science, and all he has to do is outwit the coppers.
“Thirty minutes after you left the restaurant,” said he, “a guy came in and ate his breakfast in silence. He laid down a five-dollar bill to pay his check and the four-bit piece you threw me was in the change I gave him. He jumped stiff-legged and began frothing at the mouth when he saw it.
“ ‘I was robbed last night in my room. I had to wake the clerk up this morning and borrow five dollars. This fifty-cent piece is mine. I had it last night. It has my initials on it. I’ve carried it five years. How did it get into your till?’
“I told him I took it in an hour before from a short, stout, redheaded man with a broken nose and about forty years old. He is going to have a policeman at the place until the redhead shows up again. I promised to point him out if I see him anywhere. You’re safe; but you had better eat somewhere else for a while.”

===Chinese===

那张二十元票子的惨痛教训叫我学了乖。纸币瞧着干净；银币也都干净，只除了一枚五毛的，磨得溜光，一面镌着一个花押——主人的随身物，念想。
“拿到后头扔了它，”我对他说，“这是毒药。”
他反倒把它 ringing 收进了收银机——“no，他反倒把它当啷一声收进收银机，“这就抵了你的账；白撟了可惜。明早进银行。”
我回他房间睡了。七点钟他进来，带来这样一个惊人的故事。凡以为偷是一门精密科学、只消斗得过条子的年轻人，我都劝他把这故事细读一遍。
“你走了半个钟头，”他说，“进来一个家伙，闷头吃了顿早饭。他撟下五块钱会账，我找他的零头里，就有你给我的那枚五毛。他一见，直挺挺蹦起来，嘴上冒沫。
“‘昨夜我在屋里被偷了。今早我只好叫醒茶房借了五块。这枚五毛是我的。昨夜还在我身上。上面有我的花押。我带了它五年。它怎么会进了你的钱匣？’
“我告诉他，一个钟头前从一个矮壮、红头发、塌鼻子、四十来岁的人手里收的。他要派一个警察守在我店里，等那红头发再露面。我应承了，一见他就指认。你没事；只是往后一阵子，你顶好换个地方吃饭。”

## XXI / 二十一
===Original===

This strange coincidence of the marked piece of silver more than ever convinced me of the necessity for keeping something ahead so I wouldn’t be forced to go out and take long chances for short money. With enough in my pocket now to last me a month, I gave the town a thorough canvassing for something worth while. I found many places that appeared to be advertising for a burglar, and the most promising was the big general store. It was packed to the roof with merchandise, and the owners, to save floor space, had placed the safe behind stairs, where it could not be seen from the street. I pegged the spot for a week and satisfied myself that after the store was closed at night no one entered it till opening-up time in the morning.
The expression, “I have him pegged,” which has crept into common usage, is thieves’ slang pure and simple, and has nothing to do with the game of cribbage as many suppose. The thief, to save himself the trouble of staying up all night watching a spot to make sure no one enters after closing hours, puts a small wooden peg in the door jamb after the place is locked up. At five or six o’clock in the morning he takes a look. If the peg is in place the door has not been opened. If it is found lying in the doorway, that means somebody has opened the door in the night. If he finds the place is visited in the night he must then stay out and learn why and at what time and how often. He now has the place pegged and plans accordingly or passes it up as too tough.
Dynamite and drills were to be had for the taking at any mine. I invited my friend, the waiter, to come in on the caper, but he declined for the very good reason that he had “done enough time.” Compared with the work I had put in getting the mine payroll this was simple and I went against it alone, confident of success and glad I wouldn’t have to split the money with anybody. The box was of a make that has long been extinct. It was an experiment on the part of the manufacturers, and a costly one, for the box men soon found a fatal weakness in its makeup and hungrily sought them out till the last one went into the junk pile.
The one I had designs on looked more like an old-fashioned clothes closet than a receptacle for money. Its four wheels rested on a heavy wooden platform that served to reinforce the thin floor of the storeroom.

===Chinese===

这枚带记号的银币如此离奇地撞在一起，比先前更叫我相信：手头必须存下点富余，免得被逼得出去为几个小钱冒天大的险。如今口袋里的钱够我撑一个月了，我便把这镇子彻底踩了一遍，找一票值得一干的活儿。我发现许多地方简直像是在登广告招贼，最有指望的是那家大杂货铺。货堆得顶到屋顶，店主为了省地皮，把保险柜放在楼梯背后，街上望不见。我把这地方“下了钉”盯了一个星期，摸清楚每晚铺子关门后，直到第二天早晨开门，没人进去过。
“下了钉”（pegged）这个说法如今已渗进日常用语，其实是地地道道的贼话，同许多人以为的克里比奇牌戏毫无关系。贼为了省得通宵守着一个地方、看关门后有没有人进去，就在那地方锁门之后往门缝里插一根小木钉。清晨五六点钟他来看一眼。木钉还在原处，门就没开过。要是木钉躺在门槛上，就说明夜里有人开过门。如果发现这地方夜里有人来，他就得守在外面，弄清为什么来、几点来、来得多勤。到这一步，这地方就算“下了钉”，他据此定计划，或者嫌太扎手，把它放弃。
炸药和钻头，随便哪家矿上都是伸手可得。我请我那朋友——堂倌——入伙干这一票，他推辞了，理由十分充分：他“坐牢坐够了”。比起我弄矿上薪水那一趟的功夫，这桩简单得多，我便独自一人干了下去，满有把握，又庆幸不必同任何人分钱。那口柜子是一种早已绝了种的货色。那是制造厂家的一次试验，还是一次代价惨重的试验，因为开柜的行家们很快就在它的构造里找出了致命的弱点，饿鹰似的专寻这一种下手，直到最后一口进了废铁堆。
我打主意的那一口，看着与其说像装钱的柜子，不如说像一口老式衣橱。四只轮子架在一座厚木台上，木台正好给储藏室单薄的地板加了固。

===Original===

The work of putting a hole in it, placing the shot, and laying a five-minute fuse took an hour. The man that does this kind of work alone must now take a look at the street to be sure there are no late stragglers around. When he satisfies himself on this point he returns and lights his fuse. While it is burning he goes back to the street some distance away and plants himself in a hall or doorway till he hears the explosion. Then when he is satisfied there is no alarm, he goes after his money.
I was in the door of an all-night saloon when my explosion arrived. Nobody appeared on the street and after a few minutes I went back to the store to finish the business. Inside, I saw that my shot had resulted in something entirely unforeseen. The outer plate of the door was torn from its place and lying to one side, bent and twisted. The force of the explosion had shifted the ponderous box from its platform. It had fallen forward on its face and ten sturdy burglars couldn’t have turned it over. Had it remained upright the money would have been mine in five minutes. Lying on its face, its contents were as safe from me as if they had been in the town bank. The next day a gang of men turned it partly over and one of the clerks finished opening it with a crowbar I left in the store.
The storekeepers said “there was nothing in the safe anyway.” My friend, the waiter, who stood by when it was opened, said they hurried to the bank with a fat package and “what was in it was plenty.”
This failure took the heart out of me for a few days, and I don’t know what depths of despondency I might have wallowed in but for my friend. He suggested, with the best intentions I am sure, that I take a job washing dishes in the place where he worked. This was a jolt to my pride. Of course I had nothing against dishwashers or dishwashing. I saw that any able-bodied dishwasher would have more to show for his ten years’ hard work than I had for mine, and if I had been in the notion of going to work I would have taken that kind of job as quick as any. But the thought of working was as foreign to me as the thought of burglary or robbery would be to a settled printer or plumber after ten years at his trade. I wasn’t lazy or indolent; I knew there were lots of easier and safer ways of making a living, but they were the ways of other people, people I didn’t know or understand, and didn’t want to.

===Chinese===

在柜身上打个洞、装药、接一根五分钟的信管，用了一个钟头。单独一人干这种活的人，此刻得先上街看一眼，确定附近没有夜游的落单客。这一层放心了，他回去点信管。信管烧着的当儿，他退回街上，离得远远的，把自己安顿在门厅或门洞里，直到听见爆炸。然后等他确信没有惊动谁，再回去取钱。
我的爆炸响起时，我正在一家通宵酒馆的门洞里。街上没人露面，过了几分钟，我回铺子去收尾。进去一看，这一炮炸出了个全然没料到的结果。柜门的外层铁板被掀离原位，甩在一边，弯了扭了。爆炸的力道把那口笨重的柜子从木台上掀了下来。它脸朝前扑倒在地，十个壮实的贼也休想把它翻过来。倘若它还立着，五分钟里钱就归我了。如今趴着脸，里头的东西对我而言，同存进了镇上银行一样够不着。第二天，一伙汉子把它半翻了过来，一个店伙用我留在铺子里的一根撬棍把柜门捅开了。
店主们说“保险柜里反正什么也没有”。我那堂倌朋友当时站在旁边看开柜，说他们抱着一个鼓鼓囊囊的包裹直奔银行，“里头装的东西可多着呢”。
这一败叫我有好几天心灰意冷，若不是亏了我那朋友，真不知要陷进多深的沮丧里去。他给我出主意——我相信完全出于好意——叫我到他干活的地方去洗盘子。这于我的自尊是一记闷棍。当然，我对洗盘子的、对洗盘子这行当毫无偏见。我看得出，随便哪个四肢健全的洗碗工，十年苦干下来，落下的东西都比我多；我若当真起了做工的念头，这行我同哪行一样肯干。可“去做工”这个念头之于我，就像“去撬门”“去抢”之于一个安分守己的排字工或管子工——在本行干了十年之后。我不是懒，不是怠惰；我知道世上多的是又轻省又安稳的谋生路子，可那是别人的路子，是我不认识、不明白、也不想去明白的人的路子。

===Original===

I didn’t call them suckers or saps because they were different and worked for a living. They represented society. Society represented law, order, discipline, punishment. Society was a machine geared to grind me to pieces. Society was an enemy. There was a high wall between me and society; a wall reared by myself, maybe﻿—I wasn’t sure. Anyway I wasn’t going to crawl over the wall and join the enemy just because I had taken a few jolts of hard luck.
I did go over the wall in the end and take my hat off to society and admit I was wrong, but I didn’t do it because of discouragement, because I was afraid of the future, because of the police. I didn’t do it because I realized I was wrong; I knew I was wrong years before. I did it because﻿—but that’s a separate story, to be told later, in its place.
So, instead of following the waiter’s advice, I busied myself with a few careful hotel burglaries, got a small bankroll together again, bought some presentable clothes, and kept looking around for a decent piece of money. The booming mining town had its share of gamblers, women of the night, thieves, and hop fiends﻿—nearly all of them renegades or fugitives from the American side. Their leisure time was given to drinking or smoking hop. I had weaned myself from gambling. I was naturally a light drinker. So it fell out that, in this town of no amusements, the hop joint claimed me.
One afternoon as I lay smoking my day’s portion of hop a voice, a woman’s voice, strangely familiar, came to me through a thin partition from the adjoining room. When she finished talking to the Chinese boy that attended the hop layouts, I fell to wondering who the speaker could be. My contact with women had been very limited, and it didn’t take much elimination to fix the voice as that of the street girl of Chicago, the girl from whose crib I had carried the dead man; the girl I avoided because I believed she had carelessly killed him with an overdose of some drug. Just to satisfy myself that I was right, more than anything, I got up and stepped into her room. No mistake; it was she. She knew me instantly and I needed no introduction, no sponsor.
Irish Annie had changed. She was now a well-poised, confident woman. The world had treated her better than it does most of her kind, and yet she was not spoiled.

===Chinese===

我不叫他们冤大头、傻瓜蛋，因为他们同我不一样，靠做工吃饭。他们代表着社会。社会代表着法律、秩序、纪律、惩罚。社会是一架上好了齿轮、专等着碾我成齑粉的机器。社会是敌人。我和社会之间隔着一堵高墙；这墙也许是我自己砌起来的——我说不准。反正我不打算只因为吃了几次倒霉的闷棍，就爬过墙去投奔敌人。
到头来我确实翻了墙，向社会脱了帽，认了自己错——可那不是因为灰心，不是因为怕了将来，也不是因为警察。不是因为明白了错了才这么做；我明白自己错了，早好些年了。我那样做是因为——但那是另一码事，往后到了地方再讲。
于是我没听堂倌的主意，只悄悄做下了几桩小心谨慎的旅馆盗窃，重新攒起一小卷钱，买了几件见得了人的衣裳，继续物色一票像样的钱。这个日渐兴旺的采矿镇上，赌棍、暗娼、小偷、抽大烟（hop）的烟鬼，各色俱全——几乎全是从美国那边逃过来的亡命者或逃犯。他们的闲暇光阴，不是拿去喝酒，就是抽大烟。我早已戒了赌，天生酒量又浅。于是，在这个没有任何娱乐的镇子上，烟馆收留了我。
一天下午，我正躺着抽我每日分内的一口烟，一个女人的嗓音，熟得出奇，隔着薄薄一层板壁从隔壁房间传了过来。她同照管烟榻（hop layout）的中国伙计说完话，我就开始琢磨这说话的是谁。我同女人打过的交道少得很，没怎么费排除法，就认定了：是芝加哥（Chicago）街头那个姑娘，就是我曾从她屋里背出死人的那个姑娘；我一直躲着她，因为我相信是她不小心用药过量害死了他。多半是为了证实自己没认错，我起身进了她的房间。错不了；正是她。她一眼认出了我，用不着介绍，也用不着引荐。
爱尔兰安妮（Irish Annie）变了。如今是个从容镇定、有把握的女人。世界待她，比待她这一行的大多数人都厚，可她并没有因此变坏。

===Original===

She instantly referred to “that awful night,” and sincerely acknowledged the service I had done her. We compared notes roughly. She had left Chicago to avoid going to jail for a theft, and after many hardships and adventures found herself in this British Columbia mining camp. A lucky prospector in the mines, finding himself rich overnight, had bought an establishment for her and she was prospering.
To show her gratitude to me, she gave a blowout at her place, introduced me to her girls as “an old friend from the States,” set a room apart for me, and insisted upon me making her home my home. The Chinese boy brought meals to my room, a hop layout was procured so I could smoke in peace and security. My room was more comfortable than any I could have had in a hotel, the meals were better than at the restaurants, I was treated with deference by everybody in the place, so I remained there. I paid the rent on rent day, ordered the liquors and provisions, and slipped the town marshal his “once a week.” The Tenderloin, the marshal, and his deputies accepted me as Annie’s protector and the man about the place﻿—something I was not and did not aspire to be. I didn’t take the trouble to enlighten them. I preferred to have them believe anything rather than the truth. This would make my stealing easier.
This soft life that loomed before me in Irish Annie’s had about as much lure for me as the dishwashing job. Without taking her into my confidence, I went on with my burgling. That was my trade and I would not leave it for a job in a restaurant, a “job” in Annie’s, or a position in a bank.
Winter came with a rush and a roar. I had thought of getting away from the North, but it came before I was prepared and there was nothing to do but stick it out with the others.
Of all the gamblers that found their way into this camp “Swede Pete” was probably the cleverest. I knew him from towns on the American side, where he played in all the big poker games with more or less success. He submitted a scheme to me that looked very good. His plan was to buy a box of playing cards, mark each deck, and have me, as a capable burglar, go into one of the big poker rooms, open the card locker, and substitute his box of marked cards for a box of legitimate cards belonging to the house. I looked at the place and saw my end of the business would be simple.

===Chinese===

她立刻提起“那可怕的一夜”，诚心诚意地认了我那回帮她的忙。我们大略交换了各自的经历。她为了一桩偷窃官司离开芝加哥，免得坐牢，历尽艰辛波折，落脚在这个不列颠哥伦比亚的矿营。矿上一个走运的探矿人一夜暴富，给她置下一处宅子，她过得兴旺。
为报我的恩，她在她那儿摆了一场大宴，把我说成“美国来的老朋友”介绍给她的姑娘们，腾出一间房给我，硬要我拿她的家当我的家。中国伙计把饭送到我房里，又置办了一副烟具，好让我安安稳稳地过瘾。我的房间比哪家旅馆都舒坦，饭菜比馆子里的强，那宅子里上上下下都敬着我，我就住下了。交房租的日子我交房租，酒水杂用由我点单，每礼拜照例塞给镇警长他那一份“周例”。田德隆区（the Tenderloin）、警长和他那班副手，都把我当作安妮的保护人和宅子里的当家男人——这我既不是，也不想当。我也懒得给他们点破。我宁肯让他们信随便什么，也别信实话。这样我下手偷更方便。
在爱尔兰安妮这里向我招手的这份软绵绵的日子，同洗盘子的活儿一样引不起我的兴致。我没对她交底，照旧干我的撬门买卖。那是我的行当，我不会为了饭馆里的一个差事、安妮宅子里的一个“差事”、或者银行里的一分职位丢了它。
冬天轰隆隆地一下就来了。我原想着离开北方，可它来得比我的准备还快，只好同大家一齐硬熬。
在钻进这矿营的赌棍里头，“瑞典佬”皮特（“Swede Pete”）大概要数最灵的。在美国那边的镇子上我就认得他，他在各大牌局里进进出出，输赢不定。他向我提了一个看着很妙的计策。他的计划是：买一盒扑克牌，把每一副都做上记号，再由我这个有本事的贼，摸进一家大牌室，撬开牌柜，用他那盒做了记号的牌，换掉柜里店家的正路牌。我去那地方看了看，我这一头的活计简单得很。

===Original===

He went to work on his cards, and after many days and nights of patient toil put his “work” on them so he could read them from the back as easily as from the front. When he had replaced the seals on each deck and on the box that contained them, he put them in my hands and it was but an hour’s work for me to put them in the locker and return to him the full box that his had replaced.
The following night he sat in the game as usual. Whenever he lost a pot he threw the unlucky deck on the floor and ordered a fresh one. After a night or two of this, the gamekeeper had to open a fresh box. This was our box and Pete’s luck changed at once. In a couple of nights’ play he got the long end of the money in the game. From what I heard and saw I estimated his winnings, or stealings, at three thousand dollars. It was understood that the money should be split evenly between us. When he gave me my end of it he declared he had won but fifteen hundred and that he had to have a man in the game with him and the money had to go three ways. I took the five hundred dollars knowing I was getting the worst of it and wondering how I could get even.
Pete was a big, six-foot, blond giant, good-natured, generous, and with a laugh you could hear a block. Everybody liked him; he was a good spender and a good loser. He carried a bankroll of several thousand dollars, as I knew, but the thought of robbing him never entered my mind till he burnt me in the marked-card transaction. He was interested in a saloon and had a game of his own in a rear room. I watched him carefully and learned that when the game and the bar were closed for the night he put his bankroll in the cash register, locked it, and went to bed in a comfortable little room that adjoined the big barroom. Apparently his money was safe. He left his room door open; the register could not be opened without waking him.
After thinking the matter over pro and con, I decided to beat off the barroom, carry the register out and into the alley and smash it open with an ax. Every night for a week I watched him from across the street and saw him make up his cash for the day, then put his own money in the register, lock it carefully, and go into his bedroom. Satisfied on this point, I now kept away from his place so as not to be too fresh in his mind on the morning after I got his money.

===Chinese===

他动手做他的牌。熬了许多天许多夜的耐心功夫，他的“活儿”做上了，从背面认这些牌，同从正面认一样容易。他把每一副牌、连同装牌的盒子重新封好火漆，交到我手里；我只消一个钟头，就把它们安进牌柜，把他那盒顶替下来的原封整盒带还给他。
第二天夜里，他照常坐进牌局。只要输了一墩，他就把那副倒霉牌摔在地上，叫换新的。这样闹了一两夜，管牌的只好开一盒新牌。开的就是我们那盒，皮特的运气当场转了。两三夜的工夫，他把牌局里的大头钱赢到了手。照我听到看到的估计，他的赢头——说偷头更准——有三千美元。讲好对半分。到他给我分钱时，他咬定只赢了一千五，还说什么牌局里得有个搭子，钱得三份分。我接过那五百美元，明知吃了大亏，心里盘算着怎么扳回这一局。
皮特是个六英尺的大个子金发巨汉，好脾气，出手大方，笑声隔一条街都听得见。人人都喜欢他；他赢得起也输得起。据我所知，他随身带着好几千的票子，可在做牌这桩交易里他黑了我之前，抢他这个念头压根没进过我的脑子。他入伙着一家酒馆，后屋开着他自己的牌局。我仔细踩看他，摸清了每晚牌局和酒柜一打烊，他就把钱卷放进收银机，锁好，到挨着大酒室的那间舒服小屋里去睡。表面上他的钱万无一失。他房门敞着；收银机一动就会吵醒他。
反复掂量之后，我决定撬开酒室，把收银机搬出去弄进后巷，用斧子劈开。整整一个礼拜，我每夜从街对面盯他，看他结当日账，把自己的钱放进收银机，仔细锁好，进卧室去。这一层放心了，我就不再上他那儿露面，免得在我得手之后的那个早晨，我在他记忆里还太新鲜。

===Original===

At last a stormy night came that drove everybody off the street. I kept out of the neighborhood of Pete’s place till four o’clock in the morning. I had no trouble getting into the barroom through a door opening into the hall that separated the saloon from the hotel office. Carefully removing a lot of glasses from around the register, I lifted it and placed it on the bar. Then I had to go around the bar, pick it up again, and carry it out into the hall where I put it on a table while I went back to close the door. All the while Swede Pete was snoring like a horse.
Lifting the heavy weight again, I made my way slowly to the sidewalk. Groping along in the blinding storm of snow and sleet, I missed my footing and fell flat on my back with the heavy register fair on my chest. Its sharp edge dug into my ribs and although I never went to a doctor, I believe to this day a couple of them were cracked or splintered. Unable to lift it again, I tied my handkerchief in the grill work at the top of the thing and painfully dragged it to an opening between two buildings and down into the alley where I had planted an ax.
I was morally certain Pete’s money was in it. I saw him put it there the night before, but on this last night I had kept away, not caring to be seen about his place. There was one chance in a million that he hadn’t locked the register, so before attacking it with the ax I touched one of the keys. The bell did not ring, but the cash drawer opened. But to my dismay it did not slide out with the slow, labored, obese movement of a cash drawer loaded with heavy gold and silver; instead it shot out with a thin, empty, hollow jerk that told me there was nothing in it.
With numb and freezing fingers I explored the little cups only to find them bare of coin. The compartments in the back gave up no fat bankroll. The thing was as empty and as inviting as a new-dug grave. This last blow was too much for my philosophy. Cursing the snow and sleet and every organic thing, I started back determined to go into Pete’s bedroom and search for his money. I was too late. At the door I looked into the hall and saw the porter limbering up for another day’s work.
Sad and sore I turned away and trudged through the storm to my room at Irish Annie’s where I found food and drink and light and heat and the consoling hop layout.

===Chinese===

总算来了一个暴风雪的夜晚，街上一个人不剩。我在皮特那地方的左近一直躲到凌晨四点。从通着酒馆与旅馆账房之间过道的门进酒室，毫不费事。我小心地把收银机四周的玻璃杯一只只挪开，把它抬起来搁上酒柜。随后又得绕过柜子，再抱起它，搬进过道，放在一张桌上，回身去关门。这半天里，瑞典佬皮特一直打鼾打得像匹马。
再抱起这沉重的家伙，我慢慢挪到人行道上。在雪珠子迷眼的暴风雪里摸索着走，一脚踩空，仰面摔倒，那台沉重的收银机不偏不倚砸在我胸口。它的利边嵌进我的肋骨；虽说我从没去看过医生，到今天我还信，有两三根肋骨是裂了或是折了茬。再抱不动它了，我把手帕拴在它顶上的格栅上，忍着疼把它拖进两栋房子之间的豁口，拖下后巷，那里我预先埋了一把斧子。
我十拿九稳皮特的钱在里头。前一夜我亲眼看他放进去的；只是这最后一夜我没去盯，不想在他那地方被人瞧见。万中有一的机会他没锁收银机，所以在抡斧子之前，我碰了碰其中一个键。铃没响，钱屉却开了。可叫我丧气的是，它没有像一只装满金银的抽屉那样，缓慢、吃力、臃肿地滑出来；它是那么薄薄地、空空地、哐当一声弹出来的——里头什么也没有。
我用冻僵的手指头把那些小格挨个摸遍，一钱不剩。后头的隔层里也没有鼓鼓的钱卷。那玩意儿空得、也凉得像一口新掘的坟。这最后一击，我的哲学也撑不住了。我咒骂这雪、这冻雨，咒骂一切有机物，转身往回走，打定主意进皮特的卧室去搜他的钱。晚了。在门口我朝过道里一望，那打杂的正活动着筋骨，预备开始又一天的活儿。
我又疼又丧，转身在暴风雪里一步步蹚回爱尔兰安妮家的我那间屋。那里有吃有喝，有灯火有炉火，还有那副能安慰人的烟具。

===Original===

I was out early the next afternoon to find out what had happened at Swede Pete’s that caused him to shift his money. He was around town drunk as a ragman, going from one bar to another, buying drinks for all hands, and telling with great relish how he had saved his bankroll from the “burglars.” I heard his boisterous laugh as I was passing one of the saloons and went in to get an earful. He threw an American twenty-dollar piece on the bar, and as it bounced up and down, roared: “Yump, you beggar, yump! Many times I ha’ to yump for you.” Then everybody was invited to drink and listen to his tale.
I gathered from it that his cash register “bane on the bum.” Something got wrong with its locking arrangement the night before, and Pete took his money to bed with him. “Ay take the old bankroll and throw her under the mattress and lay my two hundred pounds Swede beef on her,” he finished with a roar. Then, bouncing another gold piece on the bar, he ordered it to “yump” and the house to drink. If he suspected me he didn’t show it, and I went out not relishing his talk or his liquor. I thought of sticking him up, but he knew my voice and that wouldn’t do. I thought of chloroform, but my awful experience with the Chinamen warned me against it. I had no desire to get tangled up with Pete’s “two hundred pounds Swede beef” that wasn’t beef at all, but bone and muscle he put on in his youth as a laborer in the Northwest lumber camps. After much thought, I let him go and gave him up as a total loss.
I don’t know what the statistics show, but I should say that for every five hundred burglaries one burglar gets arrested. On the other hand, my experiences showed that if the burglar gets what he is after one time in five he is lucky.
After my bad luck with the big Swede I sat down and gave my system of stealing a good overhauling. As near as I could calculate ten thousand dollars had slipped out of my hands since I left Los Angeles. I wasn’t satisfied to put this down to tough luck entirely. I saw that carelessness had something to do with it. I should have planted the mine payroll in a safer place. A more careful and experienced “blacksmith” would have taken measures to prevent that big safe from falling on its face and burying the money beyond reach, and in the matter of Swede Pete I was careless and overconfident, in not checking on him the night I went after his money.

===Chinese===

第二天下午我一早就出门，去打听瑞典佬皮特那儿出了什么事，害他挪了钱。他正满镇子烂醉如泥，从一家酒馆喝到另一家，见人就请，眉飞色舞地讲他怎样从“贼骨头”手里保住了他的钱卷。我路过一家酒馆听见他那炸雷似的笑，便进去听了满耳朵。他把一枚美国二十元金币抛在柜上，看它一蹦一蹦，吼道：“跳啊，你这要饭的，跳！多少回俺为你跳来着。”于是满屋子的人都有酒喝，都听他的故事。
我听出来，是他的收银机“坏了事”。前一夜它的锁出了毛病，皮特就把钱带上床睡了。“俺把钱卷往褥子底下一塞，再拿俺这两百磅瑞典牛肉往上一压，”他吼着一收尾。接着又抛一枚金币在柜上叫它“跳”，叫满屋子人喝酒。他若疑到我头上，可没露出来；我出了门，他那套话和他那家的酒都叫我倒胃口。我想到过蒙面抢他，可他认得我的嗓音，使不得。我想到过氯仿，可我对那几个中国人的可怕经历是前车之鉴。他那“两百磅瑞典牛肉”——其实根本不是牛肉，是他年轻时在西北伐木营里当苦力攒下的骨头和筋肉——我没兴趣去招惹。左思右想，我放过他，把他记作一笔勾销的总损失。
统计数字怎么说我不知道，可依我看，每五百桩盗窃，才有一个贼落网。反过来，我的经历表明：贼若五次里有一次得手，就算走运。
在大个瑞典人手里栽了这一跤之后，我坐下来，把我这套偷的章法好生检修了一遍。粗粗一算，离开洛杉矶以来，已有一万美元从我指缝里溜走。我不肯把这全记在运气坏上。我看出了粗心大意也有份。矿上那笔薪水，我该埋在更稳当的地方。一个更细心、更老到的“铁匠”——开柜的行家——本该想法子防着那口大柜子扑倒、把钱埋到够不着的地方；而在瑞典佬皮特这件事上，我既大意又自信过头，动手那夜没先去核实他。

===Original===

Figuring that my luck was about due to change for the better, I resolved to pull myself together, tighten up my system, and look around me for something else.
If business houses took as much precaution in protecting themselves from thieves as thieves do in protecting themselves from the police, the business of burglary and robbery would reach the vanishing point in no time. Thieves are occasionally careless; businessmen are habitually so; both pay for their carelessness sooner or later.
In looking about for some worthwhile endeavor, I came upon an instance of carelessness on the part of a businessman that I never saw equaled in all my years of stealing. It cost him a small fortune in diamonds and sent him into the bankruptcy court. The Christmas holidays were coming on, and the jewelry store in this mining camp spread out a display of stones that would have done credit to any large city. I was naturally attracted; and began investigating the jeweler’s system. I found that he slept on the premises in a room back of his storeroom. He testified in court later that he carried no burglary insurance, that he could not get fire insurance because the town had no fire department, and that he slept in his store to protect himself in case of fire or attempted theft. I watched him closely through every business hour of the day﻿—from seven to eight o’clock one day, from eight to nine the next, from nine to ten the next﻿—and so on through the entire day from his opening hour till he closed at night. I found that he left his store in darkness to save on the light bill, and to my amazement I also found that, instead of locking his safe carefully and completely at night, he gave the combination knob only a quarter turn, leaving it “on the quarter,” or only partly locked and at the mercy of anyone with a working knowledge of combination locks.
This putting out of the lights and careless locking of his safe was entirely due to a feeling of security because he slept in his place of business.
After gathering every scrap of information available, I was sure I could “take” the spot if I got a fair break on the luck. The rear room of the place was used as a workshop. This made it possible to enter the back door without waking the owner, who slept in a small room between the shop and the big front storeroom. When I decided to go against this thing, I sat down and looked far ahead.

===Chinese===

估摸着我的运气该时来运转了，我打起精神，把章法收紧，四下里另寻目标。
倘若商号们防贼，能像贼们防警察那样下功夫，盗窃与抢劫这行买卖不出多久就得绝迹。贼偶尔大意；生意人却是一贯大意；迟早都要为大意付账。
在寻觅值得下手的目标时，我撞见一桩生意人的大意事，我偷了这些年，再没见过能同它比的。那一票叫他赔上一小笔钻石财，把他送进了破产法院。圣诞节假日眼看要到，这矿营的珠宝店铺陈出一柜一柜的宝石，摆到大城市里也不丢脸。我自然被吸引住了，着手摸这珠宝商的章程。我探明他就睡在店堂里，货房后头一间屋里。他后来在法庭上作证说，他没保盗窃险；镇上没有消防队，火险也保不上；他睡在店里，就是为了万一失火或有人行窃好照应。我把他的每一个营业钟头都盯了个透——今天盯七点到八点，明天八点到九点，后天九点到十点——就这样从开门盯到夜里打烊。我发现他为了省电费，店里一片漆黑；更叫我瞠目的是，夜里他锁保险柜并不上足，只把暗码旋钮拧四分之一圈，“停在四分之一上”——等于只锁了一半，随哪个懂暗码锁的人摆布。
熄灯也罢，马虎锁柜也罢，全是因为他睡在自己买卖里，那份安全感使然。
把每一星半点的情况都摸齐之后，我确信，只要运气别太亏待我，这地方我能“吃”下。店的后屋是作坊，这就有可能从后门进去而不吵醒店主，他睡在作坊与前头大货房之间的小屋里。我决意吃这一票时，坐下来朝远处看了又看。

===Original===

The stones would have to go to the American side to be disposed of in some big city. I decided on San Francisco and planned my route there. Irish Annie was no small problem. I had no intention of confiding in her or giving her any part of the junk, if I got it. I was under no obligation to her. Our relations were just those of two social outcasts, thrown together by chance, and our parting could mean nothing to either of us. To disarm any suspicions she might have later on, I told her I planned to go to Carson City, Nevada, for the big fight between Fitzsimmons and Corbett. This would give me a reasonable excuse for leaving after the burglary.
With the five hundred dollars I got from Swede Pete and more I had picked up around the hotels, I was amply supplied with expense money and had no worry on that point. Two weeks ahead I bought an old Indian cayuse and a cheap secondhand saddle and bridle, planning to ride across the boundary line fifteen miles distant, plant the junk, and return before the burglary was discovered.
The old horse was staked out in a corral where I could get him at any time. I even rode to the American side, crossing the Columbia River, which marked the boundary, on the ice, and picked out a place to plant my stuff. A week ahead I rented a front room in a lodging house across the street and from my window watched every move in the store every evening. I saw the boss go out to dinner, leaving the place in charge of his clerk and an apprentice boy. I saw his man in the repair shop go out and away. I saw the boy go home after the boss returned, and later saw the clerk depart. An hour after, the owner began gathering the most valuable articles from show window and show cases, placing them in specially made trays and boxes that fitted snugly in the safe. Then the big door swung shut, and a quarter turn of the knob locked it so that it could be opened quickly in the morning by the lazy, careless owner, or just as quickly at night by an industrious, careful burglar.
My mistake in not checking Swede Pete the night before was not repeated here. When the final night came I stood in the snowstorm outside the window, cap pulled down and overcoat buttoned up, looking carelessly at the cheap articles he left there overnight.

===Chinese===

宝石得弄到美国那边的什么大城市去脱手。我定下旧金山（San Francisco），把路线都规划好了。爱尔兰安妮是一桩不小的麻烦。我无意对她交底，也不想分她任何赃物——倘若我到手的话。我于她并无亏欠。我们的交情，不过是两个被社会抛出去的人萍水相逢，分开对谁都不意味着什么。为了打消她日后可能起的疑心，我告诉她，我打算去内华达的卡森城，看菲茨西蒙斯对科贝特的那场大赛拳。这样一来，盗窃之后我离开此地，就有了说得通的借口。
拿着瑞典佬皮特那五百美元，加上旅馆里零敲碎打弄来的，我的盘缠绰绰有余，这一层毫无烦扰。提前两礼拜，我买了一匹印第安老矮马，配了一副便宜的二手鞍辔，打算骑马越过十五英里外的边界，把赃物理进窝儿（plant），再赶在盗窃事发之前回来。
老马拴在一处围栏里，我随时可以取用。我甚至骑到美国那边去了一趟，踏着冰过了作为界河的哥伦比亚河，预先挑好了藏东西的地方。提前一礼拜，我在街对面一家公寓租了间临街屋，从窗口夜夜盯店里的一举一动。我看见老板出门吃晚饭，把店交给店伙和一个学徒小子。我看见修表间的师傅出门走远。我看见老板回来后小子回了家，随后店伙也走了。一个钟头后，店主开始把橱窗和柜子里最值钱的货色收拢起来，装进特制的托盘和匣子，严丝合缝码进保险柜。然后大柜门一合，旋钮四分之一圈就算锁上——早晨那又懒又马虎的店主开起来快，夜里哪个勤快仔细的贼开起来也一样快。
我在瑞典佬皮特身上犯的没核实的错，这里没有重犯。最后那夜到来时，我站在窗外风雪里，帽檐压低，大衣扣紧，漫不经心地望着他留在那里过夜的那些便宜货。

===Original===

When he locked his safe as usual, I went back to my room across the street and saw him secure his front door, put out the lights, and go back into his bedroom.
At one o’clock I was at the back door of the store and after a few minutes of the most careful work I stood in the warm workshop where a big stove still glowed in the dark. The doors inside were open to allow the warm air passage into the sleeper’s room and the front room beyond. I had all the luck at last.
There was no serious obstacle. The sleeper slept on. The safe door opened as easy for me as for him. The inside of the safe was like a beehive﻿—fifty watches, wound up, ticked noisily. Some years ago jewelers thought watches should be kept running all or part of the time to insure perfection. I believe this is no longer done. For fear their ticking should wake the sleeper when I passed through his room on my way out, I wrapped them in their box in my overcoat. Taking nothing else except some gold rings, all the stones, and what money was in the cash drawer, I closed the safe, went back out the rear door and, closing it carefully, departed unseen.
All my junk went into a grain bag at the corral, where I kept the old cayuse. He was gentle as a dog, but the ticking of the watches almost drove him frantic. He reared and pawed and snorted in fear. I couldn’t get into the saddle, and had to snub him up to a tree where, for ten or fifteen minutes, I let him listen to the ticks and get over his fright. At last he cooled off and allowed me to mount him and turn his head south toward the line. Riding away, I looked back over the night’s work and thought with satisfaction that no human being could possibly suspect me of it.
In the small town across the line I planted the stones and cash carefully in the plant I had prepared, but put the watches in another place where they could tick themselves out in security. At seven o’clock in the morning the faithful old horse was back in the corral, well fed and rubbed down, and I was in my room at Irish Annie’s. In the afternoon she came in with the small town “Extra” paper. I saw that this burglary, one of the simplest and easiest of my life, was by far the most profitable. Diamonds valued at twenty thousand dollars, wholesale price, fifty watches, five hundred dollars cash, and a parcel of gold wedding rings roughly outlined the loss.

===Chinese===

他照常锁了柜，我回街对面的屋里，看着他闩好店门，熄了灯，回卧室去了。
一点钟，我到了店后门。几分钟最小心不过的活计之后，我已站在暖烘烘的作坊里，一只大炉子还在黑暗中发着红光。里头的门都敞着，好让暖空气流进睡觉人的屋子，再流进前头的货房。我的运气到底来了。
没有像样的障碍。睡觉的照睡。柜门为我开得同为他开一样容易。柜子里活像一窝蜂——五十只上足了弦的表，滴答滴答吵成一片。前些年珠宝商们相信，表得一直或时常走着，才保得住完美。我想如今不那么干了。怕的是我出来路过他屋子时，滴答声吵醒睡觉人，我把表连盒子裹进了我的大衣。除了一些金戒指、全部宝石、钱屉里的现钱，别的什么也不拿。我合上柜门，从后门退出去，仔细带上门，无人觉察地走了。
我全部的赃物装进一条粮袋，搁在拴老矮马的围栏边。它驯得像条狗，可怀表的滴答几乎把它逼疯。它又炮蹶子又刨地，惊得直打响鼻。我上不了鞍，只好把它拴在一棵树上，让它听了十到十五分钟的滴答，把惊吓听过去。它总算静下来，由我骑上，掉头朝南往界线去。一路走，我回想这一夜的活计，心满意足地想：没有任何人可能疑到我头上。
在界线那头的小镇，我把宝石和现钱仔细埋进我预备好的地方，又把表另放一处，让它们安安稳稳地把弦走完。早晨七点，那匹忠心的老马回了围栏，喂饱了，也擦过了；而我已到了爱尔兰安妮家的我那间屋里。下午她拿着小镇的“号外”进来。我一看，这一票——我平生最简单省事的盗窃之一——偏偏是最肥的。批发价估两万美元的钻石、五十只表、五百美元现钱，外加一小包黄金结婚戒指，大致框出了损失。

===Original===

I immediately pooh-poohed the business to her, telling her I knew enough about burglary to see that it was an inside job and that it was done by the storekeeper to beat his creditors. She believed me and no suspicion whatever found lodgment in her mind.
The next day’s paper questioned the burglary. It was hard to believe that any sane man could be guilty of such carelessness as the jeweler frankly admitted. He also admitted that the stock was taken on consignment, that the stones were not paid for, and that if they were not recovered he would be broke and bankrupted. The town was divided as to whether he had robbed himself, and the marshal and his deputies remained dormant.
I paid another week’s board for the old horse, and another week’s rent for the room opposite the jeweler’s. I had no use for them anymore, but thought it safest not to give either of them up too soon and chance arousing suspicion.
After a restless month I said goodbye to Annie and to the Canadian side. Leaving the watches and rings where they were, I dug up the plant of stones and cash, and went into Spokane, where I threw away my good clothes, put on overalls, a mackinaw coat, a lumberjack’s cap, and bought a cheap ticket to Seattle. There I changed again, buying an expensive outfit of clothes and other things necessary for the traveler. Three days later I was in San Francisco, safe, secure, and unsuspected.
My first act was to put the stones in a safety box, tear up the receipt, and plant the key in a safe, convenient place. Then began the toughest part of the business, getting the stones off my hands and into cash. Day after day I sorted out the larger ones and in my room unharnessed them from their settings. Other articles, clusters and sunbursts, were left intact in their settings﻿—to remove them would depreciate the value. I had many of the best stones reset and sold them for fair prices openly to bookmakers, prize fighters, jockeys, gamblers, and women about town. My money went into the bank, and for the first time in my life I carried a check book.
I was careful, kept clean and sober and away from the hop joints and thieves’ hangouts. For once in my life I managed to get a fair price from pawnshops for some of my junk.

===Chinese===

我立刻对她把这桩事说得一钱不值，告诉她，讲到偷我懂得够多，一看便知这是内鬼干的，是店主自己干的，好赖掉债主。她信了，脑子里没落下丝毫疑影。
第二天的报纸对这桩盗窃提出了疑问。一个神志清醒的人，竟能粗心到珠宝商自己都痛快承认的那个地步，叫人难以相信。他还承认，货是代销的，宝石没付过钱；找不回来，他就得精光、破产。全镇分成了两派，争的是他是不是自己偷了自己；警长同他的副手们按兵不动。
我又替老马付了一礼拜的草料钱，又替珠宝店对面的房间付了一礼拜租金。这两样我都用不着了，但觉得还是别太早退掉的好，免得惹出疑心。
熬过焦灼的一个月，我辞别了安妮，辞别了加拿大那一边。表和戒指留在原处，我起出埋着的宝石和现钱，进了斯波坎（Spokane）。在那里，我扔掉好衣裳，换上工装裤、伐木布袄、伐木工小帽，买了一张去西雅图的便宜车票。到了西雅图再改扮一回，置办了一身体面的行头和出门人要用的零七八碎。三天之后，我到了旧金山，安全、稳妥、无人怀疑。
头一件事是把宝石存进银行保险箱，把收据撕掉，钥匙埋在一个稳当方便的地方。随后开始了这桩买卖最棘手的部分：把宝石脱手成现钱。日复一日，我挑出大颗的，在屋里把它们从托座上卸下来。另一些货色，成簇的、太阳针形的，原托座不动——卸下来要掉价。我把许多最好的石头重新镶过，大大方方卖给赌马经纪人、拳击手、骑师、赌棍和风尘女人，价钱公道。我的钱进了银行；平生头一回，我随身带上了支票簿。
我处处小心，保持干净清醒，不沾烟馆和贼的落脚点。平生头一遭，我拿几件东西进当铺，得了公道价。

===Original===

Taking one of the reset rings that was perfectly safe and impossible of identification, I would step into the pawnbroker’s at lunch time and always when there were other patrons in his place. The average thief is duck soup for the hockshop man. He will walk by the hockshop and look in. The hockshop man sees him and knows he has something “hot,” or crooked. If there is anybody in the place but the employees, the thief waits till they go out before going in. This convinces the pawnbroker he has a thief to deal with and he offers him half what he would offer an honest man with a legitimate article.
Instead of sneaking into a hockshop, taking a ring out of my pocket, and saying, “How much can I get on this?” I walked in confidently, held out my finger with the ring on, and said: “I want to pledge this ring for one hundred dollars till pay day. My name is so-and-so. I work for such and such a firm. I lost some of my employer’s money at the racetrack and must have it tonight.”
I always asked for a sum far in excess of what I expected, but that served to convince the pawnbroker I knew nothing about pawning things, that I was honest, that the ring was mine and that I would probably redeem it. After inspecting it, he would offer me much more than if he thought it was crooked. If it was redeemed he would get his big interest, and if not he would still be safely below the wholesale price, which is the dead line for him.
In one way and another I unloaded most of my stones to advantage. I could go about with them one at a time in safety; they were impossible of identification when out of their original settings. While I was trying to find some safe way of selling the pieces I had left in their settings, I met by chance a young chap I had known on the road. He had settled down, got married, and was making a semi-legitimate and uncertain living gambling. He was square enough, and I arranged with him to sell them for me. He was in San Francisco when the burglary was committed and was in no danger of being charged with it if he did get arrested.
He got rid of them quickly to his friends in the Tenderloin and to small pawnshops, getting a price that satisfied me and left him a good profit. I cleaned everything up and quit with eight thousand dollars in the bank and several very nice stones that I wanted to keep.

===Chinese===

拿一枚重新镶过的、绝对安全、无从指认的戒指，我总在午饭时分踏进当铺，而且专挑铺子里另有主顾的时候。寻常的贼，在当铺老板眼里就是一碗现成饭。他从当铺门口过，朝里张一张。当铺老板一眼看见他，就知道他手里有“烫手的”、来路不正的东西。铺子里只要还有外人，贼就等他们走了才进去。这倒叫当铺老板认准了来的是贼，出的价便只有对诚实人、对来路正派物件的一半。
我不偷偷摸摸进当铺、从口袋里抠出一枚戒指问“这个能当多少”，而是大大方方走进去，伸出戴戒指的那根手指，说：“我想把这枚戒指当一百美元，发了工资就来赎。我姓甚名谁，在哪家字号做工。我在赛马场丢了东家的一点钱，今晚非补上不可。”
我要的数目总比我预期的高出一大截，可这正叫当铺老板相信：我全然不懂当东西的行情，我是诚实人，戒指是我自己的，而且多半会来赎。他验看之后，开的价比他把这当贼货时高出许多。赎了，他得他那笔重利；不赎，他也稳稳落在批发价之下——那是他的死线。
就这样七拐八绕，我把大部分宝石卖了个好价钱。我可以揣上一件、一次一件地四处走动，稳稳当当；它们一离开原来的托座，就无从指认。正当我还在给那些连托座处理的货色找稳妥出路时，我碰巧遇上一个当年在道上认识的年轻人。他已安顿下来，结了婚，靠赌博过一种半正经、没准头的日子。人还实诚，我同他说定由他替我卖。案发时他在旧金山，即便被抓，也没有被算作同案的险。
他把货很快出脱给了他在田德隆区的朋友和小当铺，价钱叫我满意，他自己也落了可观的利。我把一切收拾干净，收山时银行里存着八千美元，手里还留着几颗我舍不得的好石头。

===Original===

Of late I had thought of buying a saloon sometime and leaving the burglary business. Now, as I looked over the small dives and joints with their hangers-around, their discordant pianos and beery-voiced singers, and drunken, bedraggled women, I found they had no attraction. Now that I could have one of those places I didn’t want it.
The notion of going into any decent business never occurred to me. Without any definite notion of what to do, I settled down to have a few months of ease and relaxation. The racetracks and gambling houses were running wide open, but I kept away from them and didn’t get hurt. The wine dumps, the Coast, Chinatown, and the dingy dives that fascinated me when I first saw them, no longer held anything of interest.
I’m not finding fault with these brave days of jungle music, synthetic liquor, and dimple-kneed maids, and anybody that thinks the world is going to the bowwows because of them ought to think back to San Francisco or any other big city of twenty years ago﻿—when train conductors steered suckers against the bunko men; when coppers located “work” for burglars and stalled for them while they worked; when pickpockets paid the police so much a day for “exclusive privileges” and had to put a substitute “mob” in their district if they wanted to go out of town to a country fair for a week. Those were the days when there were saloons by the thousand; when the saloonkeeper ordered the police to pinch the Salvation Army for disturbing his peace by singing hymns in the street; when there were racetracks, gambling unrestricted, crooked prize fights; when there were cribs by the mile and hop joints by the score. These things may exist now, but if they do, I don’t know where. I knew where they were then, and with plenty of money and leisure I did them all.

===Chinese===

近来我常想，什么时候盘下一家酒馆，收了这撬门的行当。可如今我把那些小酒窟、小馆子连同围着它们转的闲人、走调的钢琴、满口酒气的歌女、醉醺醺蓬头垢面的女人打量一遍，发觉它们对我毫无吸引力了。如今买得起这种地方了，反倒不想要了。
进什么正经行当的念头，压根没起过。要说做什么，也没有个准主意，我便安顿下来，清闲松快地过他几个月。赛马场和赌场都敞着大门大干，我躲得远远的，没吃亏。那些卖散装酒的酒肆、海滨游乐场、唐人街，还有我初来时迷过眼的那些昏暗小窟，都不再有什么意思。
我并非要挑剔这些满耳丛林音乐（爵士乐）、满杯人造假酒、满台酒窝膝小姐的勇敢日子；谁要是以为世道就此完蛋，倒该回想一下二十年前的旧金山，或随便哪座大城——那时列车长专把冤大头往骗子堆里领；那时条子替贼“找活”，贼干活时替他们望风；那时扒手按天给警察交“专营费”，想出城赶一礼拜的乡村集市，还得在自己地段里搁一拨替班的“伙”。那才是千家酒馆林立的年月；酒馆老板一声令下，警察就得去“捏”（pinch）在街上唱赞美诗搅了他清静的救世军；那时赛马场敞开、赌不受限、拳赛做假；那时妓寮论英里、烟馆论打数。这些东西如今兴许还有，可真有的话，我也不知道在哪儿。那时候我可都知道；钱又多，闲又长，我一样样全试过来了。

## XXII / 二十二
===Original===

The young fellow that helped me dispose of the stones was the wayward son of a fine family and it would not be right to them to use his name. I will call him “Spokane,” the monoger he was known by among his associates. He had been in San Francisco for years and was familiar with an underworld that I had seen very little of. Most of my life had been spent on the road or roughing it in out-of-the-way places, broken by a few months now and then in city slums. He introduced me into the elegant hop joints where we smoked daily, into the hangouts of polished bunko men and clever pickpockets, into the gilded cafés and other exclusive and refined places of entertainment.
I grew tired of this life after a few months and suggested to Spokane that he go up to the Canadian line and lift the plant of watches and rings. The burglary cry had died out and I had arranged to sell them all where they would never rise up to accuse me. This trip suited him, as it would give him a chance to visit his people in Spokane, Washington, and it pleased me to have him go because I didn’t want to take chances with the watches which were all numbered and easy of identification. I had left them behind me rather than carry the bulky package around and chance losing the more valuable stones because of it. I gave him a diagram of the spot where the plant was, supplied him with tickets and traveling expenses, and sent him on his way with instructions to express the parcel to the San Francisco express office, value it at twenty-five dollars, and tell the express agent it contained samples of ore.
To make everything safe and sure, I further instructed him to stand at the depot when the train was in and make sure the parcel went into the express car, then to take a train the next day and come back to San Francisco by an indirect route which would protect him in case anything happened to the package en route. I put in a couple of anxious weeks, but he showed up in due time with the good news that he had located the junk without trouble and sent it along exactly as he had promised. He called for it at the express office the next day, but it hadn’t arrived. Not caring to make too much fuss over a package valued at twenty-five dollars, we waited a few days before he went back for it.

===Chinese===

帮我出手那些宝石的年轻人是个好人家的浪荡子，用他的真名对他的家人不公平。我就叫他“斯波坎（Spokane）”，伙伴们中间都拿这个诨名（monoger）叫他。他在旧金山待了多年，熟悉一个我很少涉足的地下世界。我大半辈子都在跑码头，或者在偏僻地方风餐露宿，只问或在城市贫民窟里过上几个月。他带我进了那些体面的烟馆（hop joint），我们天天在那儿抽；带我进了油光水滑的骗子和灵巧的扒手们的聚集地；带我进了镀金的咖啡馆和别的一些高档讲究的消遣场所。
过了几个月我腻了这种生活，就跟斯波坎提议，让他北上到加拿大边境，把我埋藏点（plant）里那批手表和戒指起出来。入室盗窃的风声已经过去，我也安排好了销路，卖到那些东西永远不可能回头咬我的地方。这趟差事正合他意，因为他可以顺路去华盛顿州斯波坎市看望家里人；他肯去我也高兴，因为我不想在那批手表上冒险——表都编了号，一认一个准。当初我把它们留在后面，就是不想背着那个大包袱四处走，为它丢了更值钱的宝石。我给他画了埋藏地点的示意图，给他买好车票、备足盘缠，打发他上路，叮嘱他把包裹用快递寄到旧金山的快递营业所，保价二十五块，跟快递员说里面装的是矿石样品。
为保险起见，我又叮嘱他：火车进站时站在站台上，亲眼看着包裹进了快递车厢；然后第二天再上车，绕个弯子回旧金山——这样万一包裹在路上出了事，也牵连不到他。我提心吊胆地熬了两个星期，他按时回来了，带来好消息：东西没费事就找到了，也照说好的寄出去了。第二天他去快递营业所取，东西没到。二十五块保价的包裹，不好闹得太凶，我们等了几天他才再去的。

===Original===

The express office was in Montgomery Street then, and I stood in the Palace Hotel entrance where I could look across the street and see him showing the receipt and arguing with the clerks. His willingness to go to the office every day convinced me he was on the square. He had a receipt for the package; I assumed it had gone astray.
We talked the thing over and decided there was nothing to be afraid of. If the parcel had been seized by the police he would have been pinched at the express office. I began to get suspicious after a week and was for abandoning the whole thing, but Spokane insisted on making another call. From my spot across the street I saw two men fall on him when he spoke to the clerk and knew he was arrested.
I went straight to my room, got everything and moved to another, where I sat down and tried to figure out what had happened and what to do. His arrest was a mystery, but it was plain to me that I must either jump out on him or stay and help. I was clean, there wasn’t a scrap of evidence against me. If I deserted him that might cause him to squawk, and with his testimony they could convict me if they got me. I knew the police would accuse him of the burglary and that he could and would convince them in an hour that he was in San Francisco when it occurred. That would start them on a hunt for another man, and that would bring them to me, for I had been seen with him daily for months. I saw I would be picked up; I saw they could prove I was in the town where the burglary was committed, and at the time. But they could get no further. The evidence to convict me was not in existence. I could not be convicted without Spokane’s testimony.
I was reasonably certain he would protect me if I protected him. I sent and found that the police had not been to the room I deserted; that meant he was standing up. I decided to stay and take a pinch and have the business settled while I had money to fight. After making this decision I cleaned up a few odds and ends of jewelry, put them away in my safety box, and left the key in a secure place. I took a thousand dollars in hundred-dollar bills and secreted it in different parts of my clothes. The pawn tickets had all been destroyed the minute we got them. I felt perfectly secure, and went openly around the hangouts waiting for the blow to fall.

===Chinese===

那时快递营业所在蒙哥马利街，我站在宫殿饭店（Palace Hotel）门口，隔着街就能看见他出示单据、跟职员们交涉。他天天肯往营业所跑，我信了他没有二心。他有收件单据；我只当包裹寄丢了。
我们把这事商量了一遍，断定没什么可怕的。包裹若是被警察扣了，他在营业所当场就会被捏（pinch）。一个星期后我起了疑心，主张整个撒手不管，可斯波坎坚持再去一趟。我在街对面我那个位置上，看见两个人在他跟职员说话时扑到他身上，就知道他被捕了。
我径直回住处，收拾了所有东西搬进另一处，坐下来琢磨出了什么事、该怎么办。他怎么会被捕是个谜，可我心里明白：我要么掳下他跑路，要么留下帮他。我是干净的，没有一星半点对我不利的证据。可我若甩了他，可能逼得他乱咬；有了他的证词，他们只要抓到我就能定我的罪。我知道警察会指控他入室盗窃，而他有一个钟头就能让他们相信案发时他在旧金山。那他们就会去搜另一个男人，也就会搜到我，因为一连几个月天天有人看见我跟他在一起。我明白我早晚被抓；我也明白他们能证明案发时我在那个镇上。可再往前就一步都走不动了。给我定罪的证据并不存在。没有斯波坎的证词，定不了我的罪。
我相当有把握：只要我护着他，他就会护着我。我托人打听，警察没去搜我搬离的那个房间；这说明他扛住了。我决定留下，挨一次捏，趁手里有钱打官司，把这件事了结。拿定主意后，我把零碎的首饰收拢收拢，放进我的保险箱，钥匙藏在稳妥的地方。我带了一千块的一百元大钞，分藏在衣服各处。当票是我们一到手就全烧掉的。我自觉万无一失，大摇大摆地在各处窝点出没，等着那一刀落下来。

===Original===

It came in a hurry, and to my surprise I was put in the cell occupied by Spokane. I learned later that the cell had some kind of listening arrangement, but we did not discuss our troubles. We were both “on the small book,” which meant incommunicado, but I promised a trusty prisoner a dollar if he would get word out to an attorney I had in mind. He was one of the leading criminal lawyers at that time, and had us both out to talk to him the next morning. The evening papers carried the story of my arrest and of the burglary. The attorney had read it, and seeing it was a case of considerable magnitude he came at once. He did not believe in any fat fees being held incommunicado.
I gave him a substantial retainer and asked him to find out what we were charged with and what we were being held on. Spokane had got barely an hour’s sleep at one time in the three days he had been in. Policemen and detectives took turn about questioning him, trying to wear him down to the weakening point. They now started on me, threatening, blustering, cajoling, wheedling, and promising. I made no statement except that I was innocent. There was no local interest in the case, and we were not mistreated. Captain Lindheimer, long dead now, was in charge of the city prison. He frowned on the beating-up of prisoners, and there was very little of it done when he was in charge. He was a very humane man, and of all the prisoners that passed through his hands I don’t recall ever hearing one with a harsh word for him.
Our attorney had influence enough to get our meals sent from the outside; we bought clean, new blankets from the head trusty and plenty of opium, which we ate. Spokane’s wife was permitted to visit us, the police probably figuring they could follow her to something that would convict us. She had not been told anything, knew nothing, and could do us no harm.
Out of my thousand dollars I got in with, I saw to it that she was taken care of. This was only fair to Spokane, and helped him to help me by keeping his mouth shut.
The attorney could learn nothing except that we were being held on telegrams. “This is soft,” he said. “They can’t hold you on that. I’ll take you out on a writ of habeas corpus.”
The next morning we went into court on the writ. The court gave the police twenty-four hours to get something more substantial than telegrams, threatening to liberate us if they failed.

===Chinese===

它来得很快，而且叫我意外的是，我被关进了斯波坎那间牢房。后来我才知道那间牢房装了什么窃听的玩意儿，但我们没谈我们的麻烦。我们俩都上了“小册子”，就是不许见客不许通信的意思，可我许了个杂役犯一块钱，让他捎信给我心里盘算的一位律师。他是当时数一数二的刑事律师，第二天上午就把我们俩都提出去谈话了。晚报登了我被捕的消息和那桩盗窃案。律师看了报，见这不是个小案子，立刻就来了。他可不信“不许见客”能拦得住大笔律师费。
我给了他一笔可观的定金，让他去打听我们被控什么、凭什么押着。斯波坎被押的三天里，一次统共没睡上一个钟头。警察和探子轮班审他，想把他熬到撑不住。接着他们对我来这一套，威吓的、咋呼的、哄骗的、灌迷汤的、许愿的，样样都有。我除了一句“我是清白的”，什么口供也没给。这案子本地没什么人关心，我们也没受虐待。林德海默队长——如今早已过世——那时管着市监狱。他不赞成揍犯人，他当家时这种事很少发生。他是个极厚道的人，凡是从他手里过过的犯人，我不记得听谁说过他一句重话。
我们的律师有门路，让我们的一日三餐从外面送；我们向头号杂役犯买了干净的新毯子，还弄到不少鸦片（hop），和着吞服。斯波坎的太太获准来探视，警察多半盘算着盯她的梢，好找到给我们定罪的东西。她什么都没被告知，什么都不知道，害不了我们。
从我带进去的一千里头，我安排人照应她的生活。这对斯波坎才算公平，也等于帮了他——让他闭紧嘴巴，就是帮我的忙。
律师打听来打听去，只问出我们是凭几封电报押着的。“这好办，”他说。“凭电报押不住你们。我申请人身保护令，把你们提出去。”
第二天上午我们凭令状上了法庭。法院给警察二十四小时，让他们拿出比电报实在点的东西，拿不出就放人。

===Original===

When we appeared the next day they had nothing except more telegrams, and we were discharged from custody.
I grabbed the attorney’s hand. “Don’t get excited,” he said, “they’re not done with you yet.”
Although the judge released us we were promptly rearrested at the courtroom door and put in our cell. The attorney applied for another writ. This was granted and we went to court again the next day, but were put over twenty-four hours on the plea of the police that witnesses were on their way from Canada. No witnesses appeared, and we were discharged only to be arrested again. The court warned the police that they would be in contempt if we were held longer without evidence.
On our next day in court we were confronted by the chief of provincial detectives from Victoria with a warrant of extradition and a day was set for him to make his showing. One night a strange attorney came to me in the prison, told me he had had a talk with the judge, and that for a sum of money he would point out a flaw in the case that would upset the extradition plea. I sent for my attorney and asked his advice. He investigated, and told me to put up the money.
When we went into court the Canadian officer produced his warrant. It was the biggest document I ever saw﻿—a piece of parchment about three feet long, embossed, engraved, and signed and sealed with the seal of Queen Victoria﻿—a formal demand from the British Government on the Secretary of State to deliver us over to the Canadian authorities. He had it in a bag carefully rolled and wrapped in paper. Taking it out carefully, reverently, he placed it in the judge’s hands.
“Where did you get this document?” the court asked.
“From Ottawa, the capital of Canada,” the officer answered.
“Did you go to Ottawa yourself for it?”
“No, it came to Victoria by mail.”
Looking at it closely the judge pointed to a name signed at the bottom. “What’s this?” he asked.
“That is the signature of Lord Aberdeen, governor general of Canada.”
“Did you see him sign it?”
“No.”
“Hm,” said the judge, laying it down.
Our lawyer picked it up and appeared to read it carefully. Turning to the officer, he asked: “How do you know that is Lord Aberdeen’s signature? Were you there when he signed it? Did you ever see him sign his name to any paper? Did you ever see Lord Aberdeen? What’s this thing?” He tapped the seal, the big red seal half as large as your hand.

===Chinese===

第二天我们出庭，他们除了又几封电报外什么都没有，我们当庭开释。
我一把抓住律师的手。“别高兴得太早，”他说，“他们跟你没完。”
法官虽然放了我们，我们在法庭门口立刻又被捕，押回牢房。律师又申请了一张保护令。批了，第二天我们再次出庭，可警察说证人正从加拿大赶来，法院又给了二十四小时。证人没露面，我们又被开释，接着又被捕。法院警告警察：再没有证据这么押着人，就是藐视法庭。
再一次出庭，对面站着维多利亚省的探长，手里拿着引渡令，法院定了一天让他举证。一天夜里，一个陌生律师到监狱来见我，说他同法官谈过了，只要一笔钱，他就指出案子里的一处破绽，足以掹翻引渡申请。我把律师请来问他的主意。他去查了查，让我掏钱。
开庭那天，加拿大警官出示了他的令状。那是我平生见过最大的文件——一张三英尺来长的羊皮纸，压花、雕版、签名，钤着维多利亚女王的印玺——英王政府向美国国务卿提出的正式要求，把我们移交加拿大当局。他把这宝贝装在袋子里，仔细卷好包上纸。取出来时小心翼翼、毕恭毕敬，双手捧到法官面前。
“这份文件你是从哪儿弄来的？”法庭问。
“从渥太华，加拿大首都，”警官答。
“你亲自去渥太华取的？”
“不是，是邮寄到维多利亚的。”
法官凑近细看，指着末尾一个签名。“这是什么？”他问。
“那是加拿大总督阿伯丁勋爵的签名。”
“你亲眼见他签的？”
“没有。”
“嗯。”法官说着，把它放下了。
我们的律师拿起来，装作仔细读。他转向警官，问：“你怎么知道那是阿伯丁勋爵的签名？他签的时候你在场吗？你见过他在任何文件上签名吗？你见过阿伯丁勋爵本人吗？这是个什么玩意儿？”他戳了戳那枚火漆大印——足有你半只手掌大。

===Original===

“That is the seal of Her Majesty, Queen Victoria of England,” the man answered, getting indignant and red in the face.
“Oh, it is, is it? How do you know it is? Did you see her put it there? Is she here to identify it? There’s no Victoria here in this court, is there? This might be the seal of Jane Doe, a waitress. Anybody can have a seal. Do you think the court is going to recognize this thing? You’ll have to come in here with something better than that,” he said, throwing it on the floor and kicking it under a table.
The officer got down from the witness stand, picked it up, brushed it off, and carefully put it away.
The judge then said: “This court cannot recognize that as a valid document. You come here with a paper demanding custody of these defendants, but we have here no proof that it is genuine. It might be a forgery. We know there is such a person as Lord Aberdeen, of Canada, but we have no proof that his signature is on that warrant. I cannot hold defendants on such evidence.”
We were discharged again. I don’t know where the money went that I gave the lawyer who sold us the point that beat the extradition; I know the judge didn’t give us any the worst of it.
We were arrested again at the door. That night we were indicted by the grand jury; I for bringing stolen property into the state of California, and Spokane for receiving it from me. The police saw we were framing and using money, so they, rather than be beaten, began framing us. Our cases were transferred into another court where they could do some fixing, and we couldn’t. The indictments meant that we would have to wait for trial. I began to get uneasy about the stones I had in the safety box. The police were tearing the town open for them, not knowing that nearly all of them had been sold. I told our lawyer where to get the key to the box, gave him a password, and told him to take the junk out and put it in some safe place. The police tailed every visitor we had and even the lawyer in hopes of locating the stones, and he had to be careful.
Making another application for a writ of habeas corpus, which he knew would not avail us, he had the hearing set for ten o’clock in the morning. This alarmed the coppers and every one of them was at the courtroom when the case was called. Everybody was there but our attorney. The hearing was held up for fifteen minutes.

===Chinese===

“这是英王维多利亚陛下的御玺，”那人答道，脸涨红了，动了气。
“哦，是吗？你怎么知道是？你亲眼见她盖上去的吗？她本人到庭辨认了吗？这法庭上可没有维多利亚，对吧？这没准是哪个叫珍妮·多尔的女服务生的图章。谁都能刻个章。你以为法院会认这玩意儿？你得拿点像样的东西来，”他说着把那文件掼在地上，一脚踢到桌子底下。
警官走下证人席，捡起来，掳干净，仔细收好。
法官于是说：“本院不能承认这是有效文件。你拿着一纸文书来要求羁押这两名被告，可本庭没有任何证据证明它是真的。它可能是伪造的。我们知道加拿大有这么一位阿伯丁勋爵，但没有任何证据表明他的签名在那张令状上。凭这样的证据，我不能押被告。”
我们又开释了。我给那个卖破绽的律师的钱进了谁的腰包，我不知道；我只知道法官没让我们吃亏。
我们又在门口被捕。当天夜里大陪审团对我们提起公诉：我，把赃物带入加利福尼亚州；斯波坎，从我手里收赃。警察见我们在做局、在使钱，便索性不甘落败，做起局来对付我们。我们的案子被移到另一家法院，在那里他们使得动手脚，我们使不动。被公诉就意味着得候审。我开始为保险箱里那些宝石不安起来。警察把全城翻了个底朝天找它们，殊不知那批东西差不多全出手了。我告诉律师去哪儿取保险箱的钥匙，给了他一句口令，让他把那批货起出来放到稳妥的地方。警察盯梢我们每一个访客，连律师也盯，指望找到宝石的下落，他不得不处处小心。
他又申请了一次人身保护令——他知道这救不了我们——把听证定在上午十点。这一下把条子们吓着了，开庭的时候一个不落全到了。人人都在，只有我们的律师没到。听证拖了一刻钟。

===Original===

During this fifteen minutes he was at the safety vault lifting the plant, while every detective on the case was waiting in court. He came in, nodded to us, apologized to the court, and went on with the case with the parcel of loot in his pocket. The writ was denied, of course. I told him to put the stuff away, and he did.
When I asked him about it later, he looked me straight in the eye and said: “Did you think I was damn fool enough to keep that stuff around me? Say, when court adjourned that day I went straight down to the foot of Powell Street and threw it in the bay.”
This didn’t look just right to me, but what could I do? I was only a poor, honest burglar in the hands of a highway lawyer. He got us a copy of the testimony given the grand jury, and from it I learned what had caused our arrest﻿—Spokane’s passion for gambling.
After lifting the watches and rings in the little Northern town, he tried his luck at the faro table and lost his expense money. This forced him to open the parcel and sell a couple of the watches to a gambler for enough money to buy his return ticket. The next day the gambler took them to the town where I stole them, fifteen miles away, across the line, and tried to pawn them. He was arrested and told where he got them. In an hour the police learned about the express package of “sample ore” Spokane shipped. It was seized en route to San Francisco. There was a big reward for the burglar, and the Northern police, wanting it, decided to capture him. Instead of wiring to have him arrested the first time he went to the express office, they hurried here and got him themselves.
Spokane was repentant, but it was too late. I did not reproach him.
His wife was faithful, and devoted to him, and visited us daily at the prison, bringing papers and small delicacies. One evening, while we were chatting on a bench in the visiting room, an officer came and took him out to another room. Several policemen were standing around, and there seemed to be something stagy going on that I couldn’t understand. Suddenly, as I sat talking to Mrs. Spokane, a screen at the other side of the room was knocked aside and Irish Annie dashed from behind it, heaping abuse on me and trying to get her hands on my friend’s wife.
Annie’s furious appearance was like a thunderbolt out of a clear sky. I saw instantly that she had been poisoned against me.

===Chinese===

就在这一刻钟里，他在保管库起那批货，办这案子的探子们全都坐在法庭里干等。他走进来，朝我们点点头，向法庭致了歉，口袋里揣着那包赃物接着办案。保护令当然被驳回了。我让他把东西处理掉，他照办了。
后来我问他东西的下落，他直盯着我的眼睛说：“你当我傻到把那玩意儿留在身上？听着，那天一休庭我就直奔鲍威尔街口，把它扔进海湾了。”
我听着不对味儿，可我能怎样？我不过是落在一个吃黑道饭的律师手里、穷兮兮、老老实实的贼。他给我们弄到了大陪审团证词的副本，我这才知道我们是怎么栽的——全怪斯波坎好赌。
他在北边那个小镇起出手表和戒指之后，去法罗赌桌碰运气，把盘缠输了个精光。这下他只好拆开包裹，卖了两块表给一个赌棍，换了张回程车票钱。第二天那赌棍把表拿到我偷表的镇子——十五英里外，边境那边——去当，被当场抓住，招出了表的来路。不出一个钟头，警察就查到了斯波坎寄的那包“矿石样品”。包裹在寄往旧金山的路上被扣。盗窃犯悬赏不菲，北方警察想要这笔钱，决意亲自拿人。他们没打电报让我们这边在他头一回取件时就地抓他，而是自己火速赶来，亲手捏了他。
斯波坎悔之莫及，可晚了。我没有责备他。
他太太贤惠，一心向着他，天天到监狱探视，带来报纸和些许可口的小食。一天傍晚，我们正在探视室的长椅上闲谈，一个警官来把他叫到另一间屋去。几个警察站在四周，似乎在演一出我看不懂的戏。突然，就在我跟斯波坎太太说话的当口，屋子那头的屏风被人撞开，爱尔兰安妮（Irish Annie）从屏风后头冲出来，对我破口大骂，伸手就要抓我朋友的太太。
安妮这副怒气冲天的架势，好比晴天一个霹雳。我立刻看出，有人在我背后下了毒。

===Original===

I tried to talk to her but she wouldn’t listen, and the coppers stood between us pushing me away to make her think I was trying to get my hands on her and strangle her. Mrs. Spokane had collapsed on the bench, almost fainting from fear and wondering what it was about. Annie was led away screaming threats. Spokane’s wife went home, and we back to our cell where we tried to piece together this unexpected angle.
I saw the police were playing my game better than I could. They knew I was trying to frame myself out; they began framing me in, and with Irish Annie they had a dangerous lead on me. It was plain to me that they had told her she was discarded for another woman; that they had called Spokane away to leave me alone with his wife while Annie watched from behind the screen. I could almost hear them saying to her: “There you are. There’s the woman he left you for. There’s the woman he hung his diamonds on, and spends his money on. How he got her is a mystery to us; she’s a decent woman. She’s crazy about him and believes he is innocent.”
Then Annie, too ready to believe herself “a woman scorned,” turned copper on me, knocked the screen away, and poured out the vials of her wrath. Still I wasn’t much alarmed about the case and I couldn’t help admiring the coppers for the way they tooled Annie and chiseled her out of her right senses. They assumed I was guilty; that gave them the right to assume that she, being my companion, knew something about the burglary. They were justified in getting it out of her by fair or unfair means. I knew she was ignorant of the least detail of it, and felt like laughing at them for wasting time on their elaborate scheme.
Our attorney made many efforts to see her, but the police had her in a hotel under the eye of a matron, and he and his runners failed.
At last he subpoenaed her as our witness and had her brought to his office. All his eloquence got nothing out of her. She was mute and stayed mute till the day of my trial. She had not been before the grand jury. We had no police-court examination and there was no way of finding out what she was going to say till she went into court. The attorney was apprehensive. I wasn’t. I told him of the night I first met her shivering in a Chicago doorway, of the warm clothing I got for her, and of the night I staggered out of her crib with the dead man over my shoulder.

===Chinese===

我想同她说话，她不听；条子们横在我们中间，把我往一边推，好让她以为我要对她动手掐死她。斯波坎太太瘫在长椅上，吓得几乎晕过去，不明白这是怎么了。安妮被带了下去，一路尖叫着威胁。斯波坎太太回了家，我们回了牢房，琢磨这从天上掉下来的一角是怎么回事。
我看出来了：警察打我的牌，打得比我自己还漂亮。他们知道我想把自己摘出去，就开始往里把我套；有了爱尔兰安妮，他们手上多了一条危险的线。明摆着，他们对她说，我是为了另一个女人才把她用了的；他们把斯波坎支开，好让我单独同他太太待着，让安妮在屏风后面看。我几乎听得见他们对她说：“瞧见没有。那就是他为你用了你的女人。他把钻石挂在她身上，把钱花在她身上。他怎么弄到手的，我们也不知道；她是个正经女人。她为他疯魔，相信他是清白的。”
于是安妮，本来就巴不得信自己是“被负心的女人”——掉头来咬我，撞翻屏风，把一腔怒火劈头浇下来。即便这样，我对案子本身并不怎么慌，反倒不能不佩服那些条子摆弄安妮的手段，硬是把她的理智全凿走了。他们认定我有罪；由此便有权利认定她，既然是我的相好，就必然知道盗窃案的底细。用正路邪路从她嘴里掏出来，在他们都是理直气壮。我知道她连案子最细枝末节的一星都不知道，忍不住想笑他们白费那么大工夫布这个精巧的局。
我们的律师想尽办法见她，警察却把她安置在一家旅馆里，由一个女看守盯着，他和他的跑腿们全失败了。
最后他给她发了传票，以我方证人名义把她传到他的办公室。他浑身解数使尽，什么也没掏出来。她一言不发，一直哑到我开庭那天。她没上过大陪审团。我们没有治安庭预审，她要说什么，直到她走上法庭谁也无从知道。律师掹着一把汗。我不。我把头一回遇见她的那晚讲给他听——她蜷在芝加哥一处门道里瑟瑟发抖；我怎样给她置了寒衣；还有那晚我肩上扛着个死人、踉踉跄跄从她的窝里出来的事。

===Original===

I told him in detail of our meeting in the North by chance, and of our relations there. He was old and wise. He shook his head thoughtfully and quoted the poet’s line about scorned women and their hellish fury.
The prosecution clamored for a speedy trial of the case. Almost ten thousand dollars had been spent by the Canadian authorities in retaining a firm of high-priced lawyers to prosecute; in transporting witnesses and paying hotel and other bills, and for private detectives hired to spy on the local police. Still no diamonds showed up. Spokane was offered a job with the Pinkerton Detective Agency if he would turn on me. I was promised my liberty if I would turn up the stones. We kept up our cry of innocence and made no statements at any time. A week before the day set for my trial, our attorney was stricken in his study and died in his chair, of heart failure.
We had to get another lawyer and pay another stiff fee. I went over the case with him, but somehow was unable to convince him that I had not talked too much to Irish Annie.
At my trial she swore, and she wasn’t mealymouthed about it, either, that I told her of the burglary when I was planning it; that I offered her part of the stones when I got them; that she was afraid to take them because they were stolen.
